<meta>This page continues the history of the Bohemian Reformation following the Council of Constance. It describes the rise of Sigismund, the leadership of Jan Žižka, and the beginning of the Hussite Wars.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

<header>Bohemia</header>

<meta>catchword: Persecu- (original: fequu-)</meta>

**Sigismund**, the brother [of the late King Wenceslaus] and King of the Hungarians and Romans, received the kingdom. Through delegates (as he did not enter himself until later with an army), he began to order various measures intended to diminish liberty of conscience. From this, it happened that the **Hussites** (as they were now called) chose for themselves a leader: **John of Trutnow**. Born of a noble family, raised in the Royal Court, and trained in war, he was called **Žižka** (meaning "one-eyed") because he had lost an eye. He performed formidable deeds, as is well known from history. This man, pitching camp with his army on a certain rocky mountain ten miles from Prague, caused it to be surrounded by a wall and a city to be founded, to be defended by arms if it were necessary (as indeed it was). They called this city **Tabor** (which means "The Camp").

32. For although the new Pope elected by the Council of Constance, **Martin V**, had sent flattering letters to the Bohemians in 1418, *inviting them to renounce the Wycliffite and Hussite errors*, he later (in 1420) published an Excommunication against them at Florence. He invited and incited the Emperor, Kings, Princes, Dukes, Marquises, Counts, Barons, Prefects, Cities, and Republics to take up arms against them. He begged them by the wounds of Christ and by the salvation of each person to attack them with joined forces and *utterly exterminate that sacrilegious and cursed race*.

33. Inflamed by this incentive, the Emperor Sigismund, together with all of Germany and the neighboring kingdoms, offered himself as a servant to this Antichristian tyranny. Soon, in that same year, he entered Bohemia with a powerful army and exhausted the land with wars that continued into the thirteenth year. These were called the **Hussite Wars**. Concerning these wars, **Aeneas Sylvius** (who was later called Pope Pius II) wrote that *they would hold more wonder than belief among posterity*, because they were carried out with absolutely no success on the [Imperial] side, as Žižka, the leader of the Bohemians, and his successors were always victorious.

34. But at home, the affairs of the Bohemians were also in utter confusion. While some stood with the Emperor and the Pope, others defended the **Cup** <term>The Cup: Referring to the "Utraquists" or "Calixtines" who insisted that the laity should receive both the bread and the wine (the chalice/cup) during Communion, rather than just the bread.</term>, and both sides were strongly inflamed toward mutual slaughter with horrible savagery. The greatest cruelty was shown by the Papists against those men who were more zealous than the others against superstitions: As in the History of the Persecu- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with the word "Persecutions."</note>

<summary>This page details the transition of Bohemia into the Hussite Wars following the rise of King Sigismund and the military leadership of the one-eyed general Jan Žižka. It describes the founding of the fortress-city Tabor and the Pope's call for a crusade to "exterminate" the Hussites.</summary>

<keywords>Sigismund, Jan Žižka, Tabor, Hussite Wars, Pope Martin V, Aeneas Sylvius, Calixtines, religious war, Bohemia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Bohemian Wars following the invasion of Emperor Sigismund in 1420. It details the internal divisions among the Hussites and their miraculous defense against the Imperial and Papal forces.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

# The Rebellion Against the Pope

<meta>catchword: 38. Caesar-</meta>

[of the] **Persecutions of the Bohemian Churches**, Chapter 10 and those following, where the story is told and various examples of martyrdoms are recounted.

35. *The Bohemians thus suffered a schism among themselves: with the greater part of them falling away and helping to persecute the true faithful.* There was indeed in all of them a fire for opposing the Antichrist <note>Antichrist: A term commonly used by Hussite reformers to describe the Papacy, which they viewed as a corrupt institution opposing the true Gospel.</note>, following the example of Master Hus. But since, after his death, they were destitute of any distinguished, prudent, and spirited leader who could hold the stirred-up commoners in some certain order, it happened that they went off into opposing interests and incited terrible dissensions among themselves. For the common crowd (of both the people and the Clergy) were focused only on the **Cup** <note>original: "Calicem"</note> (hence they were called <term>Calixtines</term> <note>Calixtines: Also known as Utraquists, they were moderate Hussites who believed the laity should receive communion in "both kinds" (bread and wine/the cup), a practice then forbidden to commoners.</note>), and they securely neglected the rest of Master Hus’s teachings. The <term>Taborites</term> <note>Taborites: The radical wing of the movement, named after their fortified city, Tabor. They sought a total return to the simplicity of the early Biblical church.</note>, however (among whom **Wenceslaus Koranda** and **Nicholas of Pelhřimov** <note>original: "Nicolaus Episcopius"</note>, excellent men, were prominent), together with a few others, began to urge for simplicity and purity in all Articles of faith and ceremonies. *The former cried out that they must not withdraw from the rites of the Church in everything; the latter cried out that no superstitions should be tolerated.*

36. However, certain imposters <note>original: "personati quidam," meaning people in disguise or playing a role.</note> mixed themselves in, who, intending to promote the cause of the Pope and the Emperor <note>original: "Caesaris"</note>, stirred up these mutual dissensions and incited the hatred of the common people against the followers of the purer teachings, slandering them with the hateful name of **Piccards**. Moreover, they called the **Waldensians** "Piccards"—those who, having been driven out of France before that time, had settled in Austria and were already known by the name of most foul heretics. There were also those found who deceived <note>original: "dementarunt," literally "made mad" or "blinded."</note> **Žižka**, the leader of the Taborites, so that he would join with the Calixtines and persecute those called Piccards with fire and sword, just as he did the Papists themselves. Thus, everything everywhere was conducted in a state of riot.

37. It certainly would not have been a wonder if the Papal faction had completely exterminated them while they were at odds. *But the wonderful goodness of God, who knows how to temper human stupidity so wisely, ensured that neither enemies nor friends had anything to boast about regarding themselves.* For as often as the Emperor with his armies loomed over the Bohemians while they were disagreeing at home, just as often did they enter into peace with one another and defend the common cause with common arms. **Žižka** and **Prokop** <note>Prokop the Great, who succeeded Žižka as the leader of the Taborite forces.</note>, the leaders of the Taborites, were chiefly in charge of affairs, while God granted miraculous successes and victories.

<summary>This page describes the internal "schism" between the moderate Calixtines and radical Taborites during the Hussite Wars, as well as the external threat from the Pope and Emperor Sigismund that forced these factions to unite under leaders like Žižka and Prokop.</summary>

# The Bohemians Led Back Again

<meta>catchword: Mere- (Likely referring to "Meretrix" or "Merely" in the following page's opening sentence)</meta>

38. The Emperor, therefore, when he and the Pope realized they were making no progress through force of arms, turned to deceit. A Council was again called at Basel (in the year 1432), and they very peacefully invited the Bohemians to a friendly settlement of their disagreement, promising to satisfy their demands for public safety in whatever way they wished. Consequently, Legates were sent to the Council: among them the most prominent were the theologians **John Rokyzana** <note>John Rokyzana (c. 1396–1471): An influential Hussite theologian who became the Archbishop of Prague, though the Pope never officially confirmed him.</note> of Prague and **Nicholas**, Bishop of the **Taborites** <note>Taborites: The more radical, militarily focused wing of the Hussite movement who sought a total return to primitive Christian simplicity.</note>. From among the nobility, there was **Procopius the Great** <note>original: "Procopius Holy"; a former priest turned general who led the Hussite armies after the death of Jan Žižka.</note>, leader of the Taborites, and **William Kostka, Baron of Postupice**, along with others. When they were received with kindness and asked about their demands, they offered four Articles, requesting they be permitted to follow them, or else they would defend them. The Articles were drafted as follows:

> I. *That the use of the Cup <note>The "Cup" refers to Communion in both kinds (bread and wine) for the laity, which the Roman Church had restricted to priests only.</note> be restored to the common people, and all sacred services be performed in the native language.*
> II. *That the clergy should not rule in a secular manner.*
> III. *That the Word of God be taught freely.*
> IV. *That public sins be publicly punished.*

To the Pope’s Legate, who asked, *“Do you not have more demands? For it is said that you believe the Monastic Orders are of the Devil?”* Procopius replied: *“From where else can they come, since they were instituted neither by the Patriarchs or Prophets, nor by Christ and his Apostles?”* and so forth.

39. Deputies were afterward appointed from both sides to debate the proposed Articles; the dispute lasted for a full fifty days. *When the Bohemians could not be refuted, they reached a friendly compromise. Everything was settled in such a way—with Rokyzana being blinded by the hope of the dignity of an Archbishop, and leading the other Legates into the same delusion—that the aforementioned Articles were granted to the Bohemians, while they, in turn, promised to return to the obedience of the Roman See and to observe its rituals.* They called this settlement the **COMPACTS** <note>original: "COMPACTATA"; a landmark legal agreement that allowed the Bohemians to practice a modified form of their faith while remaining within the Catholic Church.</note>. Legates were then sent into Bohemia by the Council and the Emperor *to declare that the Bohemians had been received back into the bosom of the Church and were held as dear sons.* There, in a convened Assembly, Rokyzana magnificently explained how they had obtained by their wishes those things for which so much blood had been shed in fighting, and for which the Kingdom had fallen into such desolation. He spoke far differently now about the Pope and the Emperor than he once had, when...

<summary>This page details the diplomatic negotiations at the Council of Basel (1432), where the Bohemians presented the Four Articles of Prague. It describes the formation of the "Compacts," a compromise that granted religious concessions to the Bohemians in exchange for their return to the Catholic Church.</summary>

# The Popes Rebel
<margin>The Popes rebel.</margin>

...calling [Rome] the Harlot and the Beast of the Apocalypse <note>A reference to the Book of Revelation; the Hussites frequently identified the Papacy with the "Whore of Babylon" and the "Beast."</note>, while speaking.

40. It grieved many, and most especially the zealous **Taborites** <note>The radical wing of the Hussites who rejected all rituals not found in Scripture.</note>, that in this way they were receding from the footsteps of **Jan Hus** and returning to the camp of the **Antichrist**. They protested as much as they were able, and it came to arms once again. In this conflict, the Taborites were defeated <note>This refers to the Battle of Lipany in 1434, where moderate Hussites and Catholics joined forces to break the power of the radicals.</note>, and their entire military strength collapsed so completely that afterwards they defended themselves only with the sword of the word and with patience. This took place in the year 1434.

41. In the year 1435, at a public assembly <term>Diet (Comitiis)|The formal deliberative assembly of the Estates of the Kingdom of Bohemia.</term>, **John Rokyzana** was solemnly elected **Archbishop**. However, when he saw that his formal consecration was being blocked by the Emperor and that he was being mocked, he took it poorly. He began to attack both the Imperial injustice and the Papal domination with his faction once again. Finally, out of fear of the Emperor, he withdrew from Prague for nearly three years. For the Emperor **Sigismund** died shortly thereafter, and his successor **Albert** died within two years, leaving behind a posthumous son, **Ladislaus**, who was being educated at the court of Emperor Frederick.

42. During this time of interregnum <note>The period between the reigns of kings (1439–1453).</note>, and much more so when **George of Poděbrady** (whom Rokyzana had completely in his hand) succeeded to the throne after **Ladislaus** died <note>Ladislaus the Posthumous died at age 17, leading to the election of George of Poděbrady, the "Hussite King."</note>, that prophecy of the Prophet began to be greatly fulfilled: *Manasseh shall devour Ephraim, and Ephraim Manasseh, and both shall be against Judah.* <note>original: *Manaffes Ephraimum vorabit, & Ephraim Manaffem, ambo autem contra Iudam erunt.* A quote from Isaiah 9:21, used here to describe the civil strife between factions that should have been united.</note> For the Pope raged against George and the **Calixtines** <term>Calixtines|The moderate Hussites, named from the Latin *calix* (cup), because they insisted on the laypeople receiving the wine of the Eucharist.</term> with interdicts, censures, and curses; meanwhile, they raged no less violently against the Pope and the monks. But the true believers were oppressed by both sides until the remnants of the Taborites were crushed by trickery and force.

43. For Rokyzana, most charmingly allaying them (both by himself and through others), pretended he was still prepared to yield if they could *demonstrate that the truth was purer among them*. He claimed he did not wish to make himself, or any of his followers, the judge of that cause, but would permit the Estates in a public Diet to investigate and pronounce judgment upon it. The Taborites, trusting in the goodness of their cause, agreed and appeared for the Assembly. There, after many disputes, **Arbitrators were chosen** to deliver a definitive sentence, which was to be confirmed by the authority of the Diet so that neither side would be permitted to appeal further, but would be bound to join the other side immediately. When these arbitrators [decided] in favor of Rokyzana...

B

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Bohemian Reformation during the mid-15th century, focusing on the suppression of the radical Taborites by George of Poděbrady and the subsequent rise of John Rokyzana’s anti-papal rhetoric, which sparked the initial formation of the Bohemian Brethren.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>

<header>The Bohemian Brethren</header>

43. Once the resolutions had been pronounced (for the matters had been settled), the good and simple **Taborites** <note>The radical wing of the Hussites who sought a total return to New Testament simplicity.</note> finally recognized they had been circumvented by trickery. Yet, not daring to go against the agreements, they promised consent to the rituals. However, since they delayed in reforming the Church upon returning home (that is, in submerging it again in superstitions), the Viceroy **George** <note>George of Poděbrady, then regent and later King of Bohemia.</note> intervened with an army, besieged the City, and forced the citizens to surrender and hand over their priests to his power. These he condemned to perpetual imprisonment in the castles of **Poděbrady** and **Litice**, while **Martin Lupáč** and several others went over to **Rokyzana**.

44. Now, when the Bohemians had for several years sought the investiture of their Archbishop in vain, the Pope refused unless **Rokyzana** renounced the **Chalice** <note>The practice of Utraquism, or receiving both bread and wine in the Eucharist, which was the central demand of the Hussites.</note>. Indeed, when the Cardinal of St. Angelo was sent to Prague (in the year 1448), he ordered the *Compactata* <note>The legal agreements between the Hussites and the Council of Basel that allowed the use of the Chalice.</note> to be withdrawn and snatched from them under the guise of an inspection. Although the Cardinal had fled secretly with these documents, he was caught in his flight and forced to restore them. **Rokyzana**, having resumed his hatred for the Pope, demonstrated from the *Apocalypse* and *Daniel* <note>Biblical books of Revelation and Daniel; Hussite reformers frequently used these to identify the Papacy as the Antichrist.</note> that the Pope was that great *Antichrist*. He declared that unless we withdrew from obedience to him, we were a miserable and damned crowd. He kept saying that the *Law of Christ* <note>original: "Legem Christi"</note>, rather than the *Compactata*, should be held as the form of religion: everything belonging to the Pope should be deserted as if contaminated, and everything should be raised anew from Apostolic foundations. In a word, he thundered against the Antichrist so loudly that most people believed him to be another **Jan Hus**, and the hope of a full reformation seemed to shine brightly.

45. But since he did not set his hand to the work, the more courageous of his listeners (among whom the foremost was **Gregory**, the nephew of **Rokyzana** by his sister, a noble man) consulted together and approached **Rokyzana**. They revealed the anguish of their consciences and sought his advice. He received them kindly, praised their zeal, and offered them certain treatises to read (written against the abominations creeping into the Church), thus inflaming them even more—though he advised silence and patience. When they pressed him a second and third time, now increased in number, urging him not to delay in showing through deeds what he taught in words, he replied: *The business is of great weight; nothing can be done hastily here, etc.*

46. Meanwhile, **Rokyzana** strove against the Pope with whatever means he could,

<summary>This page describes the final suppression of the Taborite faction and the rising tensions between John Rokyzana and the Papacy, which led his more zealous followers to demand a formal break from Rome.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the 15th-century Bohemian religious crisis. It follows John Rokyzana’s attempts to find legitimacy for the Bohemian Church through the Greek Orthodox Church and his eventual realization that reform must come from a complete break with existing institutions.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

<header>The Origin</header>

<sig>B 2</sig>

it was possible *to find agreement even with the Greek Church*. For when the decree of the Synod of Florence <note>The Council of Florence (1431–1449) attempted to reunify the Western and Eastern Churches; it also reaffirmed the condemnation of the Hussites.</note> was promulgated (by which the Bohemians were again alienated from the Church as heretics), a Diet of the Estates and a Synod of Priests were held in Prague in the year 1450. Here, by the counsel of **Rokyzana** and by the common *consensus of the Estates and the Clergy, an appeal was made to the Greek Church, and Legates were sent to Constantinople with letters*. The Greeks heard the primary points of doctrine from the Legates and rejoiced in their mutual agreement; having given letters to the Bohemians, they confirmed them in the Faith, *adding a promise regarding the ordination of their Church Ministers*. But since these matters offered only slow-moving hope, **Rokyzana** again *encouraged his followers, publicly offering hope of a Reformation that was just about to appear*. For he used to say, while criticizing various abuses: *“We are merely pressing the surface, but shortly there will come those who will unearth the foundations of Truth from the deep.”* <note>original: "Nos superficiem premimus, sed prodibunt brevi, qui fundamenta Veritatis è profundo eruent."</note>

47. When he had led these pious souls along in this way into the seventh year, and was asked for his counsel again, he said: *“I have already surveyed all things in my mind, and I find nothing to decide upon, except that a secession should be made, from both the Calixtines as well as the Papists, entirely. For all things are overflowing with abominations, and this evil is incurable.”* <note>The Calixtines or Utraquists were the moderate Hussites; the Papists were those loyal to Rome.</note> The advice of **Lupáč** was similar. When the people said: *“Let us secede then; we shall acknowledge, venerate, and follow You as our leader, our teacher, and our father,”* he replied: *“You offer a difficult task; you are too bold; the matter is full of danger,”* and such things.

48. *In the meantime, Constantinople was conquered by the Turks* (In the year 1453), and two of the dispersed Greeks entered Prague. They were kindly received by **Rokyzana** and (after many conversations about religion) were permitted to celebrate the Mass. *When it became apparent that among them, too, the purity of faith was equally buried under superstitions*, those good **Zelotæ** <term>Zealots; here referring to the earnest reformers who would become the Bohemian Brethren</term> were even more inflamed in spirit, seeing what they should flee, but having no one whom they might follow.

49. Therefore, they approached **Rokyzana** again and pleaded by the glory of God *that he would take pity on their souls and the misled common people, and not desert them in such a great crisis of salvation*. But that man, though good, did not have enough strength to cast aside the hope of the glory of the Archbishopric (however vain it might be) and [go] with the people...

<meta>catchword: ...people</meta>

<summary>This page describes the Bohemian reformers' failed attempt to find a pure religious model in the Greek Orthodox Church and Rokyzana's internal struggle between his desire for reform and his ambition for the Archbishopric.</summary>
<keywords>John Rokyzana, Council of Florence, Constantinople, 1453, Calixtines, Martin Lupáč, Bohemian Brethren, Greek Orthodox Church, Utraquism</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the 15th-century Bohemian religious crisis. It follows John Rokyzana’s recommendation that a secession was necessary, leading to the physical relocation of his more radical followers to the estate of Litice, which marks the formal beginning of the Unitas Fratrum (Unity of the Brethren).</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

<header>The First of the Brethren</header>

choosing disgrace and the cross with the people of God, he, now indignant, began to reproach their persistence and asked that, having dismissed him, they should withdraw by themselves. He promised that he would obtain for them from the King (**George Podiebrad**, who had sworn obedience to the Pope so that he might obtain the Crown) some place where they could live securely and serve God with a pure conscience.

50. He therefore obtained the *Estate of Litice*, in the mountains near Silesia; to which those citizens of Prague, Bachelors and Masters, soon betook themselves; while others from elsewhere—commoners and Nobles, learned and unlearned—flocked together in no small number, intent on the reading of the Scriptures, prayers, and works of piety. They had Pastors from the Calixtines <note>Also known as Utraquists; these were the followers of Jan Hus who insisted on receiving communion in "both kinds" (the chalice/cup as well as the bread), but who otherwise remained closer to traditional church structures than the radical Brethren.</note> who, having abandoned superstitions, conformed themselves to the pattern of Apostolic simplicity. The foremost among them was *Michael Bradacius* (the Pastor of Žamberk), a pious and devoted old man. They all generally began to address one another by the name of BROTHERS and SISTERS—a name common to Christians and dear to the pious. This name prevailed, so that the title "Bohemian Brethren" was attributed to them *preeminently* <note>original Greek: κατ' ἐξοχὴν (*kat' exokhēn*)</note>, even to this day.

51. They had indeed begun to call themselves *Brethren of the Law of Christ*; both to distinguish themselves from the Papal Orders (*the Benedictine Order, the Franciscan Order*, etc.) and to stand upon the foundation of Master Hus, that *the Law of Christ is sufficient for the government of the Church militant*, and so on. But after they saw this being turned into a slander (with their adversaries crying out that *a new form of Monasticism was being founded*), they abandoned it, not unwisely naming themselves the *Unity of the Brotherly Church*, or the UNITY OF THE BRETHREN. For just as the CHURCH *is a gathering of those called out from the world to share in salvation through faith in Christ, bound together by the laws of mutual charity*: so an Ecclesiastical UNITY *is for us a gathering of churches, bound together by the laws of mutual love for the sake of mutual edification in their common salvation*. This is entirely in accordance with the mind of the Holy Spirit, as expressed in Psalm 133, Ephesians 4, and throughout the Scriptures.

52. These beginnings of a Church starting to be reformed according to the laws of the Gospel displeased Satan; *he therefore stirred up a new and atrocious storm to overwhelm it*. For as it spread...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the early Unitas Fratrum. On the previous page, the Brethren had settled in the estate of Litice to practice their faith in simplicity, but their growth has now begun to draw the ire of the religious and political establishment.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>

-> # Persecutions <-

-> ### B 3 ### <-

52. For as the report of this matter spread far and wide, the Priests everywhere incited the common people to hate them. "Suffocate," they shouted, "suffocate the spark, lest it grow into a flame." Even **Rokyzana** himself (which may surprise you) took on a hostile mind, accusing them of impious recklessness. For there were many who reported them to the King and the Consistory under various charges.

53. Thus it happened (in the year 1461), when **Brother Gregory** was visiting his people in Prague with several others, and they were holding a meeting in a certain house, that they were betrayed and captured; here, a notable thing occurred. The Magistrate, having entered, stood on the threshold of the heated room and addressed them with the words of Scripture: *"All who wish to live godly in Christ Jesus,"* he said, *"shall suffer persecution. Therefore, you who are here, follow me to prison."* <note>The magistrate is quoting 2 Timothy 3:12. Even as an officer of the law, he seems sympathetic to their piety.</note> For he was an upright man, not thinking ill of the Brethren’s purpose; but because of the command of his superiors, he could not do otherwise.

54. The King, believing the slanders of wicked men—that the Brethren were attempting some sedition following the example of the **Taborites** <note>The Taborites were a radical, sometimes militant, faction of the earlier Hussite wars; the King feared the Brethren were a new violent uprising.</note> (as they said)—ordered the holy man Gregory to be subjected to torture. But he fell into a deep state, whether a faint or an ecstasy <note>original: *lipothymia*</note>, and felt no pain; and being regarded as dead by the torturers, he was taken down from the rack <note>original: *fidicula*</note>. His uncle **Rokyzana** came to him (after he had heard he had died under torture) and mourned him with many tears, repeating: *"Oh, that I were in your place, my Gregory!"* But he, having later returned to himself, recounted a vision he had seen in the meantime. He had been led into a most pleasant meadow, in the middle of which stood a **Tree laden with fruit**; upon which **Birds** of various kinds, perched on the branches, were feeding. In their midst stood a **Youth**, guiding them with a reed in such a way that none dared to rush out of order. By this sign, God undoubtedly wished to show him an image of that "little Church" <note>original: *Eccleſiola*</note>, of which he was, as it were, the Patriarch. He also saw three other Men, like guardians of that same Tree; whom he testified he saw and recognized in reality six years later, with the very same facial features, when the first **Overseers** <note>original: *Antiſtites*; these were the first bishops or chief ministers of the Brethren.</note> were being chosen by vote.

55. Indeed, after Gregory was released (through the intercession of Rokyzana), royal decrees were sent out by order of the King, to all...

<meta>This page continues from the previous account of Brother Gregory’s arrest and torture. It documents the increasing legal pressures on the Brethren and their forced transition into a life of concealment and wandering to escape state and church persecution.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>

# Of the first Brethren
<meta>catchword: three</meta>

55. Decrees were issued prohibiting the pastors of sacred things from celebrating rites without the traditional ceremonies; they threatened the death penalty for anyone who dared to administer to the Brethren (now marked with the hateful name of **Picards** <note>The term "Picards" was a derogatory label used by opponents to link the Brethren to foreign heretics, implying they were outsiders to the Bohemian faith.</note>) without these ceremonies. The Brethren, therefore, placed in great distress and left like sheep without shepherds, fled again and again to **Rokyzana** <note>John Rokyzana was the leader of the Utraquist Church in Prague; the Brethren initially hoped he would support their reform.</note>. They entreated him by the glory of God and for the sake of his own salvation not to desert a cause he knew to be of God, nor to hinder by his example the conversion of many from error—a conversion for which they were most ready—nor to suppress the recognized Truth in injustice for any reason whatsoever. They reminded him that he was the head of all the clergy in the Kingdom and would have to give an account to God for all of them, and many similar things. But when they saw that he remained hardened, they finally bid him farewell, including these words in their last letter: *“Rokyzana, you are of the world, and with the world you shall perish, etc.”* <note>original: "Rokyzane Mundus es, & cum Mundo peribis, &c."</note>

56. Irritated by these words, he again stirred up the King against them. New decrees were issued in the name of the King and the Consistory: *That those destructive men should not be tolerated anywhere in Bohemia and Moravia.* Although there were some who wanted them to be captured and put to death, **Jost of Rosenberg**, the **Bishop of Wrocław**, advised against it. He added the reasoning that *“Martyrdom is a half-cooked roast from which worms are easily born”* <note>original: "Martyrium esse semicrudam assaturam, ex qua facilè Vermes nascantur"</note>; by this, he meant that the faithful multiply through martyrdoms. He argued they could be more conveniently brought back if they were proscribed everywhere; for when they no longer knew where to turn (he said), they would return to a sound mind.

57. Because of this sharp inquisition established everywhere against the Brethren, it happened that most of them, especially the leaders, were dispersed through the mountains and forests and inhabited caves; even there they were not safe enough. Therefore, they did not dare to light fires for cooking except at night (lest the rising smoke betray them). Sitting around the fire in the bitter cold, they devoted themselves to the reading of the Scriptures and to pious conversations. Whenever they went out in deep snow to acquire necessities, they all stepped in the same tracks to avoid being recognized by their footprints; the last person would then drag a fir branch behind him to fill the tracks with snow. *Thus, nothing could be seen but the footprint of a peasant dragging a bundle of wood.* <note>original: "Ut non nisi vestigium rusticelli abustum trahentis videri posset."</note> Because of this living in hiding, they were later mockingly called **Jamnici** (that is, **Cave-dwellers**) by their enemies. <note>The term *Jamnici* is derived from the Czech word *jáma*, meaning a pit or hole.</note>

58. Indeed, the Brethren established a certain Order among themselves...

<summary>The Brethren are officially outlawed in Bohemia and Moravia after their final break with Rokyzana. To survive, they flee into the wilderness and caves, leading to the derogatory nickname "Jamnici" or Cave-dwellers.</summary>

It describes their clandestine meetings and the theological dilemma of how to maintain a valid priesthood without the traditional Catholic/Utraquist apostolic succession.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

<header>persecutions.</header>

<sig>B 4</sig>

<meta>catchword: those [referring to the slips of paper/lots]</meta>

Those three, having elected Seniors <note>In this context, "Seniors" refers to the Elders or governing leaders of the church community.</note> by vote, promised them obedience after the direction of affairs was entrusted to them. Once the principal leaders had been called out from their dispersion throughout Bohemia and Moravia by the counsel of these men, they even celebrated Synods <note>A "Synod" is a formal meeting or council of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine or administration.</note> in those mountains and established certain statutes. These rules dictated how they should conduct themselves among one another and toward others—both friends and enemies, as well as the King and all Magistrates—and similar matters. They frequently called for prayer and fasting for themselves and those scattered abroad, consulting the Word of God regarding those things required for a fuller reformation of life and doctrine.

59. Their greatest anxiety concerned the *Pastors of Souls*, and from where they would obtain them after those they currently had passed away. *To wait until some of the Roman Ordination <note>The "Roman Ordination" refers to the traditional apostolic succession through the Catholic Church, which was the only recognized way to create valid priests at the time.</note> might cross over to them out of love for the Truth was a matter of uncertain hope.* They also remembered that **Rokyzana** <note>John Rokyzana, the Utraquist Archbishop-elect, had previously suggested the need for a total reform but ultimately refused to break with the established church order.</note> had so often protested that *all things must be restored from the foundations*. Therefore, they felt the Ordination <term>Ordination: the formal act of appointing someone to the ministry</term> ought to be established at home, *by that power which Christ had given to the Church*. Their reasoning was that as long as they still had ordained men among them, those men could ordain others, and these in turn could ordain others to succeed them. Their desires and opinions certainly leaned that way, but fear shook their minds: *Would the Ordination be sufficiently legitimate if a Priest created a Priest, and not a Bishop? And how would they defend such an Ordination, if a dispute should arise, either among others or among their own people?*

60. Finally, after some years of anxious deliberation, the principal men—about seventy in number—convened from all of *Bohemia* and *Moravia* (*in the year 1467*). After many tears and prayers were poured out to God (*that He might deign to show whether the proposal pleased Him, and whether the time was now right*), they decided to test the will of God by lot <note>The "lot" (drawing names or slips of paper) was a practice used in the Bible, notably by the Apostles in the Book of Acts, to determine God’s choice for a new leader.</note>. They chose from their midst, by vote, nine men whom they judged more fit than the others to undertake the Ministry. Twelve closed slips of paper were given into the hand of a boy (who had been called into the assembly and was ignorant of what was happening), and they ordered them to be distributed among those nine men. Nine of the slips were blank; only three were inscribed with the word EST <note>original: "EST", meaning "It is" or "It is so," indicating God's affirmative choice.</note>: namely, the *will of God*, which they were seeking to have revealed to them. It could have happened, therefore, that all of them received blank slips, which would have been an indication of God's negative will. But it happened that those written...

Having used lots to select their first three leaders, they now seek a way to provide these men with a legitimate "apostolic" ordination that would be recognized by other Christians, eventually looking toward the ancient Waldensian community for help.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

<header>Brothers of the Consistory</header>

<meta>catchword: 63. And so [original: Atque]</meta>

...those three <note>referring to the slips of paper used in the lot-drawing described on the previous page</note> came into the hands of three among them: **Matthias of Kunvald** <note>original: "Matthiæ Kunwaldii"</note>, a most pious man; **Thomas of Přelouč** <note>original: "Thomæ Prelaucii"</note>, a learned man; and **Elias of Křenov** <note>original: "Eliæ Krenovii"</note>, notable for his remarkable skill.

61. They embraced these men with joy, as if they had been given to them by God, and began to make plans for their Confirmation, or **new** Ordination. Since they knew there were certain Waldensians <note>The Waldensians were an older medieval movement that sought a return to Apostolic simplicity; they were frequently persecuted by the Catholic Church.</note> on the borders of Moravia and Austria, they decided to consult them—entirely to satisfy the scruples of their own consciences and those of others, both now and in the future. They sent **Michael of Žamberk** <note>original: "Michaëlem Zambergium"</note> with two others to explain what had been done and to seek their judgment on the matter. There they found the Waldensian Bishop, **Stephen**; he called for another Bishop and several ministers to explain their own origins, the articles of their doctrine, and the horrific things they had suffered until now throughout Italy and France. In turn, they heard the report from our men regarding their separation from the Pope and the Calixtines <note>term: Calixtines: A branch of the Hussite movement that focused on the right of the laity to receive the wine (the chalice or "calix") during Communion. They were more moderate than the Brethren.</note>. The Waldensians approved and offered their congratulations; indeed, having conferred the power to create ministers upon those three men, they created them Bishops by the *laying on of hands* and sent them back to their own people.

62. Our people received this news with joy, and being eager for **Christian Unity**, they deliberated: *Whether they could merge into one people and one Church with the Waldensians.* They were highly pleased by the purity of the Waldensian doctrine and their dedication to the Christian life. However, they were displeased that the Waldensians hid the Truth and did not profess it freely; instead, out of a desire to avoid persecution, they would attend Papal churches and participate in idolatrous rites <note>The Brethren were strict about "separation from the world," while some Waldensians practiced "Nicodemism"—outwardly conforming to the Catholic Church to survive.</note>. It was concluded: *they must be warned about this, as well as several other matters.* Suitable men were sent once again to explain these things to the Waldensians, who testified that the proposal for Unity pleased them. They stated that they neither ignored nor defended the faults that had been pointed out, but rather *confessed that they had strayed from the original purity of their fathers; therefore, they were thinking of reform.* It was agreed by both sides that they should meet at a certain time to settle this business more fully. But before that time arrived, the matter was betrayed, and the Papists <note>The Roman Catholic authorities.</note> began to rage against the good Waldensians. **Bishop Stephen was executed by fire in Vienna**, and the rest fled in all directions (most were brought to the Margraviate <note>The Margraviate of Moravia.</note>, and from there later to Moravia, especially **Fulnek**).

<meta>This page continues the history of the Unitas Fratrum (Unity of the Brethren) following their contact with the Waldensian bishops. It explains the complex relationship between the two groups, the onset of severe persecution under King George of Poděbrady, and the eventual transition to the more lenient reign of King Vladislaus II.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>they raise up.</header>
<sig>B 5</sig>

63. And it was from this interaction between the **Bohemian Brethren** <note>original: "Fratrum Bohemorum"</note> and the **Waldensians** that the name "Waldensian" was later applied to them as well. However, they never wished to accept this title, often complaining in their public writings that it was attributed to them by error. This was done for the sake of both **Truth** and **Necessity**. For **Truth**, because they had not borrowed their doctrine from the Waldensians, nor had they founded their **Unity** <note>original: "Unitatem fundârant"</note> upon the authority of the Waldensian founders, nor were they reformed by them; rather, they desired that the Waldensians might be reformed by the Brethren in regard to certain harmful defects. For **Necessity**, because they prudently judged that the broad and public decrees issued against the Waldensians by the authorities should not be turned against themselves, but rather avoided. Nevertheless, they never denied that they had received the power of ordination, and through it an external succession, from the Waldensians—though they sometimes prudently passed over this in silence, as the conditions of the time required.

64. But God willed that the Brethren in Bohemia should share in that persecution of the Waldensians in Austria. In the following year (1468), **King George** <note>George of Poděbrady, the King of Bohemia who initially supported the Hussites but later turned against the more radical Brethren to appease the Pope</note>, having solemnly summoned the Estates to the Assembly, passed a bloody decree: that every nobleman within his own territory must work to seize any of the **Piccards** <note>term: Piccards: A derogatory label, likely a corruption of "Beghards," used by enemies to paint the Brethren as dangerous heretics.</note> he could find, and punish those captured according to the circumstances and his own discretion, so that this severity might suppress their separation. Thus, there were fierce persecutions until the death of King George on March 22, 1471. The death of **Rokycana** <note>Jan Rokycana, the Archbishop of the Utraquist church who had originally encouraged the Brethren but later became their persecutor</note> had preceded the King's death by fifteen days, and "halcyon days" <note>original: "halcyoniáque"; a period of peace and calm</note> seemed to return, with God miraculously protecting this tiny flock. In this way, the **prophecy** <note>original: "vaticinium"</note> of **Matthias of Janov** <note>A 14th-century Bohemian reformer who died in 1394, nearly a century before these events</note>, who had died a full century earlier, was fulfilled: he spoke of an "ignoble people" who would arise without the sword or external power, whom the enemies of Truth would be unable to prevail against.

65. For George was succeeded by **Vladislaus the Pole** <note>Vladislaus II, of the Jagiellonian dynasty</note>, a most gentle prince. Under him, affairs were to be more peaceful, had adversaries not stirred him up—though for a long time they did so in vain. For although in the very second year of his reign (1472) he permitted edicts for their extermination to be prepared, he ultimately received the Brethren's **Apology** and their humble petition—asking that he might tolerate them in their place of birth as faithful and quiet subjects who sought nothing but to please God, their King, and all men for the sake of good...

This page describes his reluctance to enforce it and the Brethren's subsequent search for a pure church throughout the known world.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>The Brethren with other Churches.</header>
<meta>catchword: edict</meta>

...soon inhibited it, as he did several times afterward: until the year 1508, when they extorted that kind of bloody instrument <note>An edict of persecution, often called the St. James's Day Edict</note> from him, though he was very reluctant; yet it was without the effect they intended. (Among other instigators <note>original: "flabella" — literally 'fans' or 'bellows' used to stir up a fire</note> was *Bohuslaus of Lobkowitz, Baron Hassenstein*, the most famous Orator and Poet of his age, and a member of the King's secret council. In the fourth book of his Letters to the King, written in 1497, these words are read:
,, This especially distresses me: that this treachery—which under
,, King George and Rokycana, although they were accused of heresy,
,, did not even dare to mutter—now under the Catholic King
,, both takes root and spreads its branches so widely that
,, it can hardly be uprooted and torn out by human strength.)

66. The Brethren, therefore, seeing themselves accused of schism <note>The charge of breaking away from the established Church</note> and fearing they might eventually be driven from their homeland, *having taken counsel with their Patrons* (whom they now had among the nobility), sent four men (*Luke of Prague, Mareš Kokovec, Martin Kabátník, and Caspar Marchicus*) to seek out, wherever they might be, a people serving Christ purely, to whom they could more fully join themselves as to the true Church of Christ. They went out, therefore (provided with a most generous travel allowance by the Barons), the first to *Greece*, the second to *Muscovy and Russia*, the third to *Thrace and Bulgaria*, and the fourth to survey *Asia, Palestine, and Egypt*. When they all returned home, they reported nothing except that all things were most corrupt, and that Christians, as if by agreement, were poured out into vice and superstition.

67. Therefore, the Brethren, gathered in Synod, deliberated once more on what must now be done. And so that they might clear their conscience from the guilt of schism, or at least before God in the meantime, and show some certain path to posterity, they established a canon: "If God shall anywhere yet raise up godly Teachers ,, and Reformers of the Church, they should join ,, themselves to them," etc. This was done in 1486.

68. Uncertain, however, from where they should expect these reformers, they sent once more (*Luke of Prague and Thomas Germanus*) into Italy and France to survey the assemblies of the Waldensian Brethren and to learn everything about them more fully. But when these men reported that they had found nothing except the remains of the godly hiding in secret, and the burning of some who had been betrayed...

<summary>Frustrated by local persecution and the lack of a "pure" church, the Bohemian Brethren sent explorers across Europe, Asia, and Africa to find a true Christian community, eventually decreeing they would join any future Reformers God might raise.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Bohemian Brethren’s search for a pure church and their early efforts to distribute the Bible following the invention of the printing press. It follows the account of their emissaries returning from travels across Europe and the East.</meta>

<header> ->they search for a union.<- </header>
<meta>catchword: -ly</meta>

...[those who] had been handed over <note>original: "diti erant," likely part of "traditi erant," referring to the execution of reformers</note> (among whom was *Jerome Savonarola* <note>A famous Italian friar and reformer executed in Florence in 1498</note>) they reported back; the good Bohemian Brethren saw that nothing remained except to sigh to God for the Christian people, and to maintain constancy and patience in enduring their trials at home. Just how many adversities they suffered (up until the time Luther was stirred up in Germany), the *History of the Persecution of the Bohemian Church* (from Chapter 21 to 28) records.

69. Meanwhile, the recently invented art of Printing began to spread in Germany, with various pamphlets and books, both sacred and profane, being produced (especially in Italy, at Rome and Venice). The Bohemians, therefore, thinking it right to use this great gift of God primarily for the salvation of souls, arranged for an edition of the Bible in their native tongue around this time (being again the first among Europeans to do so) at *Venice*. Shortly thereafter, once those copies were distributed, they printed it again and again at *Nuremberg*. Finally, they established their own printing houses at home (at *Prague*, *Jung-Bunzlau*, and *Kralice in Moravia*) and printed it even more frequently.

70. Around the year 1511, *Erasmus of Rotterdam* began to be celebrated in the talk of men, known as the restorer not only of languages but of a purer Theology. The Brethren sent their *Apology* <note>A formal written defense of their faith</note> to him (which had been written to King Vladislaus in the year 1508 and printed at Nuremberg) via two of their members (*Nicolaus Claudius* and *Laurentius Wotik*). They pleaded with him to read the book through and, if he noted any errors, to candidly point them out (as they were prepared to correct them); but if not, they asked that he might deign to honor it with his own Testimony. In this way, he would defend the innocent from injuries as much as was in his power. He replied (after several days) *that he had noticed no errors; however, it seemed to him that giving testimony about these matters was neither safe for himself nor necessary for the Brethren.* This was partly because those who had heard the rumors about the "Piccards" <note>term: Piccards; a derogatory label used by enemies to associate the Brethren with earlier heretical groups</note> would by no means be satisfied by it, and partly because by recommending people who were hated by everyone, he would only achieve one thing: his own writings—from which the seeds of true piety were said to be commonly drawn and by which the gradually reviving Religion was greatly helped—would be snatched out of the hands of men by Papal authority, though they were currently read freely by all. Since, therefore, the Brethren could not be made more noble or better by the addition of his judgment, he felt it was better that he preserve himself for the greater benefit of the Church. The Brethren, however, just as they had begun, pure-

<header> ->Of the Brethren with<- </header>

...so that they might propagate the Kingdom of Christ in their own way.

71. Nevertheless, Erasmus bore witness to the Brethren in two ways. First, incidentally, in his preface to the New Testament; second, openly, by responding to Jan Šlechta <note>Jan Šlechta of Všehrdy (1466–1525) was a Bohemian humanist and diplomat who initially viewed the Brethren with suspicion</note>, a slanderer of the Brethren. For in the Preface to the New Testament, he discussed it thus:

> „ To me, he is truly a theologian who teaches—not by convoluted syllogisms,
> „ but by his disposition, by his very expression and eyes, and by
> „ his life itself—that a Christian must despise riches;
> „ that one must not trust in the protections of this world, but
> „ must depend entirely on heaven; that one must not retaliate against
> „ injury, but pray well for those who curse; that one must deserve
> „ well from those who treat him poorly; that all good people should
> „ be loved and cherished equally as members of the same body; that
> „ the wicked must be tolerated if they cannot be corrected; that
> „ those who are stripped of their goods, who are driven from their
> „ possessions, who mourn—these are blessed, not to be pitied;
> „ that even death is to be desired by the pious, as it is nothing
> „ other than a passage to immortality. *If anyone, I say, inspired*
> *„ by the spirit of Christ, preaches these things, emphasizes them,*
> *„ encourages, invites, and animates others toward them, he is*
> *„ finally truly a Theologian, even if he were a ditch-digger or a*
> *„ weaver. If someone also demonstrates these things in his very*
> *„ conduct, he is finally a great Teacher.* Another person, perhaps
> „ even a non-Christian, might argue more subtly about the
> „ logic by which Angels understand; but to persuade us to live
> „ an angelic life here, pure from all stains, that is finally the
> „ duty of a Christian theologian. If anyone should object that
> „ these things are too simple and unrefined <note>original: "crassula, & idiotica," referring to a lack of formal academic or scholastic training</note>, I would give him
> „ no other answer than this: that Christ primarily taught these
> „ "crude" things, the Apostles emphasize them, and these things—
> „ however unrefined—so many true Christians and swarms of
> „ noble Martyrs have passed down to us. This "unlettered"
> „ philosophy, as it seems to them, has drawn the greatest
> „ princes of the world, so many kingdoms, and so many nations
> „ into its laws—something which no force of tyrants and no
> „ learning of philosophers could achieve. Nor do I object to
> „ their speaking that [scholastic] wisdom among the "perfect," if
> „ they so wish. But here, certainly, let the humble crowd of
> „ Christians console themselves; as to whether the Apostles
> „ knew those subtleties, let others see to it—they certainly did
> „ not teach them.

<meta>catchword: 72. Šlech-</meta>

<header> ->Erasmus’s Actions<- </header>

72. However, to Šlechta <note>Jan Šlechta of Všehrdy (1466–1525), a Bohemian humanist who had criticized the Brethren in a letter to Erasmus.</note>, who was portraying the Brethren in a bad light, Erasmus (acting as if he were their advocate) wrote back in this way:

> That those Brethren choose their own priests for themselves is not at odds with the custom of the ancients. Thus was Saint Nicholas chosen, and thus Ambrose. That they choose unlearned men would be more tolerable if the piety of their lives compensates for the lack of erudition. That they call one another Brothers and Sisters, I do not see why this should be criticized; and if only the same naming of mutual charity might endure among all Christians! That they attribute less to the Doctors <note>original: "Doctoribus"; refers to the Scholastic academic authorities of the Middle Ages.</note> than to the Divine volumes <note>The Holy Scriptures.</note>—that is, that they defer more to God than to men—they think rightly, etc. Regarding feast days, their opinion is not far removed from the age of Jerome. Now the crowd of feasts has grown immensely, etc.

73. As a century passed since the death of Hus, so that the prophecy of the most holy martyr might be fulfilled (*"After a hundred years, you will answer to God and to me"* <note>A famous prophecy attributed to Jan Hus at his execution in 1415.</note>), God raised up the reformer of Germany, the high-spirited **Luther**. He, being greatly offended by the very shameless sale of Papal Indulgences (just as *Hus* once was), began to openly and with great zeal attack errors in the year 1517. And indeed, he did so with greater success than anyone before him, to fulfill once again our Martyr’s prophecy: *"Today you roast a goose"* (*Hus* in Bohemian signifies a *goose*) *"but a swan will come whom you will not be able to roast."*

74. The Brethren, greatly gladdened having heard of Luther’s rise and his progress in purging the Church, sent to him in the year 1522 **Johannes Cornu** <note>Jan Roh (d. 1547), a prominent bishop of the Brethren.</note> and **Michael Albus** <note>Michal Bílá.</note> to congratulate him on the light of the acknowledged Truth and to pray that through his work the same light might most successfully spread throughout the world of the Church. By this means, Luther himself testified in his writings (to Spalatin <note>Georg Spalatin, a key figure in the Reformation and advisor to Frederick the Wise.</note> and others) that courage had been added to him, though he was already spirited enough. And having been greeted by them again in writing in the year 1523, he wrote back: "We are not yet able to achieve such an exercise of Doctrine and an upright life as we hear of concerning you. Things among us are still raw and proceed slowly; but pray for us."

75. The second legation, via the same two men, was in the year 1524, for the purpose of more fully learning about the discipline in the reborn Church—

...for the sake of improvement. But *when it was realized that no such regard was being given to Discipline <note>Church discipline refers to the internal rules for moral conduct and community accountability that the Brethren prioritized.</note>, they broke off communication with Luther for a full eight years.* He, also suspecting something sinister about them, began to criticize some of their institutions more freely in certain pamphlets written then, and in his *Table Talk* <note>original: "sermonibus Convivalibus"; a famous collection of Luther's informal conversations.</note>, as they are called.

76. But when the educator of King Louis of Bohemia, *George, Margrave of Brandenburg*, sought fuller information about the institutions of the Brethren from the Baron of Krajek, they wrote (in the year 1532) the *Apology of Doctrine and Rites, etc.*, which Dr. Luther, having adorned it with an elegant preface, saw to it was printed at Wittenberg in 1533, and again in 1538. From that time, the friendship between him and the Brethren remained intact until the very end, despite the grumbling of their common adversaries; as is evident from the books of Johann Faber, Bishop of Vienna, published in 1537, where he writes: *"The Picards <note>A term often used disparagingly for the Bohemian Brethren.</note>, who are also Waldensians, differ from Catholics in many ways. Luther has behaved quite variedly toward them in his writings. In some, he condemns them as the worst heretics; but later, given over to a depraved mind <note>original: "in reprobum sensum datus"; Faber is accusing Luther of losing his spiritual way by siding with the Brethren.</note>, he wrote to them most flatteringly, extolling them with full praise above all other Christians, commending what he had previously disapproved of in them, and rejecting what he himself had approved."*

77. In the year 1536, the Brethren visited Luther again *to discuss the Discipline and Government of the Church with him. Luther confessed (says the historian Lasitius <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602), a Polish historian who chronicled the history of the Brethren.</note>) his grief that, from the very beginnings of the blossoming Gospel, he had admitted everyone without distinction to the Lord's Supper, and that he had not established a discipline similar to the discipline of the Brethren among his own people.* The witnesses of these things which I report in good faith (continues the historian) were the then-delegates of the Brethren, recently departed from this world: John Augusta, a leader <note>original: "Antistes"; a high-ranking overseer or bishop.</note> of the Brethren; Erasmus Sommerfeld of Tumice, a nobleman; and George Israel, who told me these things—then a young man, now a bishop in his eighties. (Lasitius adds on his own account:) "But such a great man must be forgiven if, while breaking the Papal dominion and wiping away other manifold Antichristian filth, and being occupied with foreign wars, he forgot something at home. For who alone, at one and the same time, [could manage]
->all things<-

# Interactions with Luther

...notice everything or resolve everything? "We cannot all do everything, nor does God give all things to one man," as Luther himself writes in *The Bondage of the Will* <note>original: "de Servo Arbitrio"; one of Luther's most famous theological works, published in 1525.</note>. Human wisdom has always grown and continues to grow with the passage of time; add to this that what is lacking in one person may be supplied by another. So says Lasitius <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602), a Polish historian who wrote a detailed history of the Bohemian Brethren.</note>.

78. But when these matters with Luther were concluded (the same author writes), *two learned men came from France to Bohemia, bringing greetings from the Waldensian brothers* <note>The Waldensians were a pre-Reformation proto-Protestant movement; they sought alignment with the Bohemians due to shared beliefs.</note>. They had been sent to lament their calamities, arising both from continued hostile persecution and from certain dissensions that had started at home. They hoped that by learning of the perhaps gentler lot of the Brethren in Bohemia, they might draw some comfort for themselves and bring it back to their people. They stayed with our people for six full months, observing the Order and the unanimity in all things: the pastors' diligent care for the flock, the respect shown by the younger members toward the elders, and the daily exercises of all their labors, and so on. Just as our people, in turn, having observed their pious conduct and zeal, formed a judgment about the rest of the Waldensians based on these two men. And because they found them sound in doctrine and faith, they admitted them to communion. At last, these beloved guests departed with mutual consolations.

79. *The theologians of Strasbourg in Alsace, while discussing plans to restore Church Discipline among themselves*, wrote to the Brethren in Bohemia in the year 1533 by the hand of *Wolfgang Fabricius Capito* <note>A leading German Reformer based in Strasbourg.</note>. That letter was answered in writing, and they wrote again in the year 1540 by the hand of *Martin Bucer* <note>Another major Strasbourg Reformer known for his efforts to unite different Protestant factions.</note>. To these men, the Brethren sent one of their own, *Matthias Erythreus*. Lasitius described these proceedings, but here he admits to having gathered a few "little flowers" <note>A metaphorical way of saying he selected the best or most beautiful excerpts.</note> from the speeches of that most pious theologian, Bucer. *Church Discipline* (he used to say) *is the throne of Christ ruling in the Church.* When Matthias asked that he not be offended by his unpolished speech, Bucer replied: *Piety is what should be looked for in a Christian, not elegance of speech. It is clearly a divine work that you, though not especially learned, possess a church so well-ordered. We, however, although we may seem more learned, make little progress because we neglect Discipline. Therefore, we are discussing how to establish it by every means.* Indeed, while Matthias was explaining the Order of the Brethren's Church (when the theologians had gathered to hear him)...

# Interactions with Others
<meta>catchword: to be retained</meta>

...observing their usual practice, Bucer, unable to hold back his tears and turning to the theologians sitting with him, said: *“This is truly a heavenly hierarchy, rather than an earthly ecclesiastical one.”* <note>original: "Hæc verò est cælestis potiùs, quàm Ecclesiastica in terris, hierarchia." Martin Bucer (1491–1551) was a leading Reformer in Strasbourg known for his efforts to unify different Protestant groups.</note> In his letter to the Brethren, he also included these words: *“Among us, many have shaken off the yoke of the Antichrist <note>A term commonly used by Reformers to refer to the Papacy.</note> but still refuse to take up the yoke of Christ, etc. I am convinced that you are almost alone in the world at this time among whom a pure, useful, and healthy Discipline flourishes alongside sincere Doctrine. I have read the Confession sent to me and was filled with no small joy at seeing such a great light of Truth among you, and such order and purity in your affairs. Truly, God's generosity toward you has been great, that you possess such pure doctrine and followers who take such diligent care to ensure that Discipline is restored to the Church as if to its rightful home. <note>original: "quasi post liminio"; a legal term referring to the restoration of rights to someone returning from captivity or exile.</note> Certainly, we are quite ashamed of ourselves whenever we compare our Church with yours, etc.”* In a published book written against Latomus <note>Bartholomaeus Latomus (c. 1497–1570), a Catholic scholar and opponent of Bucer.</note>, he wrote: *“That method observed by the Brothers called Picards <note>A nickname for the Bohemian Brethren, often used by outsiders; it may have originated from "Beghards" or a reference to Picardy in France.</note> is certainly the best; they alone in almost the whole world have retained, along with purity of doctrine, the vigor of the discipline of Christ. The reality of the matter compels us to grant them this praise and to celebrate the Lord, who works in them in this way—even if those Brothers are looked down upon by certain 'preposterously learned' men.”*

80. Two other theologians wrote at the same time: Fabricius Capito <note>Wolfgang Fabricius Capito (1478–1541), a German Reformer and colleague of Bucer.</note> and John Calvin, then the Pastor of the French exile church in Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentorati." Calvin lived in Strasbourg from 1538 to 1541 after being expelled from Geneva.</note>. A portion of Capito's letter says: *“Your book of Faith, which contains the Order of the Church, was pleasing to us; I have seen nothing more complete in our age. This is because, alongside a solid Confession of Faith and the true use of the Sacraments, it breathes a spirit of holy Discipline and the vigilant care of Pastors.”* Calvin’s letter is found in the volume of his correspondence (under the title, *To a Bohemian Pastor*), of which this is a fragment: *“I congratulate your churches from my heart, upon whom the Lord has bestowed so many outstanding blessings besides sincere doctrine. For it is no small thing that they have such Pastors by whom they are ruled and organized; that they are so well-mannered; that they are established in such an excellent form; and that they are endowed with the best Discipline, which we can rightly call the best and even the only way of retaining...”*

# To the Theologians in Germany
<sig>C</sig>
<meta>catchword: new</meta>

...the bond of maintaining obedience. For we experience with great trouble how much it is worth while we long for it, yet by no means can we attain it. (N.B.) This matter causes me to often waver in my mind and to carry out my duties less vigorously. *In fact, I would despair entirely, were it not for the thought that the building up of the Church is always the work of the Lord, which He will prosper by His own power, even if all supports fail us. Yet it is a great and rare good to be assisted by such a necessary protection.* I will therefore consider our Churches to be properly supported only when they have been bound together by that sinew, etc. <note>The "sinew" refers to Church Discipline—the structure that holds the community together in shared moral standards.</note> Thus it happened by divine disposition that shortly thereafter this excellent man <note>Referring to John Calvin (1509–1564).</note>, having been called to the ministry of the Genevan Church, established there that Discipline (now known throughout the world).

81. In the year 1542, there was a new and final visit (rather than a formal legation) of the Brethren from Bohemia to Luther. Indeed, the highest overseer of the Unity <note>original: "Unitatis Antistes"; the Unity of the Brethren (Unitas Fratrum).</note> at that time, **Johannes Augusta**, accompanied by the minister **George Israel** and the nobleman **Joachim Prostiborius**, set out for Wittenberg to learn what might finally be <term>hoped for</term> <note>original: "sperandum esset"</note> regarding the adoption of Discipline in the Evangelical <note>In this context, "Evangelical" refers to the broader Protestant movement.</note> Churches (for which the people of Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentinenses"</note> had shown such great desire). Received as friends, they reported how the Hussite <note>Followers of Jan Hus, the 15th-century Czech reformer.</note> churches throughout Bohemia and Moravia, by gradually transitioning into "Lutheranism" (as they call it), were indeed receiving the doctrine of the Gospel, yet were improving nothing in the pursuit of a Christian life. They explained that this situation was detrimental to our churches, even though we had been commended by Luther himself and his colleagues for our commitment to Discipline. Meanwhile, licentious people in other places, boasting that they can have the "pure Gospel" without the yoke of Discipline, ensure that no one else joins us, and even cause some to leave. *Should such a great abuse of the Gospel be considered tolerable?* Luther replied, in the presence of the entire College of Theologians, that *the Papacy could not have been overthrown otherwise than by uprooting everything that smelled of the yoke of human superstition and the coercion of consciences; but now, while they see the world rushing into the opposite extreme, they recognize that it is necessary to resist and to restore discipline. Serious thoughts will be given to this matter as soon as it is possible to catch one's breath; for the Pope...*

# The Brothers and Discipline
<meta>catchword: truth-</meta>

...disturb everything once more with the now-flaunted hope of a new Council, and many other things of that kind. The Brethren added: that they could not expect a good outcome for their affairs <note>Referring to the German Reformation</note> (as they had already written to Luther in the year 1523) as long as they saw such great care for knowledge <note>original: "scientiae"</note> and so little care for conscience <note>original: "conscientiae"</note> being undertaken through their schools and universities. They asked that he might be pleased to think seriously about meeting the dangers which could rush in from that direction and overwhelm the Church, etc. However, after a very familiar conversation with Luther and other theologians lasting about two weeks, and a mutual comparison of all matters, when they were about to depart from Wittenberg, Luther received them with a banquet. In the presence of many professors of that University, as they were leaving, he said a final farewell and gave the right hand of holy fellowship to the entire Unity of the Brethren, uttering these words:

-> *Be you the Apostles of the Bohemians; I with my people will be the Apostles of the Germans. Carry out the work of Christ there as opportunities present themselves to you; we likewise will act as they present themselves to us here.* <-

82. A few months later in the same year, in a letter sent to the same **Augusta**, Luther inserted the following words: *Furthermore, I admonish you in the Lord, that you persist with us even to the very end in the communion of spirit and doctrine, just as you have begun; and fight with us by word and prayers against the gates of hell, etc. Salute all the Brothers reverently in the Lord.* On the day after the Feast of St. Francis, 1542. Thus the great-hearted Luther, despite so many urgings from the Brethren concerning the restoration of <term>Discipline</term> <note>The "Discipline" refers to the Brethren's rigorous system of moral oversight and communal accountability, which the German Reformers admired but struggled to implement.</note> and the Christian life, was not at all offended by them. He persevered in friendship with them until the very end: witnessing his gracious mind toward them and this matter not only to them alone, but elsewhere as well. **Lasitius** <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602), a Polish historian who wrote about the Brethren.</note> produces illustrious witnesses who heard these words from the mouth of Luther: *There have not arisen since the times of the Apostles any men whose Church approached more closely to Apostolic doctrine and rites than the Bohemian Brethren.* Also: *Even if these Brethren do not surpass us in purity of doctrine (since all the Articles of Faith are proposed by us sincerely and purely from the Word of God), nevertheless, in the ordinary **Discipline** of the Church which they use, and by which they happily govern their Churches, they far surpass us, and in this part they have greater praise: which to grant them for the glory of God, and...*

<summary>This page records Martin Luther's final meeting and high praise for the Bohemian Brethren in 1542, where he famously designated them the "Apostles of the Bohemians." Luther acknowledges that while his own movement matches theirs in doctrine, the Brethren's system of church discipline is superior.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Martin Luther’s admiration for the Bohemian Brethren's system of church discipline, while lamenting the lack of such order within the German Reformation. urgent.
C 2
<meta>catchword: year</meta>

*...to the truth, as is fitting. Because our German common people do not yet wish to submit to the yoke of Discipline, etc.* <note>original: "veritatem, oportet. Quia plebs nostra Germanica Disciplinæ jugum nondum subire vult &c." This refers to Luther’s admission that while the Germans had the right doctrine, they lacked the moral "discipline" or social order seen in the Bohemian church.</note>

83. It is absolutely beyond doubt that this greatest man <note>Martin Luther</note> saw the harm of that first deviation and desired reform; however, being distracted by other battles (against so many monstrous abominations), he could not be sufficient for all things. As wise men everywhere excuse him—including our own Lasitius <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602), a Polish historian who wrote extensively about the history and customs of the Bohemian Brethren.</note> (see above § 77)—it is certain that he commended his prayers and hope for greater perfection to God and his successors, though that hope has so far remained beneath his prayers. For when he departed from this life three years and four months after those final dealings with the Brethren, there followed his death (as the Chronicler Bucholcer <note>Abraham Bucholzer (1529–1584), a German historian known for his chronological works.</note> says) *two wars: the wars of the Theologians, and the Civil wars of the Evangelicals among themselves.* Alas, into what has the theory of the restored Gospel doctrine fallen, without the practice of discipline! (Note Well) Would that the Bohemian Brethren had been false prophets when, from the very beginning of this German Reformation, they predicted such outcomes—even in their very first letter given to Luther (in the year 1523), and often thereafter. *For there prevailed throughout the schools and churches a zeal for knowledge rather than for charity and edification* (contrary to the Apostolic protestation, 1 Corinthians 1:1, 2 <note>The text cites 1 Corinthians 1:1, but the context of "knowledge puffing up" while "charity edifies" refers to 1 Corinthians 8:1.</note>), *to such an extent that there was no room for a remedy, despite what Bucer, Melanchthon, Hemming, Saubert, Arndt, J. Valentin Andreae, and other most wise men, driven by God, confessed to the contrary.* Not that nothing was accomplished by these holy efforts, but that nothing was sufficient to extinguish the universal fire of hatreds and profanity. *May God set things right, if only by this sharp Discipline of His punishments now sent upon everyone alike!*

84. Returning to the Brethren, we must touch upon the persecution by which they also were overwhelmed in Bohemia after the Schmalkaldic War <note>A war fought from 1546 to 1547 between the Holy Roman Emperor Charles V and the Lutheran princes of the Schmalkaldic League.</note> ended in Germany. For in the year 1544, when the Pope had reconciled the King of France to the King of Spain (who was then also the Emperor, Charles V) with the condition, among others, that arms should be turned against the Lutherans on both sides: it happened that in France not long after, the Waldensians <note>A pre-Reformation Christian movement that faced severe persecution; they were often seen as spiritual cousins to the Bohemian Brethren.</note> in the province of Narbonne were cut down. The Emperor, meanwhile, prepared war against the Germans, which began shortly after Luther's death,
