It also marks the beginning of intensified persecution against the Bohemian Brethren and Waldensians as the Schmalkaldic War looms.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Luther, Bohemian Brethren, church discipline, Jan Łasicki, theological wars, Schmalkaldic War, Charles V, Waldensians, ecclesiastical reform</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the aftermath of the Schmalkaldic War and the death of Martin Luther. It details the specific persecution of the Bohemian Brethren under King Ferdinand I and their subsequent exile to Prussia, where their strict church discipline was highly praised by local Lutheran clergy.</meta>

-># Bohemian Brethren<-

The year following <note>1547, the year after Luther's death and the end of the Schmalkaldic War</note> was marked by the defeat of the Protestants. At this time, Ferdinand <note>Ferdinand I, King of Bohemia and later Holy Roman Emperor</note> brought his Bohemians to punishment because they had refused to provide aid to the Emperor <note>Charles V</note> against the Elector of Saxony. He dragged several of the leading nobles to prison and punished others with exile or the confiscation of their property. However, he vented his rage primarily against the Brethren, ordering their churches to be closed and their pastors to be taken captive.

The first among those captured was the primary Bishop, **Johannes Augusta**, along with his assistant **Jakub Bílek**, on suspicion of secret collusion—or rather, a conspiracy—with Luther. It was alleged that through his journey to Wittenberg, he had schemed to have Charles driven out of Germany and Ferdinand out of Bohemia so that the Elector of Saxony might become King of the Bohemians. They were therefore subjected to the most cruel tortures on three separate occasions. However, since not a trace of any crime appeared (for it was purely and simply a slander <note>original: "pura puta calumnia"</note>), and no excuse for a death sentence could be found, they were left in prison for a full sixteen years, until the death of Ferdinand (July 25, 1564), at which point they were finally released.

85. A portion of those who had been dispersed, including both pastors and their congregations, reached Prussia. There, they were mercifully received by Duke Albert <note>Albert, Duke of Prussia (1490–1568), a key figure in the Reformation</note>, who assigned them the towns of **Kwidzyn, Dąbrówno, Działdowo, and Gardeja** <note>original: "Guizina, Dubrawno, Soldavia, Gardia"</note> as places to establish their homes. Regarding them, there exists a memorable letter from **Antonius Bodenstein**, the Lutheran pastor of Kwidzyn (where the greater part of the exiles had settled), written to **Doctor Brentius** <note>Johannes Brenz, a prominent German reformer</note>, who was also living in exile in Basel at the time. This letter was recorded in its entirety by **Łasicki** <note>Jan Łasicki, a Polish historian of the Brethren</note> in his fifth book. Among other things, he wrote:

*“These young men are from the number of those Brethren whom their homeland of Bohemia expelled a year ago, and our Prussia received as exiles. Their Confession <note>The formal statement of their faith</note> was published in Wittenberg and commended by the judgment and testimony of Doctor Luther, of pious memory, our reverend father and teacher. Besides the fact that they hold doctrine that is in agreement with ours, they also possess certain unique qualities which are lacking among us—not without a significant risk to our consciences. They have such a vigilant care for souls that I have never seen anything more perfect: the most beautiful exercises of piety and repentance, both in the home and in the church; outward honesty of character, justice, and the true use of fasts, vigils, labor, and prayers; brotherly admonitions, their own judicial processes, and Church discipline, etc., so that...”*

<meta>catchword: me- <note>likely "meritò," meaning "deservedly" or "rightly"</note></meta>

<summary>This page describes the 16-year imprisonment of Bishop Johannes Augusta on false charges of conspiracy and the migration of the Bohemian Brethren to Prussia, where they were praised for their rigorous spiritual discipline.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of the Bohemian Brethren’s exile to Prussia following their persecution in 1547. It concludes a high-praise testimonial of their character before describing their journey through Poland and the inadvertent spread of their faith along the way.</meta>

->### [They] are received in Prussia.<-
->C 3<-
<meta>catchword: to</meta>

...they should deservedly be an object of admiration to us all; indeed, I do not know if Prussia could ever receive better men. **Martin Bucer** <note>Martin Bucer (1491–1551), a leading Protestant Reformer known for his efforts to bridge different reform movements</note>, a man of great judgment, does not hesitate to prefer them in his writings to all the Churches in the entire Christian world. He attributes to them what he grants to no others, recommending them to all the godly and proposing them as an example to be imitated. Other most eminent men—**Luther**, **Capito** <note>Wolfgang Capito, a German Reformer and contemporary of Bucer</note>, and **Calvin**—did the same. And certainly, if there are any churches in which one may find the discipline <note>original: "censuram," referring to the strict moral oversight for which the Brethren were famous</note> and dignity of the Apostolic men, with everything arranged after the example of the most holy martyrs, these would surely be the "little churches" <note>original: "Ecclesiolae"</note> of the Brethren. I judge that a people so holy was sent to these places by divine providence so that others might be stirred by their examples to think more correctly about correcting the many blemishes that cling to our own churches. It is a very sad thing to only announce the Gospel of God as a testimony to the world, etc. etc.
*Dated September 15, 1542.* <note>Although the Great Exile occurred in 1548, the author cites this earlier 1542 endorsement to show the long-standing reputation of the Brethren.</note>

86. Thus did Prussia <note>original: "Borussia"</note> congratulate itself at that time on those guests. But another proof of divine providence concerning the Church appeared at the same time: these same exiles, during their passage through Greater Poland <note>A historical region in west-central Poland</note>, scattered the seeds of the Gospel there as if they were doing something else entirely <note>meaning they spread their faith incidentally while traveling</note>. For when they were about to leave their fatherland, they did not know where to turn except to the Poles, who shared a similar language. Having met at a certain place on the borders of Silesia, they proceeded from there in a single column—about nine hundred souls with more than one hundred and twenty wagons. It happened that not only in Silesia, but even in Poland itself (though it still adhered entirely to the Papacy), they experienced more favor than hostility. **Lord Count Andrzej Górka**, the General Captain of Greater Poland, not only permitted them to be received with hospitality in the suburbs of Poznań <note>original: "Posnaniensium"</note> but even invited them into his own hereditary towns (**Szamotuły**, **Kórnik**, etc.). They would have settled there as his subjects had not a Royal edict soon been issued—which the Catholic clergy <note>original: "Clerus Pontificius"</note> had obtained through their persistent urging—ordering them to be driven out of Poland. They proceeded, therefore, into Prussia, though not without leaving a longing for them in the hearts of the Poles.

87. Because they had several Pastors with them, they devoted themselves freely to sacred exercises (with no one hindering them): singing, praying, reading the Scriptures, and holding sermons:

It also describes their journey through Poland, where despite Catholic opposition, they gained the protection of Count Andrzej Górka and began spreading their teachings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Brethren's spread into Poland during their exile to Prussia. It describes how their temporary stay in Poznań led to the conversion of Polish nobility and the establishment of dozens of churches under George Israel, eventually leading to discussions of union with the Swiss Reformed churches.</meta>

# In Poland

When various people flocked to these gatherings (as the human mind is always eager for novelty), it was inevitable that God would open the hearts of more than one Lydia <note>A reference to Lydia of Thyatira from Acts 16, the first recorded convert to Christianity in Europe, whose heart the Lord "opened."</note>. Because in the following year, the Bishop *Matthias Sionius* followed his people into Prussia, but was forced to stay in Poznań <note>original: "Posnania"</note> for the sake of his health, it happened that many opportunities for conversation with all sorts of people arose. As a result, a good number of citizens and nobles joined the Brethren’s discipline <term>discipline: The "Ratio Disciplinae," the Brethren's famous system of communal moral oversight and church order.</term> and requested that a pastor be assigned to them.

88. Consequently, *George Israel* was sent to them. Having been miraculously freed from prison in Prague and ordered by his colleagues to go to Poland, he was a man truly filled with an apostolic spirit. He spread the Gospel of Christ with such success that within just six years he brought many of the nobility—including the illustrious Counts *of Gorka*, *of Ostrorog*, and *of Leszno*—to the knowledge of the Truth. He planted nearly 40 churches (according to the count made by Vergerio, a former papal legate <note>Pier Paolo Vergerio (1498–1565), a high-ranking Catholic official who converted to Protestantism and became an advocate for the Brethren.</note> whom we shall discuss later). He governed them with such skill that the leaders and ministers of Lesser Poland, who had received their own reformers from Switzerland around the same time (and therefore called their fledgling churches the *Helvetic Confession* <note>The Reformed tradition associated with the Swiss reformers like Zwingli and Calvin.</note>), began to seek the Brethren's counsel and pursue a formal union with them.

89. However, this development did not please *Mr. Lismanini* <note>Francesco Lismanini, an Italian reformer in Poland who eventually moved toward anti-Trinitarian views.</note> (who was already leaning toward Arianism <term>Arianism: A theological movement that denied the full divinity of Christ, viewed as a heresy by both the Brethren and the Swiss reformers.</term>). For this reason, *an appeal was made to the theologians of Zurich and Geneva in the year 1556.* By this means, the affairs of the Brethren were given a fresh examination, not only in Switzerland but also throughout Germany and Italy (largely due to the zeal of Vergerio). The Swiss reformers highly praised the proposed union, primarily because they admired the Brethren’s system of discipline. Several letters written to the Poles at that time are still available; it is worth quoting a portion of them that pertains to this matter. *John Calvin* wrote as follows: *“I have the highest hopes for your agreement with the Waldensians <note>original: "Waldensibus"; the reformers often used this name for the Bohemian Brethren, identifying them with the ancient Waldensian movement of the Alps.</note>. Not only because God always blesses that holy Unity in which the members of Christ grow together, but also because I hope that the experience of the Waldensian Brethren—which the Lord has refined through long use—will be of no small help to you in your own early stages. Therefore, you must all work diligently to ensure this godly agreement is more firmly established.”* Thus he wrote.

90. Meanwhile, Wolfgang Musculus, a theologian from Bern,

[...Muscu]lus <note>The text continues a quote from Wolfgang Musculus (1497–1563), a prominent Reformed theologian.</note> wrote thus: *We embrace with the highest affection that admirable plan of God, that several years ago He willed to transfer to you the Brethren, whom they call Waldensians, having been cast out of Bohemia: so that they might be an aid to your churches in the knowledge and propagation of the truth. This was not in the minds of those by whose effort they were expelled from their homes; indeed, I do not think even the Brethren themselves understood what was being done by divine providence. But He who infatuates and blinds the wise of this world in their own counsels in wonderful ways, willed by this means to bring no small aid to the beginnings of the church being reborn among you.* And a little after: *I congratulate your churches on this decision, that you willed to be joined with the Waldensians. For I hope that fewer dissensions will arise among you, and if any do arise, they can be more quickly settled, etc., since hereafter you can aim at a certain form of doctrine in teaching.* Again, the same author says: *I think it was done wisely by you that you embraced that form of doctrine which is so simple and so tempered to the sacred scriptures that (as I remember) even to churches disagreeing among themselves, it seemed worthy to be handed over to be printed and spread throughout our Germany.*

91. *Beautiful also was the judgment of Peter Martyr <note>Peter Martyr Vermigli (1499–1562), an influential Italian theologian who taught at Strasbourg and Oxford.</note>, then a theologian at Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentinensis"</note>, when he wrote thus: I advise, moreover, that discipline, as far as it can be done, be introduced entirely into your churches. For if it is not received at the beginning, when men burn with desire for the Gospel, it will not easily be admitted later when, as usually happens, some coldness has crept in. That one labors in vain without it <insert>discipline</insert>, very many churches can be an example to you; which, from the foundations of their restoration, since they did not want to undergo this so-salutary yoke, could never afterwards be reduced to order by any just rule regarding morals and life; whereby it happens (which I say with great grief) that almost everything is insufficiently firm and threatens ruin on all sides. (Note Well) It is therefore a grave loss and the certain destruction of churches if the sinew of discipline <term>discipline: Referring to the "Ratio Disciplinae," the Brethren's strict system of communal moral oversight.</term> is lacking to them; nor should those churches be said to truly and solidly possess and profess the Gospel which either lack discipline, or* <meta>catchword: "illam" (it)</meta>

They specifically highlight the Brethren's "discipline" as a necessary "sinew" for a healthy church, warning that without such moral order, the Reformation risks collapse.</summary>

93. After these counsels were given and received, while some were still disturbing matters, it happened that several nobles of Greater Poland (among whom were one Palatine <note>A high-ranking regional administrator or governor.</note> and two Counts, *Jacob of Ostroróg and Raphael of Leszno*) undertook a journey to the Brethren—who were about to celebrate a Synod in the heart of Moravia—out of a desire to know everything about them more fully. Having arrived joyfully among them (on August 24), they openly reported many things: that everything was greater than what had been said about them regarding their true hospitality and singular piety. The number of the Clergy was greater than two hundred persons, and there was a great crowd of Patrons, Barons, and Nobles. At that time, Ministers of the Word of God <note>original: "V. D. Ministri," an abbreviation for Verbi Dei Ministri.</note>, 15 co-elders, and 2 Bishops were created there: *George Israel and John Blahoslav* <note>Jan Blahoslav (1523–1571) was a pivotal scholar who translated the New Testament into Czech.</note>; the former for the oversight of the Polish churches, the latter for the Moravian. There our people, having congratulated them on their good fortune (*says Lasitius* <note>Jan Łasicki, a historian of the Brethren.</note>), entered into a closer religious society.

94. In that same year, *Paul Vergerius* <note>Pier Paolo Vergerio (1498–1565), a former Catholic Bishop of Capodistria and papal legate who famously converted to the Reformation.</note>, *an Italian, formerly Bishop of Capodistria* <note>original: "Iustinopolitanus"</note> *and Legate of the Roman Pontiff in Germany*, arranged for the Confession of the Brethren to be reprinted (at Tübingen, in Swabia). Having recognized the truth of the Gospel, he suffered exile for it and found refuge with the Duke of Württemberg. Thus Vergerius wrote concerning the Brethren in the Preface to the Confession: *By what reason and led by what counsel I [published] this [Confession] of the Waldensians or Picards (as they are called)...*

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Reformation in Eastern Europe, specifically focusing on the author's firsthand discovery of the "Waldensian" (Bohemian Brethren) churches in Poland and his efforts to publish their Confession of Faith to dispel rumors and encourage other reformers.</meta>

# Polish Actions

C 5

<meta>catchword: "their"</meta>

*I think I must first state why I have published this Confession of the Christian Religion, which has not been widely known until now.* When God recently called me from Germany into Prussia, Lithuania, and Poland—inflamed as I was with a desire to visit and learn from the various churches of different nations—I finally, after traveling through Poland for a long time, came across about forty churches established according to the custom and prescribed rules of the *Waldensians* <note>While the text uses "Waldensians," in this geographic context it refers to the Bohemian Brethren (Unitas Fratrum), who shared similar roots and were often conflated by contemporaries.</note>. These churches truly affected me with a singular pleasure and joy. For in them, the pure and harmonious voice of the Gospel rings out; so much so that I could not notice, let alone even suspect, any error or controversy, neither in doctrine nor among the teachers. Their ceremonies are most chaste and pure, so entirely removed from Papal <note>original: "Papisticis"</note> superstitions and gestures that not even a scent or any trace of them remains. Their discipline is serious and severe, from which the renewal and restoration of life bears such fruit that one easily recognizes their root is not at all feigned, but most true and worthy of a Christian: namely, <term>repentance</term> <note>original: "pænitentiam"; here implying a total change of mind and life rather than just a ritual.</note>. 

When I had written of these things partly to the Brethren in Italy and other nations, and partly announced some things in person with great exultation to the most Illustrious Princes of Germany and other high-ranking men there, some were entirely ignorant of the *Waldensians*, while others seemed to wonder greatly at me, asking *what had finally come into the minds of the Sarmatians* <note>Sarmatians: A Renaissance-era classical term used to refer to the Polish people and their nobility.</note> *that they should especially admit the Confession of the Waldensians;* fearing, of course, that they might infect and stain the pure and sacred doctrine of Christ, which they had so recently received, with the decrees of those people. For this most serious reason, I thought I must strive with all my zeal to have the *Waldensian* confession—which was already difficult to find—reprinted and published. I did not doubt in the least that everyone who holds purer doctrine dear would, even from the first "taste" (as they say), celebrate with great praise not only the Confession itself, but also the Poles and others who had received it into their Church. And finally, they would pray to the Heavenly Father to preserve and [continue] the restorations of the churches so well begun among them, and...

<summary>The author recounts his discovery of approximately forty Bohemian Brethren churches in Poland, praising their purity and discipline. He explains that he is publishing their Confession of Faith to prove to skeptical German and Italian reformers that the Polish nobility have not been led astray by heresy.</summary>

# The Actions of Vergerio

,, may He deign to increase and make more prosperous every day through His immense goodness. 
,, Beyond the testimony of *Martin Luther*, that most Reverend and holy man, who 
,, approved and commended this Church's order of the *Waldensians* <note>As noted previously, "Waldensian" here refers to the Bohemian Brethren.</note>, 
,, I thought it not undeserved to add the testimonies of other men of the 
,, greatest learning, greatest piety, and authority: especially those of 
,, *Philip Melanchthon*, *Martin Bucer*, and *Wolfgang Musculus*, who 
,, seem to me to praise this same Confession even more than *Luther* 
,, did, although he praised it in the most serious words. 
*But though I am not unaware of who I am, I nevertheless warn the Churches reborn in Christ these past forty years not to think it enough that they could cast out Papal superstitions and other abominations from their churches; rather, they should diligently and anxiously care for the retention of the concordant, pure, and genuine doctrine of the Son of God, our Lord Jesus Christ, and finally for the introduction, in turn, of a discipline and <term>mortification</term> <note>original: "mortificatione"; the practice of subduing one's worldly desires and sins through self-discipline.</note> of life worthy of such doctrine.*

95. Sending this Confession to the Brethren in Prussia, he wrote, among other things: *“The Princes in Germany wonder what came into my mind to join myself to the <term>Picards</term> <note>original: "Picardis"; another name for the Bohemian Brethren, often used by outsiders.</note>: but when they hear me, they cease to wonder. And soon after: I hope I have brought it about that many in a great part of Europe understand what the Waldensian Confession is, and even love it; which is to be attributed to God.”*

96. Four years later, he wrote again to the Brethren (March 19, 1561) in these words: *“Just as the Spirit of the Lord drove me to depart from the adulterated Church of the Antichrist <note>A common, though harsh, Reformation-era term for the Roman Catholic Church leadership.</note> (and I departed ten years ago, by the grace of God), so now He urges me to seek that Church which seems to me the best of all, in which I may surely die and return my soul to the heavenly Father. I ought to do this (if it be His will) not only for my own sake, but as an example to others, whatever I may be. I gave an example by fleeing from the Papacy, because I abhor its doctrine with my whole heart; now I desire to give testimony that I do not disapprove of the doctrine of the Churches in which I am, yet I love those Churches in which a more severe <term>Discipline</term> <note>original: "Disciplina"; the formal system of moral oversight and community accountability that Vergerio admired in the Brethren.</note> exists. I praise, I say, these Churches: but the other...”*

# Disputes in Poland

...those shunning the yoke. Regarding this, **Jan Łaski** <note>Johannes à Lasco (1499–1560) was a high-born Polish reformer who, after being influenced by Erasmus and Zwingli, gave up his path to a bishopric to lead Protestant congregations across Europe before returning to Poland.</note> (a Polish Baron who, twenty years earlier, having spurned episcopal dignity in his homeland out of love for the truth, had gone to foreign lands and served as the Pastor of the Church of exiles in **London, Emden, and Frankfurt am Main**; and in the year 1556, having been recalled to his country, had returned) wrote thus to Jan Černý <note>Latinized as Johannes Nigranus, he was a physician and a leading bishop of the Bohemian Brethren.</note>, the Overseer of the Brethren in Bohemia, and his colleagues (in the year 1558):
*We have not yet established anything certain among us, etc. But a meeting between us and you is plainly necessary. For we must by all means resist the various attempts of Satan, which he seems to be plotting among us with his arts to disturb the churches and hinder the progress of the Gospel. They do not approve of our common doctrine in all things, and they are reluctant to submit to ecclesiastical discipline. Therefore, we must deal with these people through our shared counsel. And the Lord will grant grace so that we do not meet in vain, etc.* This the excellent Łaski wrote with the greatest hope, but it was in vain: for the meetings were held to no effect, both in that year and for several years following; the shifty **Antitrinitarians** <note>A radical wing of the Reformation in Poland that rejected the Trinity; they were often seen as a threat to the unity of the more mainstream Reformed and Brethren churches.</note> (still hiding their poison) were always pushing in the opposite direction, according to that saying of the Savior: *Whoever does evil hates the light.* <note>original: "Quisquis operatur mala, odit lucem," from John 3:20.</note>

99. After that pious old man, Łaski, was called to his heavenly home, a Synod was again assembled in the town of **Książ** <note>Historically recorded as Xians, a town in Greater Poland.</note> in the year 1560. There, the haters of Truth and Order became bolder and denied that the Elder, or Superintendent, should be chosen from among the Ministers, but rather from the politicians—convinced that their poison could more easily be administered to such men. They pretended two reasons for this: 1. That Pastors should have enough to do, each within his own flock. 2. Lest anything "smacking of Popery" <term>Papatum redo-lens: literally "smelling of the Papacy," referring to hierarchical structures reminiscent of the Catholic Church.</term> be left among those who had seceded from the Papacy. The delegates of our Churches who were present (Vavřinec and Rokyta) <note>Vavřinec Krasonický and Jan Rokyta were prominent theologians and defenders of the Bohemian Brethren's traditions.</note> did not yet understand the "depths of Satan," but when asked for their opinion, they said that our custom was contrary to this, confirming it with the authority of the Scriptures and with reasons. The Pastors agreed with them, but the Nobles protested with great contention.

100. At last, the haters of Order broke out so far as to say that a more perfect Reformation of the Church could be obtained from the Canonical Scriptures than that which the Waldensian Brethren possessed. Therefore, arguments were drafted by our men; this very re- <meta>catchword: re-</meta>

It addresses the tensions between "high theory" and "practical godliness."</meta>

# Concerning Church Discipline

...that such a reformation was established as closely as possible to the image of the ancient Apostolic Church; this testimony is given to them by the most distinguished Evangelical men of this time. Nor was our Reformation made hastily, or by any single person; rather, only that which could be found to be the best through the gravest deliberation of many, and whatever experience taught, the reason of the times added, and usage confirmed, was admitted. What need, then, is there for new and uncertain experiments? Especially since, through the experiment of forty years, the fruit of the Gospel is now sufficiently perceived to come through sublime theory rather than through humble and obedient practice. Namely, that the Gospel sticks only in the churches and on the lips of many, without the worthy reward of such great light: the renewal of life. For the testimony of **Paul Speratus** <note>Paul Speratus (1484–1551) was a significant reformer and the first Lutheran bishop of Pomesania in Prussia.</note> of pious memory, Bishop of Pomesania (who died six years ago), is found to be most true; complaining that things were conducted no differently, he used to say: *“We preach, and we do not act; the people hear, and they do not believe.”* <note>original: "Nos prædicamus, & non facimus; Populus audit, & non credit."</note> Finally, unless the Polish Evangelicals bind themselves on all sides by the chains of Order, the Work will not be stable for posterity. For Order consists in this: that some lead and others obey, for perpetual mutual edification.

101. Finally, because it was objected that *“the Brethren do not have an open Church with the full communion of saints, but administer the sacraments only to certain people dedicated to them,”* it was answered: Christ prohibited giving holy things to the unholy. Christianity must begin with repentance, not with the sacraments. Nor, according to the institutes of Christ, should absolution be announced except to those who are repenting and believing. Since both of these (Repentance and Faith) can be superficial and deceptive, they require examination; and examination requires a proper length of time. 

And because ascribing the power of salvation to bare sacraments, **by the work performed** <note>original: "ex opere operato" — a theological concept suggesting that sacraments are effective simply by the act of being administered, regardless of the recipient's inner faith. The reformers strongly rejected this idea.</note>, is the basis of errors in the Papacy, this error cannot be corrected otherwise than by a certain proof—and not a sudden one—so that the secrets of hearts may be revealed, and novices may be both instructed and tested for a long time and with caution. For **Cyril** <note>Likely referring to Cyril of Jerusalem (c. 313–386 AD), known for his detailed instructions to those preparing for baptism.</note> correctly observed that *“Christ did not immediately entrust himself to those who had professed faith in him.”* <note>original: "Christum ſe non ſtatim credidiſſe illis, qui fidem apud eum profeſſi erant."</note> **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an early Church Father renowned for his powerful preaching and emphasis on moral reform.</note> indeed [called them] *abortive Chri-*

<meta>catchword: -stians</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Reformation in Poland must prioritize practical discipline and the renewal of life over mere theological theory. It defends the practice of the Bohemian Brethren in carefully examining candidates for the sacraments to avoid the "error" of believing that religious rites work automatically without genuine faith.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for church discipline, specifically the necessity of ensuring that congregants are spiritually prepared before receiving the sacraments. ->Fratrum cum aliis<-
<note>original: "The Brethren with others"</note>

...to call those Christians, who are admitted to the use of the **Sacraments**, before they have been taught to abstain from all things forbidden by the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>. Therefore, a true minister of Christ, so as not to produce *abortive* Christians <note>Those brought into the faith prematurely or without proper spiritual formation.</note>, must first require worthy fruits of repentance from all his people. And because there are three parts of the Ministry: 1. **Doctrine**. 2. **The Keys** <note>The "Power of the Keys" (Matthew 16:19), referring to the church's authority to discipline members and forgive or retain sins.</note>. 3. **The Sacraments**: the Sacraments cannot be administered except to those who both fully receive sound Doctrine and submit their lives to the laws of **Discipline**. If someone is unwilling, how will they be part of the flock? And before they become part of the flock, how will they be mingled with the flock? Should the laws of Order be disturbed for the sake of the licentious, who exempt themselves from the laws of Order? etc.

102. These matters, then discussed at greater length, are at our hand; yet their force was not then enough to make the stubborn yield. On the contrary, the Conclusions of that Synod were so conceived that it was clearly evident that *political* prudence had prevailed over *divine* wisdom, and the *carnal* over the *spiritual*. For the body of the Church was not being formed as the Apostles wished (*Ephesians 4:16*), so that it might receive growth from the power acting within for its own building up in love; but rather that which the Apostles went to guard against (*ibidem v. 14*), where unstable men, always children, might be tossed about and carried by every wind of doctrine, through the craftiness of old men for the sake of skillful deceit. This, I say, became evident as the dizzying **Arian spirit** <note>Arianism was a Christological heresy that denied the full divinity of Christ; in Poland, this led to the rise of the Minor Reformed Church or "Polish Brethren."</note> exerted itself more uncontrollably day by day, disturbing the faith and peace of the Church; until finally the Orthodox were forced to wake up, drive those disturbers away from themselves, and bind themselves together within by the firmer bonds of the Spirit.

103. For finally, the counsels of so many pious and wise men found a place, so that they received the Order of the **Bohemian Brethren** into the Church of **Lesser Poland**. They established from the very number of the Pastors—for their churches scattered through the widest provinces—five **Bishops** or **Seniors** (**Cracow**, **Sandomierz**, **Lublin**, **Russia** <note>The region of Red Ruthenia/Ruś.</note>, and **Belz**) with as many Co-Seniors, to be both elected and ordained hereafter by a solemn rite. Likewise, they established Pastors and Deacons (or Catechists), and Acolytes (or Lectors); also Political <note>Lay elders who handled the secular or administrative affairs of the church.</note> **Elders** for each individual church; and Synods

->### Union in Poland<-
<note>original: "in Pol. conjunctio."</note>

...to be celebrated every year (five at the district level, and one at the Provincial level). The churches of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania also followed this example; they divided themselves into six dioceses under the same number of Seniors and Co-Seniors. And because some differences in rituals remained, all these churches eventually set out to smooth these over among themselves, so that nothing seemed to remain in the way of full fraternal concord.

104. Nevertheless, because the Jesuits—with their shouting and accusations to the King—accused the Protestants <note>original: "Evangelicis"; a term used broadly for those of the Reformation.</note> of being a chaos of errors and schisms, the Protestants took action. Just as they had already openly separated themselves from the overt heretics, the *Arians* (later called *Socinians* <note>Followers of Fausto Sozzini who denied the Trinity; they were often excluded from mainstream Protestant agreements.</note> by the recent reviser Socinus), they were now filled with a desire to unite themselves more and more. They entered into an even fuller union <note>original: "syncretismum", used here in its 16th-century sense of a formal agreement or theological "blending" for the sake of peace.</note> by calling a general Synod for all Protestant churches at *Sandomierz* <note>original: "Sendomiriam"</note> in the year 1570, by the permission of the most pious King Sigismund Augustus. There, a *Consensus* <note>An agreement or accord.</note> was established in the Christian Faith and Religion among all the churches of the *Augsburg*, *Bohemian*, and *Helvetic Confessions* <note>These represent the three main branches: Lutherans, Bohemian Brethren, and Reformed/Calvinists.</note>. This event is well known, as the booklets of that Consensus have been reprinted many times in Poland and Germany—even though later the *Augsburgers* <note>Lutherans</note> (disturbed in various ways by their own people in Germany, who were accustomed to theological disputes) seemed to shrink back from it.

105. And so, at that time in Poland, the moderate counsels of the Bohemian Brethren found this outcome, with the applause of all good men. It is worth remembering one man, *Jan Łasicki* <note>original: "Iohannis Lasitsky", a significant historian of the Brethren.</note>, a learned Polish Nobleman. While still a young man, four years before this consensus was entered into, he converted from Catholicism <note>original: "ex Pontificio"</note> to the Protestant faith. When he heard that, besides the Helvetic Confession already accepted in his homeland, there were other Protestants in Greater Poland belonging to the Augsburg and Bohemian Confessions, he was eager to learn how they differed, and having undertaken a journey to them, he examined everything in person. Just as when he was leading the sons of certain illustrious nobles into Germany and France, he visited the Brethren on a journey through Bohemia, inquiring into everything and recording everything he saw that was worth remembering. From this, he later composed their history (under the title *Eight Books on the Origin and Deeds of the Bohemian Brethren* <note>original: "De Origine & rebus gestis Fratrum Bohemicorum libri octo"</note>). Where, in *Book VII*, he [describes] this...

->### Testimony of Lasitius regarding the Brethren<-
<note>original: "Lasitii de F F. testim." - Jan Łasicki (1534–1602) was a Polish historian and theologian who wrote extensively about the Unity of the Brethren.</note>

...he even ascribes the Consensus of the Churches at Sandomierz <note>original: "Sendomiriensem Ecclesiarum Consensum"</note> primarily to their moderation.

106. Regarding himself, however, Lasitius relates these things: *In the year 1567, setting out in the month of April for the sake of my studies to Heidelberg, and then in 1571 returning home from Strasbourg <note>original: "Argentoratô"</note>, I turned aside slightly from my journey to visit the Brethren in Prague and Boleslavia <note>Mladá Boleslav, a major center for the Unity of the Brethren.</note>. At that time, their presiding bishop throughout Bohemia was Jan Kalef <note>original: "Iohannes Calephus"; a significant leader of the Unitas Fratrum known for his strictness.</note>, a man serious and stern, as they all are. Immortal God! What and how great a joy then flared up in my heart when I saw and learned all these things which are now written here, and which will be faithfully reported in the eighth book. Certainly, by diligently examining every detail and inquiring about everything, I seemed to myself to be in the Church of Ephesus, or that of Thessaloniki, or any other Apostolic church. I was seeing with my own eyes and drinking in with my own ears those very things we read about in the Apostolic writings, the letters of Ignatius the Martyr, the Apologies of Justin, and the Apologeticus of Tertullian.*

*Truly, all those people are extremely unjust and harmful to the Brethren who—under the influence of that malignant and envious one <note>A reference to the Devil.</note>—detract from their holy way of life, disparage the faith that produces pious works, and thus cause harm to good, innocent, and quiet men. Bohemia is ignorant, and Moravia does not know what kind of inhabitants it possesses; otherwise, the land would offer them more honor and love them more than it currently does. They are entirely worthy of having the rudders of the whole Church entrusted to them, if we wish for the Church to be holy and without blemish, and to return to that flower and vigor of the Apostolic age. But all these things are hoped for in vain: the "whitewashed walls" <note>original: "parietes dealbati"; a biblical reference to hypocritical leaders from Acts 23:3.</note> will never yield to true Bishops, nor will the Sheep of Christ who have been turned into goats endure any Pastors other than those like themselves. Babylon <note>A common Reformation-era metaphor for what they perceived as the corrupt or worldly Church.</note> will live in luxury until that one hour when the destined plagues arrive—plagues which Noah, Lot, Daniel, and Tobias shall escape safely, with God as their defender.*

*These were the things written then by a man who was perhaps a too-fervent convert <note>original: "proselytus"</note>; nevertheless, they serve as a stimulus for examining matters more keenly, considering not only those things which shine on the outside, but weighing everything. For it is indeed possible that under a very rough clod of earth, a very beautiful little gem may lie hidden. God mostly chooses those things which are foolish to the world, and weak, and ignoble, and held for nothing, and as if they are not. (1 Corinthians 1:26-29)*

<summary>This page records the historian Jan Łasicki’s glowing 16th-century eyewitness account of the Unity of the Brethren, whom he compares to the pure, original Apostolic Church. He rebukes those in Bohemia and Moravia who persecute the group, suggesting their disciplined lifestyle is a model for all Christendom.</summary>

### Beza

107. Lasitius had sent his history, in whatever form it was first arranged, from Germany to the authorities in Geneva for their review; he wrote about this matter to *Jan Łorenc* <note>original: "Ioh. Laurentium F. F."; Jan Łorenc (1519–1587) was a prominent senior/bishop of the Brethren in Poland.</note>, an overseer in Poland: *I have written to Master Beza regarding your affairs, so that if he advises it, they may be published. And why not? Christ commanded the light to shine openly, etc. Perhaps they could be published until something better comes along; for in the meantime, the interest for the Lord would increase among many from those good things which He has bestowed upon you.* But Beza replied to *Lasitius* in this way: *I am finally returning to you the writing on the Churches of the Waldensian Brethren <note>original: "Valdensium Fratrum Ecclesiis"; here referring to the Unity of the Brethren, who were often historically linked to or called Waldensians by outsiders.</note>, which you had sent to me to be read and judged. There are many things in it that I plainly admire; and would that there were some hope that they could be introduced into our Churches! Some things, however, I judge should not be proposed for imitation, because in them, consideration was given to the specific time and circumstances.*

108. But let us return to the Brethren and how they were faring in the meantime at home (in Bohemia and Moravia); they fared better, certainly, under the peaceful Prince *Maximilian* <note>Emperor Maximilian II (reigned 1564–1576), known for his religious moderation.</note>, who employed moderate counsels. Since *Johannes Crato* <note>Johannes Crato von Krafftheim (1519–1585), a famous physician and humanist.</note>, the Chief Physician and a friend of the Brethren, was among his closest confidants, it happened once that while he was riding alone in a carriage with the Emperor, who was wandering into the countryside for the sake of his health, the Emperor sorrowfully recounted *so many and such great dissensions in Christianity*. Finally, he asked Crato *which of the many sects he thought came closest to Apostolic simplicity*. Crato replied: *I do not know whether that might not be granted to the Brethren, whom they call Piccards <note>original: "Piccardos"; a common, though sometimes derogatory, nickname for the Bohemian Brethren.</note>.* When the Emperor added, *I feel the same way*, Crato dared to suggest to the Brethren (whom he knew were preparing a new edition of their *German Hymnal*) that they should dedicate it to the Emperor. This was done in the year 1566. In that dedicatory letter, they inserted their hope, and that of all good men, regarding the promotion of a universal Reformation of the Church by His Imperial Majesty. They did not hesitate to encourage him with the examples of *David, Jehoshaphat, Josiah, Constantine, and Theodosius*, and finally to offer their own talent—according to the measure of the gifts God had distributed to them—for such a common work. This is shown by the preface itself (which all the books of those Hymns, even those reprinted later, still display). And it is likely that the most holy Prince would not have lacked the will, if not for the arts of those who... <note>The text breaks off, to be continued on the next page.</note>

# Testimonia

(had it been permitted for them to hold the scepters, even though they held his hands bound)

109. Indeed, in the third year of his reign (1565), the haters of Truth were brewing a new persecution against the Brethren; for this purpose, they misused the authority of the Chancellor of Bohemia, Lord **Joachim of Neuhaus** <note>original: "de Nova domo"; Joachim von Hradec (1526–1565), a powerful Catholic noble.</note>. Having traveled to Vienna, he used every possible insistence to ensure that the Emperor—though most unwilling—would sign a new Mandate to enforce the **Vladislav mandate** <note>original: "Wladislavianum"; the Mandate of St. James (1508) which outlawed the Brethren's activities.</note> against the **Picards** <note>original: "Piccardos"; a derogatory term used by opponents to refer to the Unity of the Brethren.</note>. But the goodness of God watched over His own, and He did not wish the best of Princes to be stained with the blood of the innocent or burdened by the groans of the oppressed. For when the Chancellor was returning, now armed with the decree, he had scarcely passed through the gates of Vienna and was crossing the bridge of the **Danube** when it happened that the structure of the bridge gaped open as the piles gave way, delivering him to be swallowed by the waters of the Danube. He was thus drowned along with his retinue; only six horsemen swam out, along with a noble youth who finally departed this life at a very advanced age, a long-lived witness to that horrific judgment, but who had professed the religion of the Brethren, for which he had learned that God then stood watch. While floating on the waters of the Danube, this youth saw his Master cast up again by the waves and held him by his golden chain until the fishermen, whom he saw approaching with a small boat, could come to his aid. The Baron was thus pulled out, but he was dead; as for the Chest, in which he had enclosed those deadly instruments <note>original: "feralia instrumenta"; referring to the legal documents for persecution.</note>, it was submerged so deep that it never came into the sight of any man again. Nor was there anyone who wished to attempt the matter a second time. (**Thuanus** <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a famous French historian.</note> also mentions this story in Book 36, and dates this fall to the 10th of December.)

110. When the copies of the **Confession of the Brethren** (even the recent Tübingen edition) had been so widely distributed that they were in short supply, it was reprinted at **Wittenberg** (in the year 1573), more elegantly than previous editions; it included the old preface by **Luther**, and a letter from the living Professors to the Brethren, in which these words were inserted:

> *We congratulate you and your Church for that spirit, because by the laudable example of your ancestors, you apply yourselves chiefly to this care: that you may progress daily in the knowledge of Christ, and faithfully transmit the precious deposit of the Word to your neighbors and posterity. We also approve of your **Church Discipline** <term>original: "Disciplina Ecclesiastica"; the strict system of moral oversight and communal organization for which the Brethren were famous.</term>, which, following the example of the ancient Church, you maintain without...*

It also notes the 1573 Wittenberg reprint of the Brethren's Confession of Faith, supported by Lutheran professors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bohemian Brethren's Confession and its reception by prominent European theologians. It follows the previous account of the political and providential events surrounding the Brethren in the mid-16th century.</meta>

-> ### Zanchius and Sarnecius <-

...superstition, and the snare of consciences, you retain among yourselves, severity, etc. Wittenberg, February 8, 1573.

111. After Dr. Crato <note>Johannes Crato von Krafftheim (1519–1585), a prominent physician to three emperors and a mediator for the Brethren.</note> had sent this Confession to Girolamo Zanchi <note>Hieronymus Zanchius (1516–1590), a renowned Italian Reformed theologian then teaching in Heidelberg.</note> (who was then living in Heidelberg), Zanchi wrote back in these words:

> *I have read the Confession of the Brethren which you sent with great care, and with great pleasure of mind. For I saw not only that the entire doctrine is consistent with the Holy Scriptures, but I also seemed to see the sincere and truly Christian piety of their hearts. Indeed, they seem to have set this goal for themselves: not to utterly undermine and tear up everything that is in the Roman Church from its very foundations without any distinction (as some do), but rather to build their Churches according to the true, Apostolic, and therefore salvific standard of piety. They reject what is to be rejected, correct what is to be corrected, and retain what ought to be retained; this, after all, is the true and legitimate method of reforming the Churches. And I wish that we all, following the example of these excellent Brethren, would apply ourselves to the same pursuit! We would certainly feel more piety, peace, concord, and charity (by which God is glorified and the kingdom of Christ is promoted) within ourselves, and we would behold it in others. And for what purpose, I ask, is the whole of Holy Scripture, the preaching of the Gospel, the administration of the Sacraments, and the institution of Discipline, unless so that—supported, propped up, and aided by these external helps, and with the Holy Spirit working in us—we might progress more and more every day in true faith and charity, as well as in the pursuit of good works and a holy life? And (as the Apostle says) so that the man of God may be perfect, equipped for every good work. Therefore, although I partially concede to you what you write—that there is more **Conscience** than **Knowledge** <note>original: "plus esse Conscientiae quam Scientiae"; a play on words suggesting the Brethren were more driven by moral conviction than academic learning.</note> in those men—I do not grant it simply. For these **infants** <note>original: "νήπιοι" (nēpioi); a biblical reference to the "little ones" or those of simple faith.</note> seem to me to be wiser in this ignorance of theirs than many **wise men** <note>original: "σοφοὶ" (sophoi); referring to sophisticated or prideful scholars.</note> with their multifaceted knowledge, etc.*

112. Lasitius <note>Johannes Lasitius (Jan Łasicki, 1534–1602), a Polish historian who wrote a famous history of the Brethren.</note> adds the testimony of Stanisław Sarnicki <note>Stanislaus Sarnecius (1532–1591), a Polish Reformed theologian and superintendent of the Kraków district.</note> (who was counted among the hundred illustrious Polish writers by Starowolski, and who was a Minister of the Word of God of the Helvetic Confession, and later Superintendent of the Diocese of Kraków) regarding the same Confession of the Brethren, which was as follows:

> *If anyone were to ask me how the Bohemian Confession differs from the Augsburg <note>The Lutheran Confession.</note> and Helvetic <note>The Reformed/Calvinist Confession.</note> ones, I could demonstrate it; all...*

### Ursinus and Others

...the German and French Confessions aim primarily at refuting and condemning Papal abuses; only the Confession of the successors of the Martyr Hus <note>Jan Hus (c. 1369–1415), the Czech reformer whose martyrdom led to the formation of the Bohemian Brethren.</note> not only destroys what is contrary [to the faith] but also repairs and restores the building of the new and regenerated man and of a well-ordered Church.

113. Nor was the judgment of the Heidelberg theologians any different (who were then piously concerned, under Frederick the Pious <note>Frederick III, Elector Palatine (1515–1576), who oversaw the creation of the Heidelberg Catechism.</note>, with establishing Order in the Palatine Churches), as is clear from their embassy and letters to the Brethren in Bohemia. Thus *Zacharias Ursinus* <note>Zacharias Ursinus (1534–1591), a primary author of the Heidelberg Catechism.</note> wrote to *Andreas Stephanus*, the Bishop of the Brethren:

> From your Confession, we are aware of both your agreement with us on all points of Christian doctrine and your care and diligence in maintaining a life worthy of Christians. And in this regard, we deservedly congratulate you on your good fortune compared to ours; and we ask of you that you aid us with your prayers before the Lord in obtaining His grace, by which a somewhat greater progress in Order and Christian decorum may be granted to us, so that by judging ourselves we might not be judged by the Lord. For although our desires and studies have long striven toward this end—that some better Order might finally be seen among us—nevertheless, because the name of "Ecclesiastical Discipline" <note>original: *Ecclesiasticae Disciplinae*</note> has become so hateful (with some pressing for it too imprudently and intrusively, and others rejecting it too sharply and without distinction) that a great part of the people turns away from it and wishes it abolished, we have so far maintained scarcely the name itself, or even a thin shadow of Discipline. Therefore, we see with shame and sorrow that more is attributed to us in your opinion than we find among ourselves; yet we feel ourselves spurred on by this, so that, being awakened, we may strive to respond more fully to the honorable opinion you and other godly men have of us, etc. March 19, 1574.

114. Having received a response to these letters, the Heidelbergians sent a certain *Badius* to the Brethren with these letters to the same Bishop (written by *Mr. Olevianus* <note>Caspar Olevianus (1536–1587), a leading Reformed theologian in Heidelberg and collaborator with Ursinus.</note>). *Lord Ursinus showed me your letters and those of Mr. Languet <note>Hubert Languet (1518–1581), a French diplomat and associate of Melanchthon and Sidney.</note>: from which I learned that you have for a long time been striving vigorously toward that goal for which we also aspire; namely, that not in*

<meta>catchword: dispu-</meta>

<summary>This page records correspondence from 1574 where Zacharias Ursinus, a key figure in the Reformed tradition, expresses deep admiration for the Bohemian Brethren's church discipline, lamenting that the Heidelberg church has struggled to implement a similar "Order" due to internal resistance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the correspondence between the theologians of Heidelberg and the leaders of the Unity of the Brethren. # Olevianus.

> Let us place the beginning, the middle, and the end of our Religion not in disputation, but in action—that is, in true conversion to God and a solid faith in Christ. I ask of you, therefore, in my own name and in the name of the Brothers who serve the Church here, that you explain the whole governance <note>original: *Oeconomiam*</note> of your Church to this John Badius and that you hold him in high regard; for he is a learned and very pious man. Mr. Ursinus and all our colleagues greet you. At Heidelberg, April 28, 1574.

115. What the men of Heidelberg thought indeed of those things which Badius had seen in the Churches of the Brethren is clear from letters sent again by Olevianus <note>Caspar Olevianus (1536–1587), a German Reformed theologian and, alongside Ursinus, a primary contributor to the Heidelberg Catechism.</note> to Andreas Stephanus on September 6, where he speaks thus:

> It cannot be said how much I value that work of the Lord which He has not only begun in your Churches, but has also continued for so many years. I and my colleagues are moved not a little by the extreme corruption of this age to confer with you concerning the best governance of the Church. For we desire to build in such a way that the building may be stable for posterity. We see, however, how much variety and how many sudden changes occur in those Churches which, having been stripped of what is, as it were, their own right, depend simply upon the political states <note>original: *Politiis*; referring here to secular governments or civil administrations.</note>. Unless these evils are met in time, many pious people will feel that they have (at least for the most part) run in vain. I say this regarding the stability of the building and the preservation and propagation of the entire administration <note>original: *Oeconomiæ*</note> of the kingdom of Christ. In this matter, we recognize what the Lord has granted to you, and we desire to recognize those things more fully, and we ask the Lord that He Himself might supply the means for that purpose. Certainly, when I look upon the sad face of the Reformed Churches in Germany, I almost shudder all over. I see that the political states were once the "guest-houses" of the Church, but now in many places the guest-houses are being changed into "lordships" <note>Olevianus is using a metaphor: the state should host and protect the church (*hospitia*), but is instead beginning to rule over it as a master (*dominia*).</note>, so that they rule freely over the Churches and even over the heavenly doctrine itself. The cause of this evil seems not the least to be that too many Churches have bound themselves to the political states of this world, as if the state were an essential part of the kingdom of Christ. Therefore, I cannot but magnify your method of building; you who wish your Churches to be subject to the political states of this world—indeed, to all men for their good—in such a way that nothing is lost of that liberty which Christ purchased for them with His own blood.

<sig>D 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: 116. Hu-</meta>

<summary>This page contains a 1574 letter from Caspar Olevianus praising the Bohemian Brethren for maintaining ecclesiastical independence. He warns that German Reformed churches are falling under the excessive control of secular governments (the "Politia"), threatening the "liberty" Christ intended for the Church.</summary>

# New Edition of the Bible.

116. *Hubert Languet* <note>Hubert Languet (1518–1581) was a French diplomat and Reformer who served the Elector of Saxony.</note> (of whom mention was made before) was the Ambassador of the Elector of Saxony at the court of the Emperor in Vienna, a man of great authority. I find several of his letters to *Andreas Stephanus*, and in one of them are these words: *The Pope fears nothing more than our agreement and union, which would be his destruction if it could be established; but there are certain insane theologians among us who reject all sound counsel, and by doing so, they perform a useful service for the Pope.*

> I wish that the Bohemian Churches, which you write are seeking the *Augsburg Confession* <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, written in 1530.</note>, would first unite themselves with yours. But these matters are placed in the judgment of God alone, whom we must ardently pray to protect His flock against those who attempt to scatter it. Regarding the Noble Lord of Z. <note>likely Baron Jan ze Žerotína</note>, I feel the same as you. He seemed to me to be of that class of men who judge matters with prudence and simplicity, granting nothing to their own passions: of which kind surely very few are found; most people (who otherwise do not lack talent) are so carried away by their passions that they sometimes seem devoid of common sense when they judge matters. Vienna, February 15, 1574.

117. Meanwhile, in the year 1572, there had appeared at Antwerp the famous *Four-language Biblical Work* <note>The Antwerp Polyglot Bible, or *Biblia Regia*, which contained text in Hebrew, Greek, Latin, and Aramaic.</note> by *Benedictus Arias Montanus*; and soon a new Latin version of the Bible by *Franciscus Junius* and *Immanuel Tremellius* at Heidelberg. Stirred by this, the Brethren applied their minds to preparing a new version in their own language <note>Czech</note>, conforming to the original sources (because all versions until then were only translations of the Latin version). For this purpose, they sent several candidates of Theology to the Academies of Wittenberg and Basel to devote themselves to learning the holy languages <note>Hebrew and Greek</note> from the ground up. To these was added a man born a Jew, *Lucas Helitz of Poznań*, a both learned and pious man, who was also made a Minister of the Gospel among us. So that these men might be able to devote themselves quietly to this sacred work, a place of residence was assigned to them in the heart of Moravia, the Castle of *Kralice*, and a printing workshop was established there under the patronage of the Magnificent *Lord John, Baron of Žerotín*, residing in the nearby castle of *Náměšť*, and liberally supporting all these men with his generosity. And God prospered the work so much that [we might hear] the Prophets and Apostles [speaking] in our own tongue...

# New Bohemian Confession
->D 4<-
<meta>catchword: 119. (No-</meta>

...so that we might have them speaking most authentically in our native tongue <note>original: "germanissimè loquentes"; though "germanissimè" literally means "most German-ly," in this context it refers to the "genuine" or "native" purity of the Czech translation</note>, as much as any other Europeans do. Moreover, a total of 14 years were spent on this work—in preparing a pure version, illustrating it with commentaries, and printing it in six volumes. Indeed, the first part, the *Pentateuch*, appeared in the year 1579; the second, the *Historical Books*, in 1580; the third, the *Hagiographa* <note>The "Holy Writings," referring here to the poetic and wisdom books of the Old Testament</note>, in 1582; the *Prophets* in 1587; the *Apocrypha* in 1588; and finally the sixth part, comprising the *New Testament*, in 1593. From this source, other editions were later derived.

118. In the year 1575, Maximilian <note>Emperor Maximilian II (reigned 1564–1576)</note> held a Diet at Prague and permitted the Estates of the Kingdom "under both kinds" <note>original: "sub utraque"; referring to the Utraquists, who practiced communion with both bread and wine</note> to enter into a *religious union* <note>original: "syncretismum"</note> under the common banner of a single *Confession*. This was attempted in vain—though with every possible effort—by both the Jesuits and the Pseudo-Hussites <note>Those who claimed the Hussite name but remained closer to Catholic traditionalism</note> to prevent it. For when they had inserted into their petitions and protests, among other things, that *the Estates under both kinds were not unanimous in faith, but nourished among themselves Picards, Calvinists, and Lutherans*; the Estates, so that they could testify to their consensus, entered into a plan to write a common Confession. To this end, certain theologians were chosen, and inspectors were assigned to them from the ranks of the Barons, the Nobles, and the Citizens. To these men, the *Prague Masters* offered the books of Jan Hus and the synodical and legislative decrees of the ancient Bohemians regarding religion. Those who had embraced the *Augsburg Confession* <note>The primary statement of faith for Lutherans</note> (now a large part of the Estates) offered their own Confession, as did those who were of the Brethren. They therefore compared the meaning and the formulas of speaking of each party on every individual Article of Faith. They expressed them in such formulas as any party could and would wish to subscribe to, *not descending into the overly particular, subtle, and scholastic differences of questions*. Their Christian moderation and prudence not only benefited them then, but also pleased many great men in Germany and elsewhere. For the Emperor ratified that Confession offered to him and took all those who signed it under his Royal protection. He only postponed to another time the power they sought for establishing a Consistory <note>A governing church council</note> and an Academy <note>A university</note>, meanwhile solemnly promising that he, or certainly his son (whom they had already designated as successor), would not fail the petition of the Estates.

116. Hubert Languet (whom I mentioned previously) was the Elector of Saxony’s envoy at the Emperor’s court in Vienna, a man of great authority. I find several of his letters to *Andreas Stephanus*, and in one of them are these words: *The Pope fears nothing more than our agreement and union, which would be his ruin if it could be established; but there are certain insane theologians among us who reject all right counsel, and by doing so, they perform a useful service for the Pope.*

117. In the meantime, in the year 1572, the famous *Four-language Biblical Work* <note>original: "Opus Biblicum quadrilingue"; referring to the Antwerp Polyglot Bible edited by Benito Arias Montano</note> by *Benedict Arias Montanus* had appeared at Antwerp; and soon after, the new Latin version of the Bible by *Franciscus Junius and Immanuel Tremellius* was published at Heidelberg. Stirred by this, the Brethren also applied their minds to preparing a new version in their own language, conforming to the original sources (because all others until then were only translations of the Latin version). For this purpose, they sent several candidates of theology to the Academies of Wittenberg and Basel, so that they might apply themselves to learning the sacred languages <note>Hebrew and Greek</note> from the foundations. To these was added a man born Jewish, *Lucas Helitz of Poznań*, a man both learned and pious, who also became a Minister of the Gospel among us. So that they could devote themselves quietly to this sacred work, a place of residence was assigned to them in the heart of Moravia—the castle of *Kralitz* <note>Kralice nad Oslavou</note>—and a printing shop was established there under the patronage of the Magnificent *Lord John, Baron of Zerotin*, residing in the nearby fortress of *Namest*, who liberally supported all of them with his generosity. And God prospered the work so much that the Prophets and Apostles [spoke] in our own tongue...

# Judgment concerning it.

119. (It should be noted: this *Confession of the Estates was written in Czech*, and was not translated into Latin, much less printed, until the year 1619, when it was presented to King Frederick by the Academy and the Consistory of Prague. Therefore, it does not appear in the *Harmony of Confessions* <note>original: "Confessionum Harmonia"; a famous 1581 collection intended to show the common ground between various Protestant denominations</note>, or *Syntagma*; for the work that is included there under the name of the *Bohemian Confession* is the specific *Confession of the Bohemian Brethren*, not this common one of the *Estates* <note>The "Estates" refers to the three political classes of the Bohemian Diet: the higher nobility (lords), the knights, and the royal cities</note>. Lord Bohuslav Felix of Lobkowicz and Hassenstein, who was elected by the Estates as the director of the project at that time, had only arranged for it to be translated into German, and he soon submitted it to the review of the Wittenberg theologians following the Diet of 1575. The Wittenbergers approved it, using these words among others in their German response to the Baron:

> "Although this Confession is concise, we easily perceive that in its composition, the greatest care was taken to express the primary Articles of Faith briefly, directly, and properly, to avoid superfluous length and the strife and bickering over scrupulous questions. Perhaps some quarrelsome people in our Germany might criticize this if the Confession were to appear in the German language; however, we cannot help but approve of your Christian prudence and moderation. Therefore, we wish to advise you openly that even if other judgments arrive from elsewhere, you should not allow yourselves to be led away from this holy and pure simplicity. For it is certain that the churches are best cared for, and are most greatly built up, improved, and preserved in unanimity, when the pure doctrine of the Gospel is set before the Christian people in simplicity, without the pursuit of ambitious subtleties and the contentions that emerge from them; just as Psalm 25 prays: *Let simplicity and uprightness preserve me* <note>Psalm 25:21</note>, etc. Given at Wittenberg, November 3, 1575."

120. Having been taken from human affairs <note>A formal way of saying he died</note>, the excellent Maximilian (in the year 1576) had as his successor his son Rudolf. Following in his father's footsteps, he reigned peacefully until the year 1602, with no trouble brought upon anyone in the name of religion. Finally, in this year, through the secret machinations of the Jesuits carried out by their agents, things were brought to such a pass that Rudolf signed an Edict they had drafted (concerning the re-imposition of laws against the Piccards <note>original: "Piccardos"; a derogatory label used by Catholics and Utraquists to refer to the Unity of the Brethren, often associating them with the medieval Waldensians</note>—

<meta>catchword: dos [completes "Piccardos"]</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes the 1575 Bohemian Confession of the Estates from the earlier Confession of the Brethren and documents its endorsement by Wittenberg theologians for its clarity and simplicity. It also marks the transition from the tolerant reign of Emperor Maximilian II to the early years of Rudolf II, noting the eventual rise of Jesuit influence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the history of the legal struggles and eventual victory for religious freedom in Bohemia, shifting from the theological approval of the Bohemian Confession to the political establishment of a shared religious administration under Emperor Rudolf II.</meta>

->### The New Prague Consistory.<-

D 5
<meta>catchword: who-</meta>

(who by Wladislaus's mandate) <note>Wladislaus II Jagiello, King of Bohemia (r. 1471–1516). The text refers to a mandate against the Brethren that was being revived or enforced.</note> should sign and order it to be published: however, with no other effect than that several Churches of the Brethren were closed for a time. For the Patrons of the Brethren, the Nobles, protested that this did not pertain to them at all, since they were not such people as those *Picardites* <note>original: "Piccarditæ"; a derogatory label for the Bohemian Brethren, implying they were heretics</note> were described as being; they easily obtained a concession so that the most mild Emperor <note>Referring to Rudolf II (r. 1576–1612)</note> would overlook the fact that his edicts were not strictly observed. Nor was it permitted for enemies to urge those edicts through other Evangelical Orders. It was also reported by trustworthy sources that after the news arrived on the evening of that day, July 22, that *Székesfehérvár* <note>original: "Alba Regalis"; a historic city in Hungary</note> (the principal city of *Hungary*) had been intercepted by the Turks, he said in dismay: *“I was expecting such a thing, after I began today to usurp God’s Government, which belongs to Conscience,”* looking back at his father’s maxim. <note>original: "paternam gnomam"; likely referring to the tolerant principles of his father, Maximilian II, who famously said it was a sin to rule over consciences.</note>

121. Nor did he have any other purpose than to fulfill the promises of his most pious Father and to establish liberty of Conscience, as he declared clearly afterward, when in the year 1609 he handed over the lower **Consistory** <term>Consistory: a governing council of the church</term> and the **Academy** to be reformed at the discretion of the Estates—despite the protests of the Pope, the Spanish faction, and even some of his own Councilors. He added the **Letter of Majesty** <note>original: "Majestaticis literis"; the famous 1609 decree by Rudolf II granting religious freedom to the Bohemian Protestants</note>, by which he both confirmed the gift and permitted the Evangelicals to either possess the Churches and Schools (those they already held) or to build them (where needed). He forbade anyone from being afflicted in the name of Religion (even the subjects of his own Catholic Nobles, or indeed of the Clergy); he bound the future Kings, his successors, to sacredly observe these things; and finally, he restored to the Estates the power to elect from among themselves **Defenders** <note>original: "Defensores"; officials specifically tasked with protecting the religious liberties granted by the Letter of Majesty</note> to be the guardians of this liberty.

<margin>Turks</margin>
122. The Estates therefore reform the **Consistory**: and indeed, for the sake of Concord, they elect by vote nine Ministers—three from the *Hussites*, three from the *Brethren*, and three from other *Evangelicals*—and they join with them three Professors from the Academy. They ordered these twelve selected men to care for the Ecclesiastical business of the entire Kingdom. And because there was now a consensus among everyone regarding the abolition of the **Compacts** <note>original: "Compactatis"; the 1436 agreements between the Hussites and the Catholic Church that the Reformers now viewed as outdated and restrictive</note> and the governing of the Churches according to the norm of the divine Law alone, the first **Administrator** elected was from the *Hussites*, **Elias Schuda of Semanin**, who was hereafter to be elected from the common assembly of Deans. Regarding the Brethren, however...
