<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the 1609 Letter of Majesty, describing the practical administrative changes that followed and the subsequent tensions that led toward the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

### Full liberty under Rudolf.

(To those whose Order of Discipline was permitted to reach a fuller Harmony <note>original: "Syncretismum"; here referring to the "syncretism" or ecumenical union between the Bohemian Brethren and the Utraquists.</note>) it was concluded that they should have their own Elder, the closest colleague of the Administrator, as long as the distinction of that Order remained (though in friendly concord). To them, the Bethlehem Chapel, famous for the preaching of Hus, was granted by the Academy <note>The University of Prague.</note> as the true progeny of Hus. All these things were carried out with the public joy of all good men; everything resounded with divine praises; and on the doors of the Temples, this was posted everywhere:

->The Temples stand open, the Lion rejoices, while Rudolf confirms<-
->*The promise of faith which you, Maximilian, had given: and similar lines.*<-
<note>The "Lion" refers to the coat of arms of the Kingdom of Bohemia. Maximilian II was Rudolf’s father, who had promised religious tolerance but never fully codified it into law.</note>

123. And so, a purer Religion flourished throughout the whole Kingdom, as *Pseudo-Hussitism* <note>original: "Pseudo-Hussitismo"; a derogatory term used by the author for the "Old Utraquists" who remained very close to Roman Catholic rituals.</note> gradually faded away: so that barely one in a hundred remained who did not profess the Evangelical doctrine. But alas! along with religious liberty (as usually happens), a license of life also began to grow, and Discipline (even among those where it had flourished before) began to dissolve in a strange manner. Therefore, that Liberty, bringing with it a security of the flesh, did not please all pious men, and some began to fear the worst: until the horrific Ferdinandian storm <note>The reign of Emperor Ferdinand II, known for his militant Counter-Reformation policies.</note> overwhelmed us, and that Prophetic word, *In my peace is my most bitter bitterness*, <note>original: "In pace amaritudo mea amarissima"; a quote from Isaiah 38:17.</note> was fulfilled.

124. For the enemies of the Gospel, filled with envy and turning to their accustomed arts, constructed a machine in secret by which we—that entire Bohemian Church which for so many centuries had resisted the *Nimrod Pope* <note>original: "Nimrodo Papæ"; Nimrod was the biblical "mighty hunter," here used as a polemical metaphor for the Papacy "hunting" souls.</note>—were utterly subverted. So that she must now lament with the daughter of Zion who was once overthrown: *My enemies have hunted me like a bird; they have cast down my life into a pit and overwhelmed me with stones; they have drowned me in the deep, I have perished* (Lamentations 3:52, etc.). For once Rudolf, the defender of our liberty, was removed from human affairs, and plans were arranged concerning the execution of the Council of Trent—beginning with the Bohemians—they acted in such a way as to provoke them to impatience and then to arms through exquisite harassments (contrary to the sacred laws of privileges). If the Bohemians allowed themselves to be led that way, the enemies would have a just pretext to crush them with the arms of the entire Catholic League as rebels against the supreme majesty. And so it happened, because of our sins against the [justness] of our cause...

<meta>catchword: just-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the brief era of religious peace in Bohemia under Rudolf II, the granting of the Bethlehem Chapel to the followers of Hus, and the subsequent moral decline that the author believes paved the way for the devastating Counter-Reformation under Ferdinand II.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf II, Bethlehem Chapel, Jan Hus, Maximilian II, religious liberty, moral decline, Ferdinand II, Catholic League, Bohemian Church, Thirty Years' War</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Bohemian Reformation's collapse. The previous page ended with a warning that the religious liberty granted by Rudolf II led to a "security of the flesh" and a decline in moral discipline, which some pious men saw as a bad omen.</meta>

# Full destruction under Ferdinand II.

...justice being overcome, while God permitted the enemies to at last fulfill every measure of cruelty against God's saints even among us.

125. For after the victory was obtained in the year 1620 <note>The Battle of White Mountain, a decisive Catholic victory at the start of the Thirty Years' War.</note>, and the primary leaders of the Nobles were partly punished by execution and partly scattered, they proscribed <note>original: "proscripserunt"; legally banished and confiscated the property of</note> all the Pastors of the Churches by a universal mandate (labeling them as authors of the rebellion). Then, they first attempted to gently entice the people to change their religion, and soon after, they tried to compel them through various terrors and harassments. Finally, when they had won over only a few in this way over a full six-year period, in the year 1627, they proscribed the entire Evangelical Nobility from their fatherland. They then began to compel the common folk, through imprisonment and torture, to renounce the **Chalice** <note>The symbol of the Hussite/Evangelical faith, referring to the practice of giving the wine of the Eucharist to the laity.</note> and to promise obedience to the Pope. In this situation, many were certainly ready—and even desired—to pour out their blood rather than their souls, but this wish was mocked. *The Emperor does not thirst for your blood, but for the salvation of your souls,* those new **iron-armed Apostles** <note>A sarcastic reference to the soldiers and Jesuits enforcing the Counter-Reformation.</note> kept saying. For it had been decided in Rome that, since the experience of an entire century had shown that *bloodletting* <note>original: "sanguinis missionem"; referring to executions or martyrdoms.</note> was of no use in curing the "fever" of the Lutheran heresy, other remedies must now be tried by the benefit of a *diet*: namely, through exile, prisons, and slow tribulations of every kind.

126. By a "diet" of this kind lasting forty years, matters were brought to such a point that within Bohemia and Moravia no Temple remains for the Evangelicals, no School, no private exercise of Religion, and no sacred Books—since the volumes of the Bibles and all other aids of a purer divine worship that could be found anywhere have been burned. Furthermore, those who fled the fatherland for the sake of keeping their faith with God and scattered through neighboring Kingdoms (certainly numbering in the thousands) have either abandoned their constancy, their spirits broken by the hardship of exile, or have been so consumed by the long duration of their calamities that we, the remaining survivors, are but a thin remnant: *just as two or three clusters of grapes remain after the vintage is finished, and two or three berries on the top of a branch after the olive harvest* (Isaiah 17:5, 6). *All our lovers have deserted us, nor do they look for us; indeed, even when we asked them for help, they hardened their hearts against us* (Jeremiah 30:14).

-># Of the Bohemian Church, now fully scattered<-

127. Indeed, we endure the wrath of the Almighty, which has been justly brought upon our nation <note>original: "justè toleramus" (referencing 2 Maccabees 7:58)</note>. But how will those justify their actions before God who—unmindful of both our common Protestant <note>original: "Evangelicæ"</note> cause and our ancient treaties—did not merely fail to help those falling in that shared struggle, but even incited the Babylonians <note>A biblical metaphor for the Catholic and Imperial forces of the Hapsburgs.</note> against their own Brothers and neighbors? Like the Edomites of old, they cried out: ***“Tear it down, tear it down, even to its very foundations!”*** <note>original: "Exinanite, exinanite, ad fundamentum usque" (Psalm 137:7). The Edomites were biblically condemned for cheering the destruction of Jerusalem.</note>. Others, while securing peace for themselves, forgot that the Bohemians were the first and most constant defenders of the common cause against the Antichrist <note>In this context, the author refers to the Papacy.</note> throughout so many centuries. We were worthy of being supported by a collective effort, at least to ensure that the light of the Gospel—which was first lit here and placed upon a public lampstand—would not be so completely extinguished as has now come to pass.

128. Therefore, for this most afflicted nation—which became more hated than others precisely because it clung so studiously to the Apostolic Doctrine, the footsteps of the early Church, and the guidance of the holy Fathers—it was attacked, cast down, trampled, and abandoned by its own allies. Finding no human pity, only one thing remains: to cry out for the help of the Eternal Merciful One alone, and to say along with the once-overthrown people of God: *“For these things I weep; because the comforter who should restore my soul is far from me: my children are desolate, because the enemy has prevailed.”* <note>Lamentations (Threnos) 1:16</note> *“Zion spreads her hands, but there is no one to comfort her.”* <note>v. 17</note> *“I called for my friends, but they deceived me.”* <note>v. 19</note> *“See, O Lord, how I am troubled.”* <note>v. 20</note> *“They have heard that I sigh, and there is no one to comfort me; all my enemies rejoice.”* <note>v. 21</note> *“Remember, O Lord, what has come upon us; look and see our reproach.”* <note>Lamentations 5:1</note> *“Our inheritance has passed to others, our houses to strangers.”* <note>v. 2</note> *“We are orphans, without a father.”* <note>v. 3</note> *“We suffer persecution; we labor and have no rest.”* <note>v. 5</note> *“Slaves rule over us, and there is no one to deliver us from their hand.”* <note>v. 8</note> *“Our joy has failed; our dancing is turned into mourning.”* <note>v. 15</note> *“You, O Lord, remain forever; your throne from generation to generation.”* <note>v. 19</note> *“Why do you forget us forever?”* <note>v. 20</note> *“Restore us to yourself, that we may return! -># ...abandoned by all.<-

129. Those in this century fare much more mildly <note>original: "hoc seculo multò mitius habent"</note>, pressed on one side by the Papal yoke and on the other by the Turkish <note>Referring to the Ottoman Empire</note> yoke, or even overwhelmed by the many-formed superstitions of the Greek Church. *Have mercy on your Christian people, so cut down and torn apart in so many ways, Jesus Christ!* <note>original: "Miserere populi tui Christiani tot modis accisi & concisi, Iesu Christe!"</note>

130. For what purpose are all these things mentioned here? To provide us with consolation: recalling that just as once, in the times of the primitive Church, no force, no torments, no punishments could halt the propagation of Christianity; so now this extreme fury of Papal cruelty will not be able to halt the course of the Gospel. For although its torches may be extinguished in some places (as among us), elsewhere they flame all the more strongly. Nor is God any different now than He was then, nor is His hand shortened <note>A biblical idiom meaning His power has not diminished</note>, so that He cannot find a way out for His own from the Babylonian furnace <note>A reference to the story of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego in Daniel 3</note>, while enveloping His enemies in their own flames. *Great is our Lord, and great is His power, and His wisdom is beyond measure* (Psalm 147:5). *He does whatever He pleases, in heaven and on earth* (Psalm 135:6). *Nor does He cast off His own forever, but though He has brought grief, He will again have compassion, according to the multitude of His mercies* (Lamentations 3:31, 32).

131. Others, however, by reflecting on these things and considering how severely the Lord has chastised us (who were also a portion of His inheritance), should learn to fear the judgments of God, which usually begin at His own house (1 Peter 4:17), indeed from His very sanctuary itself (Ezekiel 9:6). For whether they believe we were closer to the sanctuary—on account of our zeal for a holier <term>Discipline</term> <note>The "Ratio Disciplinae" or the strict communal moral code of the Bohemian Brethren, which they believed restored the standards of the early Church.</note>—or whether they believe themselves to be closer than us, the matter comes to the same result. Truly, if we were indeed closer to piety: surely those who had less care for the Christian life will have to hear that saying of Christ: *If these things are done in the green wood, what will happen in the dry?* (Luke 23:31). But if they were truly closer to the sanctuary than we were, then they should truly expect greater blows than us, unless, warned by our example, they repent <note>original: "resipiscant"</note>: *for he to whom more is given will be beaten more* (Luke 12:47, 48).

132. Therefore, it is permitted to say with the Supreme Master: *If I bear witness of myself, my testimony is nothing. There is another who bears witness of me, John, etc.* (John 5:31, 32, 34). ->### Therefore, he hands over his affairs to others.<-

*I do not accept human testimony (says the* Lord*), but I say these things so that you may be saved (*verse 35*).* <note>The author is quoting John 5:34-35.</note> That is, *so that you may be stirred to weigh the truth, to recognize it, and to give glory to* God. *Nor is there any other purpose for us in recounting so many testimonies here, than that pious readers might be better stirred to an attentive review of the following* Booklet <note>original: "Libelluli"</note>, *in order to recognize whether our affairs deserve the great praises of such great* Men <note>Referring to the Reformers who previously praised the Bohemian Brethren.</note>, *and henceforth either retain their own better practices or correct their more corrupt ones.*

133. *If we have sinned by hiding our* <term>mina<gloss>A "mina" was a unit of currency mentioned in Jesus's parables (Luke 19), representing the spiritual gifts or responsibilities God entrusts to people.</gloss></term> *until now, behold, we make amends by setting it forth publicly! And if what happened to that ungrateful and lazy servant happens to us—that we experience the severe judgment of the* Lord *saying,*

> Take the mina from him, and give it to the one who has ten (Luke 19:24).

*we must say humbly with* David:

> It is the Lord; let him do what seems good in his eyes (2 Samuel 15:26).

*To you, however, O Churches, who possess more minas, behold, we now hand over ours as well! Receive it from us, we reverently pray, so that more profit may come to the* Lord *from it than when it was with us alone. We read in* Moses, *that when* God, *commanding the* Tabernacle *to be built for Him, required voluntary offerings from the people; whoever had gold, blue, purple, scarlet, fine linen, goat hair, or ram skins, etc., offered them; and the* Workmen, *divinely inspired, fashioned the works from them* (Exodus 35:23, etc.). *Now, indeed, we hear wise* Men *here and there consulting about erecting the throne of* Christ *in the Churches—that is,* Discipline, *and the best form of* Ecclesiastical Government: *why then should not everyone whom his own spirit moves (as* Moses *speaks there in verse 29) offer whatever he has? (Since, indeed, no one can offer what he does not have). And might not the* Artificers *whom the* Lord *raises up, and will raise up, select, arrange, and polish these materials to build a* Tabernacle for the Lord?

134. *I truly believe that* Luther, Bucer, Calvin, Musculus, Zanchius, Beza, Olevianus, Vergerius, etc., *were also* <term>Bezalels and Oholiabs<gloss>The two master craftsmen appointed by God to build the Tabernacle in the desert (Exodus 31).</gloss></term> (*filled with the* Spirit of God), *and were able to judge what was more or less fitting for the divine work. Yet you, who have entered into their labors, do not let their judgments prejudice you in discerning...*

## WITHIN THE UNITY

### OF THE BOHEMIAN BRETHREN,

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the necessity of discipline within the church. The text begins mid-sentence, likely completing a thought about how difficult it is to maintain order without oversight. It shifts here into the specific protocol for "Visitations," a practice where church leaders formally inspected congregations to ensure spiritual and administrative health.</meta>

# The Order of Visitations.

...it is [difficult] to keep men in their duty without *encouragements and warnings.* And for this reason, within the Unity of the Brethren, there has been introduced not only the *oversight <note>original: "inspectio"</note> of the Pastors over the Hearers <note>The "Auditores" or lay members of the congregation</note>, as already mentioned, but also the oversight of the Bishops over the Pastors themselves and over entire churches:* following, of course, the example of the Apostles, who confirmed the churches they had founded by visiting them (Acts 14:21, 22; and 15:36, 41). We must therefore speak of the Visitations of the churches:

1. *When are they usually established?*
2. *By whom are they performed?*
3. *What are the specific tasks <note>original: "expeditiones"</note> of the Visitors?*

Visitations are established so that every church is inspected once a year. This usually happens in the spring, summer, or autumn; however, if necessity demands it (such as if a Pastor needs to be presented to a church, or some scandal must be addressed), it may even take place in winter. If there are not enough Visitors for all the churches in the same year, those where there is less urgent need are deferred to the following year.

This care properly falls to the *Bishops specifically*, so that each one, together with his colleagues the Assistant Elders <term>Conseniors: senior ministers who assisted the Bishop in administrative and spiritual governance</term>, visits the churches throughout his diocese. But if he is prevented by legal obstacles, he sends the Assistant Elders, following the Apostolic example (1 Thessalonians 3:1, 2). One or two Pastors from the neighborhood are also usually brought along, to ensure greater confidence among the people—who witness the harmony of their Pastors—regarding everything that takes place here. The Pastor who is to be visited is usually notified in a timely manner.

*Some duties of the Visitors are ordinary, while others are extraordinary.*

<meta>This page continues the protocol for ecclesiastical visitations. # The Order of Visitations.

Ordinary visitations are to be carried out either privately with specific individuals or publicly before the entire assembly.

For as soon as the Visitors arrive at the location (often before Saturday noon), they immediately:

1. Inquire of the Pastor himself in private, *“How do the affairs of your church stand? What matters are most urgent to be addressed?”* Then they inquire into the man himself: *“Are you a faithful servant and a workman who need not be ashamed?”* (2 Timothy 2:15); *“How much attention do you give to reading, exhortation, and teaching?”* (1 Timothy 4:13); *“In what ways do you lead by an example of piety?”* (ibid. v. 12). Then: *“How are you supporting your life? How are you educating the students <term>alumni: young men being trained for ministry within the Unity</term> of the Unity? How does each of them conduct himself? In what way do they obey? How are they progressing?”* Afterward, they inquire about the People: *“Is the flock increasing or decreasing?”* (and they order the Registry <note>original: "Catalogum"</note> to be presented to them). *“Are there any scandalous individuals who require some form of correction? How are the Elders (both men and women) and the Almoners <term>Eleemosinarii: those in charge of distributing charity to the poor</term> performing their duties?”*

2. The Pastor’s assistants and students (the Ministers, Deacons, and Acolytes <term>Acoluthi: the lowest rank of the clergy, assistants in training</term>) are summoned and asked: *“Do you remain firm in your purpose of holiness? How are you progressing in your pursuit of piety? How are you cultivating harmony and brotherly love?”* (Colossians 4:8). *“How are you treated by the Pastor? Do you suffer from any harmful neglect? How much time is granted to you for your studies? And how do you spend it? What has each person accomplished during that time?”* (For it is the custom of the Visitors to occasionally provide books <note>original: "Autores," referring to specific classical or theological authors</note> to be read by both the Pastor and his household <note>original: "contubernio," referring to the shared living quarters of the clergy and students</note>; they check, therefore, whether this has been done and what fruit has come from it...

<meta>catchword: in- [likely continuing a word such as 'investigatur' or 'inquiritur']</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the specific interview process during a church visitation, detailing the separate examinations of the Pastor, the congregation's administrative health, and the progress of junior clergy and students.</summary>
<keywords>visitation protocol, pastoral oversight, Unity of the Brethren, ecclesiastical discipline, ministerial education, deacons, acolytes</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific protocol for an ecclesiastical visitation. The previous page ended with the Visitors questioning the Pastor’s assistants and students about their spiritual progress and conduct. This page describes the questioning of the lay elders and the civil authorities, as well as the public liturgical duties of the Visitors.</meta>

...is inquired into, and anything else needing to be examined, recorded, or completed is assigned.)

3. Then the Elders <term>Presbyteri: lay leaders who assisted the Pastor in governing the local church</term> are called in. After greetings are exchanged in the name of the absent Seniors <note>original: "Seniorum," referring here to the Bishops of the Unity</note> and the other churches, they are questioned about the Pastor: *“How does he perform his duty? Does he build up the church through his words and his actions? Does any cause for scandal arise from him or from any member of his household? Finally, do you notice anything that should be addressed publicly for the benefit of the whole Church?”*

4. They also speak to the Elderwomen <term>Presbyteras: female elders responsible for the spiritual welfare and conduct of the women in the congregation</term> who have been summoned: *“How are you conducting yourselves in your duty of looking after the younger sisters? Does any hatred, envy, disagreement, slander, or excess in dress <note>original: "luxus vestiarius"; the Brethren emphasized modesty in attire</note> exist among them?”*

5. It is also customary to review (sometimes in the presence of the Churchwardens and Elders) the inventories of household goods, as well as the buildings, to see if anything is being allowed to fall into ruin.

6. Where the Magistrate <term>Magistratus: the local civil authority or government official</term> is in harmony with the Congregation, the Visitors also greet him and find out *whether he is satisfied with his Pastor.*

The public works of the Visitors within the Church consist of: (1) *Inflaming the warmth of love for God through the preaching of the Word of GOD;* (2) *Confirming everyone in the faith and love of Christ through the administration of the Holy Supper;* and (3) *Stirring them up to perform their duties with eagerness from now on through various exhortations suited to their status.*

The extraordinary labors of the Visitors, which only occasionally arise, include: the *reconciliation of disputes* which the Pastor was unable to resolve (such as if some conflict should break out between the Congregation and the Pastor, or the Magistrate...

## I. The Ordination of Civil Elders.

<note>original: "Presbyterorum Politicorum"; "civil" here refers to lay elders who managed the administrative and social affairs of the church, distinguishing them from the ordained clergy.</note>

The ordination of Elders, when necessary, is performed with this rite. All men are commanded to present themselves early before the evening sermon, and there, after an admonition given by the Visitors <term>Visitatores: traveling overseers who inspected the spiritual and administrative health of local churches</term>, they elect by free votes those whom they deem worthy of that office. Thus, those designated by the majority of votes are called forward by the Visitor after the evening sermon has ended, and the *duties of this office* are read out to them (while the entire congregation listens). They, in turn, with their voice and a pledged hand <note>original: "stipulatâ manu"; a formal gesture of agreement or a handshake confirming a vow</note> to the *Leaders of the Unity* <term>Antistitibus Unitatis: the senior bishops or chief administrators of the Unitas Fratrum</term>, to the Pastor, and to their own Church, bind themselves to faithfulness and diligence. And so that they may also perform the duty of Inspectors within the church building, they are also honored with a special seating area (placed for a better view of the people).

The process is similar when the Elderwomen <term>Presbyteræ: female elders responsible for the oversight of the women in the community</term> are elected: except that the matter is conducted only in the presence of the female sex.

Namely, that it may be a *Bethel, a House of GOD*, and the *Gate of heaven* <note>original: "Bethel, Domus DEI, & Porta cæli"; "Bethel" is a Hebrew term meaning House of God, derived from Genesis 28</note> (Genesis 28:17, 19; Isaiah 56:7; Luke 19:46); therefore, it is now to be dedicated and consecrated for that use.

And concerning the Tabernacle <term>Tabernaculum: the portable dwelling place for the divine presence used by the Israelites</term> erected by Moses: *I will sanctify the tabernacle of the covenant, and it shall be sanctified by my glory* (Exodus 29:43, 44). And to Solomon concerning the built Temple: *I have heard your prayer, and I have sanctified this House, that I may place my name here* (1 Kings 9:3). Therefore, he exhorts that all should now turn their eyes to *GOD* and pray most humbly that He may also place His name here (Deuteronomy 12:5, 11).

3. Accordingly, the entire Assembly falls upon their knees, and with a pious prayer (in imitation of Solomon, 1 Kings 8), they fervently call upon God.

4. An admonition is given, with a solemn declaration, that this holy place should not be employed for any other uses than for holding sacred Assemblies and for the holy worship of the most high *GOD*, to be performed in the presence of the chosen Angels.

5. And because *all things are sanctified through the word of GOD and Prayer* (1 Timothy 4:5), there is also presently a preaching of the divine Word and the sacred <term>Synaxis: a Greek term for a formal liturgical gathering or holy assembly, often referring to the Eucharist</term>.

6. Finally, the name of *GOD* is invoked once more; and with a prayer for the divine blessing—which will be present for all who shall serve *GOD* here with a righteous heart—the act is concluded.

### CHAPTER VII.
# ON THE ORDER AND DEGREES
## *of Ecclesiastical Discipline.*

It has already been stated how the safekeeping of *Order* within the <term>Unity: The *Unitas Fratrum*, or Unity of the Brethren; a pre-Reformation Christian movement</term> is maintained through various inspections. Now, however, reason itself dictates that *it is not enough for a Gardener to look over his plants, a Teacher his students, or a Father of a family his workers; rather, the first must prune the wild shoots, the second must correct errors, and the third must rebuke and punish the lazy.* <note>original: "stolones putandos, isti mendas tollendas, huic ignavos increpandos & castigandos"</note> Therefore, the Church also has need of *Discipline*, by which those wandering off the path may be called back, those refusing to obey may be put to shame, the stubborn may be punished, and finally, rotten and decaying members may be cut off and cast away. Thus Christ said: *Salt is good; therefore have salt in yourselves, and have peace with one another.* (Mark 9:50.) Likewise: *If your brother sins against you, go and rebuke him.* (Matthew 18:15.) Likewise: *If your hand or your foot causes you to stumble, cut them off and cast them away.* (verses 8, 9.) Finally, the <term>Apostle: referring here to the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews, traditionally identified as Paul</term> testifies that Discipline is the token of the sons of GOD. *If you endure discipline,* (he says) *GOD offers Himself to you as to sons. But if you are without discipline, of which all sons are partakers, then you are bastards and not sons.* (Hebrews 12:7, 8.)

And for these reasons, everyone who has flowed into this holy fellowship of faith and charity has submitted themselves to this healthy divine institution of *Discipline* since the very beginnings of our Unity; the highest and the lowest; <term>Elders: the senior spiritual leaders or bishops</term>, Co-Elders; Ministers, Assistants; Hear-

<meta>The catchword "Auditores" at the bottom of the page indicates the next page begins with "ers," completing the word "Hearers," referring to the lay members of the congregation.</meta>

->On the Order and Degrees of Ecclesiastical Discipline<-

listeners all, Nobles and commoners; and even the Magistrates themselves: believing this to be the *antidote to sin* <note>original: "antidotum peccati"</note>, if they know there are those who, watching over their life and conduct, have the power to warn and recall them, and so to draw them back from a dangerous straying (Psalm 119:71 and 141:5; 2 Cor. 7:8, 9, 10, 11). Thus far, therefore, all in the <term>Unity: The *Unitas Fratrum* or Unity of the Brethren</term> are subject to Discipline, from the infant to the old man, from the subject to the Lord, *from the <term>Acolyte: the lowest rank of the ministry, an assistant</term> even to the <term>Bishop: the highest overseer</term>*: and this for their own good, and the good of others. For since every person is human, they are liable to fall (Prov. 24:16; James 3:2); but lest they persist in that fall, and so grow weak, waste away, and perish, a remedy has been prescribed by Christ, the <term>Chief Physician: Latin *archiatro*, a term for a high-ranking or royal doctor</term> of souls, which is *Discipline*, as we have seen. When this is administered carefully, it benefits not only the one upon whom it is exercised, but also others. Hence the Apostle says: *Rebuke those who sin in the presence of all, so that the others also may stand in fear* (1 Tim. 5:20).

The degrees of Discipline (according to the prescription of Christ, Matt. 18:15) are three.

**1.** *Admonition, or Correction, in private.*
**2.** *Rebuke, and Public Shame.*
**3.** *Excommunication, and total removal from the Church.*

For first, all are taught here that this right has been granted by Christ—indeed, a law has been imposed—that *everyone should have care for their brother*: and, if they see him doing wrong, *to brotherly warn him of the error* (Eccl. 17:13; 1 Thess. 5:11; Heb. 3:13). So that a brother might dare [to warn] a brother,

### *II. On Public Discipline of a Gentler Kind.*

If no improvement follows, he is brought before the whole Presbytery and is admonished in various ways, so that he may acknowledge his sin and desist. If he relents, he is dismissed with admonition and consolation; if he continues to be stubborn, his conscience is bound by the <term>Power of the Keys: the authority to forgive or retain sins, based on Matthew 16:19</term> <note>original: "Clavium potestate"</note>, and he is suspended from the communion of the <term>Holy Supper: the Eucharist or Lord’s Supper</term> until he returns to his senses and corrects his behavior.

This is the procedure for lighter offenses; but in the case of more serious and notorious sins, the process is different. Namely, after the sinner has been summoned before the Pastor and Presbytery as often as necessary, the sin is laid out in its full gravity <note>original: "exaggeratur", meaning to emphasize the weight of the offense to the conscience</note>. If he acknowledges the gravity and, being confused within himself, truly grieves, he is raised up into the hope of pardon (Acts 3:17, 18, 19), yet under certain conditions. Namely:

*First, that for some time he should exercise himself in true <term>Penance: the outward practice of repentance</term> (by praying to God for his sins, mortifying the flesh, and showing an actual improvement of life). (This kind of exercise of penance is extended either until the next Communion or beyond, according to how the fruits of repentance show themselves; meanwhile, the Church does not neglect its prayers to GOD for those who have fallen).*

*Second, that afterward [he should make satisfaction] to the Church which has been affected by sadness (all those publicly scandalized, sometimes by himself, some-*

(when, by pleading through the <term>Pastor: the lead minister</term>,) *he may make satisfaction, and in that manner reconcile himself to the church*. If, however, the sin is not entirely known publicly, the apology is settled before the <term>Presbytery: the council of elders</term>. All this is done to the end that the ashamed sinner may have the opportunity for truer repentance and for acting more cautiously thereafter, while others may learn wisdom from the danger of another (1 Timothy 5:20).

### III. *On the Excommunication of the Incorrigible.*

If the crime is too heinous, or if the sinner practices defiance instead of repentance, one proceeds to the *final degree of the Keys*, <term>Excommunication: the formal exclusion of a person from the religious community</term>: which Christ and the Apostles wish to be used in the Church (Matthew 18:17, 1 Corinthians 5:5, etc.). Here, therefore, the sinner is totally excluded from the communion of the Saints and is <term>handed over to Satan: a biblical phrase (1 Cor 5:5) referring to the removal of a person from the protection of the church community and its sacraments</term> <note>original: "Satanæ traditur"</note>, unless he yet repents. Once the excommunication is publicly announced, the whole people, as if to seal what has been done in the name of Christ, cries out, *Amen*: not without the sighs and tears of the godly. Thus, obstinate sinners who have been excommunicated are regarded as tax collectors and pagans <note>referring to people outside the faith or those seen as socially and spiritually cast out</note>, just as Christ commanded. Yet the hope of grace is denied to no one, if through serious <term>repentance: the sincere turning away from sin toward God</term> <note>original: "pœnitentiam"</note> he wishes to return from the path of damnation to the path of salvation. If they see the grace of repentance given to someone, they rejoice; and after a sufficient trial of his repentance to see if it is sincere, they receive him again publicly with the joy of the whole Church (2 Corinthians 2:7), and thus they administer the *other part of the Keys* (by opening Heaven to the penitent).

## CONCLUSION.

And these are the *Rites of our Ecclesiastical Order*, which our ancestors, having drawn them from the word of GOD, embraced and have observed for two centuries now, through much persecution and patience, but with much fruit for themselves and the people of GOD; likewise we (perceiving nothing thus far that serves edification more closely) strive, with GOD’s help, to observe them. If any other Churches find pleasure in following this same, or a similar, *system of Order*, *we are ready not to envy, but rather to praise God*; provided that Apostolic rule <note>original: "Apostolicum illud"</note> prevails: *Let all things be done decently and in order* (1 Corinthians 14:40).

May the merciful GOD establish, strengthen, and perfect this same holy Order—which is pleasing to Him—among us and everywhere throughout His Christendom, for the healthy edification of His Church, and to the
->praise of His name! AMEN.<-

# Certain Notes Regarding the System of Order and Discipline of the Bohemian Brethren.

## Concerning the Introduction.

-> (a) <-

Indeed, there are so many praises of ORDER <note>The author uses "Order" (Latin: *Ordo*) to mean both social structure and the specific rules of their church</note> from wise men, that it is a wonder so few mortals pay attention to what this thing is and how beneficial it is—much less that they understand or observe it. Truly, the Scripture is full of divine complaints that *all men wander from the laws of eternal Order, and fall so far into infinite confusion that the perverse cannot be corrected, and the number of defects is beyond counting* (Ecclesiastes 1:14, 15).

Oh, how truly he spoke, who said, *Order is the soul of things.* For just as a living body lives, moves, feels, and performs its actions through the soul—and when the soul is removed, it collapses and becomes a corpse—so whatever flourishes anywhere among well-constituted things does so by the benefit of Order. Do you wish for an example? I shall give one. Tell me, what use or delight can these letters:

-> A a, d, e, i, m m, n, oo, r r r, u, <-

provide? Or even if they are arranged into some, though incomplete, order? For instance, into syllables:

-> Am, or, dor, er, um, an, i? <-

Or finally, even if formed into words, but placed confusedly without certain distinction: such as

-> Ord orer uman ima. <-

What do you understand here? But you will understand soon, if I place them distinctly:

-> Order is the soul of things. <note>original Latin: "Ordo rerum anima." The author’s letter jumble above spells this specific Latin phrase.</note> <-

Is it not so? Yet I have said nothing different just now than before, only differently: the same letters, syllables, and words, but previously they were brought forth without order, and now with order. And behold, in the first case it was a useless thing, a corpse of speech, giving no life to the Intellect (that is, no meaning); now it is a small, animated body, vigorous and full of sense and motion. The same principle holds in every Speech, Thought, and Action: *Order alone gives life, existence, and endurance to all things.*

### Notes

...rare, when correctly applied; but to slip away and perish when dissolved. Try this in any most-wise Book: dissolve its sentences, words, syllables, and letters so that they remain present but do not cohere—soon you will have destroyed the Book and turned light into darkness. The same will happen if you dissolve the bonds of the human Body, or of any other animal, or of any human Society, or of a Building, a Clock, or any thing whatsoever. Most truly, therefore, the Poet says:

->Do you wish to destroy all things in the World?<-
->Destroy Order alone.<-

And Plato: *From Order all good things proceed; even ordered things dissipate when unordered.* Finally, Boëthius <note>A 6th-century philosopher whose work was a staple of medieval and Renaissance education.</note> says:

->Whatever by a headlong Path<-
->Deserts its certain Order<-
->Does not have happy endings.<-
<note>original: "Quicquid praecipiti Viâ / Certum deserit Ordinem / Laetos non habet exitus." from Boethius, *The Consolation of Philosophy*, Book I, Meter 6.</note>

And these things are said concerning Order in general.

->(b)<-

Something must be added regarding **Ecclesiastical Order** <note>The specific structure, hierarchy, and discipline of the Church.</note> in particular: how much it has been commended by God and holy Men, and how greatly its utility has been recognized and celebrated.

1. Origen <note>An early Christian scholar (c. 185–253 AD).</note> (in his 2nd Homily on the 2nd Chapter of Numbers) writes thus: *Moses says: Let every man march in the camp according to his Order, and according to his signs, and according to the houses of his families. And Paul says: Let all things be done decently and according to Order. Does it not seem to you that it is the one Spirit of God who speaks in Moses and Paul? Moses commands them to march according to Order; the Apostle commands all things to be conducted in the Church according to Order, etc. Therefore, to not observe the decorum worthy of Christians is to despise the statutes of the Law and the Gospel, and to conduct nothing according to Order.*

2. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), known as "Golden-Mouthed" for his preaching.</note> (in his 37th Homily on 1 Corinthians): *Nothing builds up as much as right Order, peace, and charity: just as their opposites tear down. You may see this not only in spiritual matters, but similarly in every thing. For whether in a Choir, or in a Ship, or in a Chariot, or in a Camp, if you confound the order—and having moved the greater things from their place, you put minor things into their order—you corrupt everything; and thus those things which are above are made below, etc.*

3. And what need is there to allege more? There is no one among the ancients who does not celebrate the praises of Order: and the matter speaks for itself, that the more Order any particular Church has within itself...

### On the Entrance

...is all the more unbreakable, even if other things are quite unstable, as can be seen in the Greek and Roman Church. It is a wonder that some in our age, because of the abuse of that Order, fall into a hatred of Order itself, wishing for the very bonds of Order to be removed and attempting this in their own churches. This is surely no better reasoning than if someone wanted to remove the supports and columns of a large house for the reason that they should not be granted more importance than the other beams or parts of the walls. Or if one wanted the human body not to have so many members of different forms and duties, so that they would have no occasion to exalt themselves over one another. Is this truly wisdom according to God? Would we rather the body of the Church be like the body of some worm <note>The author uses the worm as an example of a "simple" organism where every part looks the same, contrasted with the complex "limbed" human body.</note>, consisting of merely similar parts, rather than like the human body, decorated on all sides with distinct members? Who would be so foolish?

4. For because God not only arranged everything in the work of Creation according to numbers, measures, and weights; but also when giving mandates for the construction of *Noah’s Ark of the flood*, the *Ark of the Covenant* (along with the Altar, the Lampstand, and the other sacred furnishings of the Tabernacle) to be built by Moses, and the glorious Temple to be erected for Solomon, and to be sketched by Ezekiel, He fulfilled all things with numbers, measures, and weights. Finally, GOD deems it an honor to be called the GOD OF ORDER <note>original: "DEUS ORDINIS"</note> (1 Corinthians 14:33). What, I ask, shall we conclude from all this, except that all things which are of God (and the Church above all) must be full of Order?

5. Let us look at the ancient Church itself, the people of Israel! *Why did God want this people divided into twelve tribes and to depart from Egypt in military order? (Exodus 13:18) Why did He precisely define in what order the camps were to be placed around the Tabernacle, and again moved or set down? Why did He command the people to be divided into tens, hundreds, and thousands, and for their own Decurions, Centurions, and Chiliarchs <note>Chiliarchs: Greek-derived term for a commander of one thousand soldiers.</note>, and Leaders to be set over each? Why, separating one tribe for ecclesiastical ministries, did He distinguish them into the ranks of the Levites and the High Priest? Dividing these same Levites again through a threefold order, and the priests (through David) into certain classes? Why also did He prescribe all the rituals for administering divine worship, down to the smallest details? Why, I say, unless because God, the God of Order, wished for the hidden mysteries of Order to be everywhere commended to our attention?*

6. Concerning the New Church, did not the Son of God himself

# Annotations

...dwelling on Earth with us and founding the Church, did He not at the same time found an **Order**? For from the whole population of the faithful, He first chose seventy Disciples, and from these, in turn, twelve Apostles. Among these twelve, He again took only three aside to witness more secret matters <note>The author refers to Peter, James, and John, who witnessed the Transfiguration and other private moments.</note>; these three were afterwards called the "pillars" (pre-eminently <note>original Greek: κατ' ἐξοχήν</note>) of the whole building of the Church (Galatians 2:9). Did He not confirm only these men—called by Him and thus prepared—to the ministry (Luke 22:30, John 20:21), while others, who were not called but were thrusting themselves forward, were not admitted (Matthew 8:19, 20)? Does not Paul say that the whole Church of Christ—joined together from Apostles, Prophets, Evangelists, Pastors, and Teachers, and the community of the Faithful—is **one Body**, compacted and connected through every joint of administration, by the power acting within according to the measure of each individual member? (Ephesians 4:11, 16). For this reason, the same Apostle so frequently commends **Order** to the churches and is so zealous against the "disorders" <note>original Greek: "ataxias," referring to a lack of discipline or arrangement</note> that creep in. As when he writes to the Colossians: *“For though I am absent in body, I am present in spirit, rejoicing to see your Order and the firmness of your faith in Christ”* (2:5). And to the Thessalonians: *“We command you in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that you withdraw yourselves from every brother who walks in disorder...”* (2 Thessalonians 3:6).

7. From all these points, it is clear: whoever praises, commends, or urges **Order** in any matter (but especially in the greatest matter, the Kingdom of God, which is the Church), does nothing other than what God and all holy and wise men do. And conversely: whoever attempts to disturb or tear apart **Order** in any matter (especially in the greatest, the Kingdom of God), does something contrary to God and all the saints. In doing so, they admit either impiety or folly, and must give a heavy account to God. As the Poet says: *The deserter of Order even deserts God.*

->(c)<-

The first founder of our Church <note>The author is referring to the "Unitas Fratrum" or Bohemian Brethren.</note> was not any one person, but many pious people gathered into one. For **Hus** <note>Jan Hus (c. 1369–1415), the Czech reformer burned at the stake.</note> had already been taken from the living. As for **Rokycana** <note>Jan Rokycana (c. 1396–1471), the Hussite Archbishop of Prague who sympathized with the Brethren but refused to officially join their separation from the established church.</note>, they were unable to persuade him to lend his authority to the separation from the existing confusions, even though he had been the one to advise it. Therefore, they did what the Apostle urges: they *strove to preserve the UNITY of the Spirit through the bond of peace, so that they might be one Body and one Spirit...* (Ephesians 4:3, 4).

<summary>This page argues that Church hierarchy is divinely ordained, citing Christ’s selection of the Apostles and St. Paul’s metaphors of the "Body." It also explains that the author's church (the Bohemian Brethren) was founded on communal unity rather than a single leader.</summary>
<keywords>Church Order, Apostles, Saint Paul, Jan Hus, Jan Rokycana, Body of Christ, Unitas Fratrum, Ecclesiastical Discipline</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the necessity of Order within the Church, this page explores the specific identity of the Bohemian Brethren and the universal characteristics that define a true Christian community.</meta>

on the Introduction.
(B)
<meta>catchword: Mi-</meta>

nation, derived from no other source than this sacred intention: that they might give the name **Fraternal Unity** <note>original: UNITATIS FRATERNÆ; This refers to the Unitas Fratrum, or the Bohemian Brethren, the predecessors of the modern Moravian Church.</note> to this gathering of theirs for **Unanimity of Spirit**. This name still holds among those who are scattered; would that the reality itself remained in full!

->(d)<-

They dispute laboriously concerning the "marks" <note>In theology, the "marks" (notae) are the defining characteristics that identify the true Church.</note> of the true Church. But what need is there for such effort? Are those Apostolic words in Acts 2:42 and Ephesians 4:1–6 not sufficient? Can these not be conveniently reduced to these three? 
I. **Purity of Doctrine**, according to the Gospel of Christ. 
II. **The pursuit of a holy life**, in imitation of Christ. 
III. **The keeping of Order**, to preserve the unity of the spirit. 
These are the simplest points, admitting less room for false appearances.

->(e)<-

If any of us, regarding the remnants of the Bohemian Church, should wish to employ that Apostolic word in this place: *“We have cause to boast through Christ Jesus in those things which pertain to God”* (Romans 15:17), he might perhaps not be doing so in vain, since boasting in the Lord is a part of divine worship—celebrating one’s blessings not as one's own, but as God’s gifts (whether they are still possessed or already lost). However, I, who add these Notes, propose to follow the same Apostle religiously when he says: *“If I should wish to boast, I will not be a fool, for I will speak the truth; but I will speak sparingly, lest any man should think of me above that which he sees me to be, or hears from me”* (2 Corinthians 12:6). That is, I propose to speak nothing but the truth, recounting only those things about us which were truly present in our constitutions and practice. And indeed, I speak of those very things sparingly: not to capture some small glory for ourselves, but rather out of a sense of shame and grief for a Church so beautifully formed (as great men have judged) and now lost. Let God receive the honor due to Him from every side even in our confusion; and let the Church of God find in our ruins an example of how to avoid such ruin.

***

### Annotations to Chapter I.

->(A)<-

This was the first grace of God toward them: to have been able to notice the confusion <note>This likely refers to the religious chaos in Bohemia prior to the formation of the Brethren.</note> and to long for the light of distinction.

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Notes" section (Annotata) following the author's discussion on the identity and marks of the true Church. It transitions from the general character of the Bohemian Brethren to a critique of how theology is structured.</meta>

# Notes

->(B)<-

It is surprising how modern theologians, when arranging systems of theology, do not preserve that Apostolic threefold division <term>trichotomia; a division into three parts</term> of FAITH, CHARITY, and HOPE. Instead, they mostly establish only two parts of theology: FAITH and LIFE. It is as if there were nothing sublime in what God promised through faith in Christ to those living piously, or as if it were enough for those promises to be scattered here or there in passing. Yet that first and most solemn Covenant <note>original: *Foedus*</note> made with Abraham—the father of the faithful—proposes those three points very distinctly, and they can be noted equally throughout the entire Scripture. For there, God addresses Abraham:

I. *I am God Almighty* (Genesis 17:1. Here, faith is founded upon the one and only God.)
II. *Walk before me, and be upright* (Here are love and obedience.)
III. *And I will be your shield, and your reward shall be very great* (Genesis 15:1. Here is the hope of eternal protection and eternal retribution.)

Similarly, Christ, the founder of the New Covenant, says in John 14:
I. *Do you believe in God? Believe also in me* (v. 1).
II. *If you love me, keep my commandments* (v. 15).
III. *I will take you to myself, so that where I am, you may be also* (v. 3).

Likewise, when leaving the world in Matthew 28:19–20, and frequently in the writings of the Apostles, such as 1 Corinthians 13:13, Ephesians 1:15–18, Colossians 1:4–5, 1 Peter 1:3, 7–8, Acts 24:14–16, and elsewhere throughout. Therefore, Augustine rightly retained this partition in his *Hand-book to Laurentius* <note>original: *Enchiridion ad Laurentium*; a famous summary of Christian doctrine by St. Augustine structured around Faith, Hope, and Love.</note>, as did our ancestors. I say "rightly," because Christianity, having begun from theory, must be led through practice into use <note>original: *chresin*; a term derived from Greek meaning the actual use, enjoyment, or fruition of a thing.</note>. That is, we must be moved from the brightness of Faith, through the fervor of Charity, into the sweetness of Hope regarding the everlasting mercy of God.

---

->(C)<-

No Christian doubts that Apostolic traditions are the purest sources, and that the customs of the primitive Church are the streams flowing nearest to them. Therefore, no one ought to doubt that those things which afterwards...

### To Chapter I.
**F**
<meta>catchword: 4. Pri-</meta>

[that] crept in [after] the Apostles, must be measured against these rules: and those things closer to the sources are better.

---

->(D)<-

I wish it were so everywhere! So that nothing anywhere might be changed rashly or by anyone’s private judgment, but only by the common consent of the Church. Because this was not observed in the recent Reformations (attempted in various ways by various people), should it seem a wonder that the Churches have become so dissimilar to each other, and have fallen into so many disputes, even regarding <term>Adiaphora; matters of indifference that are neither commanded nor forbidden by God</term>? May God amend it!

---

->(E)<-

It is true that the Church is called a FLOCK, and the Ministers of the Church are called SHEPHERDS: yet not in a loose sense, as if it made no difference whether one preceded or followed the flock, and as if all things were indiscriminate. Rather, it is so that the pious may know and rejoice that they are the care of their God, that provision is made for them regarding the pastures of life, and that leaders and guardians of salvation are divinely given.

2. Otherwise, God wished it to be clear that more account is to be taken of order in the Church than in a flock, because He wished this same Church of His to be called His People, His House, His City, His *Kingdom*, and the *ordered ranks of His camps*. In all these, however, there is an organized society <note>original: *politica societas*</note>, distinguished by ranks of persons. Hence Gregory Nazianzen <note>A prominent 4th-century theologian and Archbishop of Constantinople</note> says: *Order has established in the Churches that some should be sheep, others Shepherds; some should lead, others should be subject; one should be as it were the head, another the foot, another the hand, another the eye, another some other member of the Body, for the harmony or utility of the whole Church, whether higher or lower. Let us revere this Order, O brothers! Let us uphold and preserve it! Let one be the ear, another the tongue, another the hand, another something else, etc.*

3. Furthermore, two things must be observed here:

I. *All Order is from God, even though the determination of Order does not always come immediately from God, but from human prudence.*

II. *Even those things which are of human institution bind the <unclear>conscience</unclear>: if they are not repugnant to divine institution, but are subordinate to it.*

<summary>The author argues for a return to historical church order and laments the divisions caused by private judgment during the Reformation, emphasizing that while the Church is a "flock," it is also a divinely ordered society with distinct ranks and responsibilities.</summary>
<keywords>Church Order, Reformation, Adiaphora, Gregory Nazianzen, ecclesiastical hierarchy, private judgment, political society, shepherds and flock</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Notes" (Annotata) section relating to Chapter 1, specifically expanding on the "first point" regarding the divine approval of human-established order within the church and state. It continues the argument that while some structures are human in origin, they carry divine weight if established by "right reason."</meta>

# Notes

4. The truth of the first point is revealed by examples of divine approval, in both political and ecclesiastical matters. For instance, God ratified the advice given to Moses by Jethro <note>Exodus 18: Jethro, Moses’ father-in-law, advised him to appoint judges to help handle the burden of leadership.</note> regarding the appointment of subordinate judges. Likewise, God frequently confirmed the choice of the Israelite people when they elected a Judge for themselves (on account of some heroic deed) by granting His assent and blessing. Furthermore, when David distributed the Levites and Priests into specific classes <note>original: *classes*; here meaning groups or divisions</note> for the sake of better order, it was accepted as if it had proceeded from God Himself, and was constantly observed as long as both Temples stood. Countless other examples could be brought forward from the Scriptures.

5. Moreover, that these things bind the conscience is abundantly shown by the single statement of the Apostle Peter, who says: *Be subject to every* HUMAN INSTITUTION <note>original: *humanae ordinationi*</note>, *FOR THE LORD’S SAKE, whether to the King, as supreme, or to governors, etc.* (1 Peter 2:13). Paul agrees, teaching that the faithful ought to be subject to political magistrates (even unbelieving ones), *not only because of wrath, but also for the sake of conscience* (Romans 13:5). The authority of these two Apostles places barriers against human rashness, so that no one who reveres God and conscience should feel free to disturb anything once it has been established by right reason.

---

->F.<-

Jan Łasicki <note>A 16th-century Polish historian and theologian who wrote about the customs of the Bohemian Brethren.</note> adds a fourth class of Hearers: the *Penitents*. These are individuals who have fallen into some scandal and are suspended from the communion of the Church for a time, until they reconcile themselves to God, their own conscience, and the Church (which they have saddened) through true repentance. This class is omitted here because only the ordinary ranks are being reviewed; however, it is touched upon below in Chapter VII, which deals with <term>Discipline; the system of corrective measures used by the church to maintain moral standards</term>.

---

->(G.)<-

No one is perfect in this life, yet all must strive to be so, according to that saying of Christ: *Be perfect, just as your Father in heaven is perfect* (Matthew 5:48), and other similar exhortations occurring frequently in the Scriptures. Hence, the Apostles called those among their listeners who had progressed further than the rest *perfect*.

# On Chapter I.

F 2

<meta>catchword: life</meta>

(1 Corinthians 2:6, 14:20; Philippians 3:12; Hebrews 5:14, etc.) whose zeal and style it is not a matter of rashness to imitate.

---

->(H.)<-

**Presbyter**, a Greek name, denotes *Elder* <note>original: *Seniorem*</note> in Latin; it is assigned by the Apostles both to the Pastors of the Church and to those who assist them in watching over the flock, *who do not labor in doctrine and speech* (1 Timothy 5:17). Such are our own: called in our vernacular <term>Gaudcowé zborů</term> <note>The Czech term for "Judges of the Assembly."</note>, that is, *Judges of the assembly, or Censors of the common people*, and likewise called *Civil Elders*. (Note Well) I am indeed not unaware that *Hugo Grotius* <note>A famous Dutch jurist and theologian (1583–1645) whose works on law and religion were highly influential.</note> laboriously proves that for the Apostles there were no "Elders" other than Pastors, and that he gives another sense to that passage in Timothy. However, because he finally admits: *Even if such Church Elders, as assessors to the Pastors in ecclesiastical judgments, are an institution of human wisdom, they are nonetheless very useful and should be entirely retained,* I hope that no one will easily find a reason to speak against it. The conditions he adds, so that no abuse can creep in, are sound; these are found near the end of Chapter 11 of the book he titled *On the Power of Sovereigns in Religious Matters* <note>original: *De Imperio summarum Potestatum circa sacra*</note>.

---

->(I.)<-

Concerning these duties of theirs, we have a small book written by which the authority of this ecclesiastical dignity is established and the parts of worthily administering the office are explained. Jan Łasicki <note>original: *Lasitius*; a Polish historian who wrote extensively about the customs and history of the Bohemian Brethren.</note> has a summary of these in Chapters 22 and 23, where they may be consulted.

---

->(K.)<-

Other Reformed Churches also have **Civil Elders**—men who are guardians of Church Discipline together with the Pastors—as is well known. However, *that matrons are employed for this purpose has not been heard of, except among the Brethren.* The cited passages show that this is done in imitation of the Apostolic Churches; moreover, experience has shown it to be of good use. I add the testimony of Grotius: *It is certain that in the ancient Church there were Matrons, who [led] other women to a [godly] life...* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Notes" (Annotata) section for Chapter 1. The previous page ended while discussing the duties of Church Elders. ### Notes.

...they might be encouraged to lead a life established in honesty; these they also called *Elder Women* <note>original Greek: πρεσβύτιδας (presbytidas)</note>: and because they sat above the other women in the churches, they called them *Presiding Women* <note>original Greek: προκαθημένας (prokathemenas)</note>. Canon 11 of the Council of Laodicea <note>A regional Christian council held around 363–364 AD in Asia Minor.</note> abolished them, although they had remained until that time, as Balsamon <note>Theodore Balsamon, a 12th-century jurist and scholar of church law.</note> notes. But why did it abolish them? On account of what abuses? Among our own people, we have been unable to note any. It seems that whatever serves for edification ought to be restored.

---

->(L.)<-

It has indeed become customary, but with a result not entirely as desired. For it has become clear, not without reason, that our ancestors feared that Apostolic saying, *Knowledge puffs up* <note>1 Corinthians 8:1</note>. Just as Erasmus himself, expressing his joy—though not without fear—regarding the rebirth of letters <note>A reference to the "Renaissance" or the revival of classical Latin and Greek literature.</note> in a certain letter, says: *Everything promises* (he says) *that the matter will succeed most happily. Yet one doubt holds my mind: lest, under the pretext of ancient literature, Paganism should attempt to raise its head.* Oh, what an ominous and all-too-prophetic voice! For under that light of heathen wisdom, holy men recognize and lament that true, pure, and simple Christianity (which strives to know nothing except Christ crucified) has almost vanished. Just as we have now experienced the truth of what Erasmus added when giving the cause of his fear: *For such is the nature of human affairs, that there has never been such a great success of any good, without some evil attempting to creep in at the same time under its pretext.* It has indeed crept in among us as well: while in many of those who affect an interest in literature, that new desire for knowing more has extinguished the former zeal for building up the Church; and we have found it necessary to chant along with Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, Roman Stoic philosopher.</note>: *After the learned appeared, the good ceased to be.* Alas, how many (among us also) has the Tree of Knowledge led away from the Tree of Life? Therefore, Erasmus's prayer was not irrational: *I would wish that these cold subtleties (of human eloquence and logical intricacies) were cut away entirely, and that simple and pure Christ be restored and planted deeply in human minds.*

---

->(M.)<-

Concerning the various grades of the Ministry among the Brethren <note>The Unitas Fratrum or Moravian Brethren.</note>, Lasitius <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602), a Polish historian who wrote extensively about the customs of the Brethren.</note> teaches in Chapter III that, not without the example of Christ, the Apostles, and the primitive...

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Notes" (Annotata) section for Chapter 1. The previous page ended with a warning about how secular learning could overshadow simple piety. Here, the author transitions into the necessity of a structured, step-by-step hierarchy within the church ministry, using analogies from nature, the military, and the arts to justify the different ranks within the Unitas Fratrum.</meta>

# To Chapter I.

...to the church. To these can be added what I set down above, in the **Notes on the Entrance (b) §§. 5. & 6.** Also that beautiful passage by **Gregory of Nazianzus** <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers" known for his oratory.</note> from his **Funeral Oration for Basil the Great**: praising him because he always ascended legitimately through the ecclesiastical ranks, he added these words:
> *I praise the law of the sea, which first hands the oars to the future Pilot, then places him at the bow, and finally, after a long time of rowing and observing the winds, appoints him to the helm. The same reasoning applies to military matters: first a Soldier, then a Centurion, and finally a Commander.*

2. Furthermore, the fact that these same ranks are not granted by a sudden leap, but in order, is according to the laws of nature, which we also gladly observe in the crafts, so that we may avoid pitfalls and confusion. Regarding this, **Chrysostom** <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople.</note> says thus (**Homily 5 on Colossians**): "On a staircase, the first step leads to the second, the second to the third; nor is it permitted to go from the first to the fourth, just as it is impossible to reach the second before the first: one is the path to the other," etc. (Truly, one does not become an old man who was not first a man; nor a man before he was a youth; nor a boy who was not first an infant. For God willed all things to be graded: to go step-by-step everywhere is to go wisely.)

3. Therefore, those who wish for the diversity of ranks to be removed from the Ministry do not seem to understand what they are doing: because the parity <note>The idea that all ministers should be of equal rank or status.</note> of Ministers contributes neither to the church's honor, nor to its stability. Not to honor: because variety delights everywhere, while sameness is everywhere the mother of satiety, and finally, of disgust. Not to stability: because every thing is only then firm when the smaller parts refer themselves to the larger, and the larger to the whole, and all things cohere to one another through their own bonds everywhere. For this reason, God placed variety among the stars of Heaven: so that there is one beauty of the sun, another of the Moon, another of the stars; indeed, star excels star in beauty (**1 Corinthians 15:41**). Why then would we want the spiritual heaven, the Church, to depart from this pattern? In a Building, are all the features beams? or columns? or rooftops? In our Body, are all members one member? Take away the difference of the Head from the body, the Eyes from the ears, the Tongue from the heart, the Hands from the feet—what will it be? Will it not be a monster? How beautiful it is not to try to be wiser than God!

---

->(N.)<-

*John Lasitius* <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602), a Polish historian who wrote a famous history of the Bohemian Brethren.</note> *has more on the Acolytes <term>Acolytes: junior assistants or trainees in the church hierarchy</term> of the Brethren in Chapter 4, from verse 10 to the end.*

# Notes

->(O.)<-

**Jan Łasicki** <note>A 16th-century Polish historian who wrote an extensive history of the Unitas Fratrum (the Bohemian Brethren).</note> describes **Deacons** at greater length in Chapter 5, Section 1, etc.

---

->(P.)<-

The word **Consistory** <term>Consistorium: Originally a Latin term for the place where the Roman Emperor's council stood or sat; here it refers to a governing body of the church.</term> originally signified the place where Judges stood or sat together. It began to be used long ago for ecclesiastical judicial assemblies, and it is still used today; just like the Greek word **Synedrion** <note>The council of elders, often associated with the Jewish Sanhedrin.</note>. A single, certain, and fixed place for this was lacking for the Brethren, yet the reality itself was not lacking; namely, the **Elders of the people**, elected and authorized for counsel; wherever they gathered for deliberation, there was the Ecclesiastical Senate, the Consistory, and the Sanhedrin.

---

->(Q.)<-

The name **BISHOPS** is very well known from the Apostolic writings: its meaning, however, from the Greek language itself, denotes an **OVERSEER** or **INSPECTOR**. This is correct, provided the truth is not lacking: namely, the diligent effort to *oversee, inspect, and attend* to the Faith, Life, and Manners of those entrusted to them. For this reason, **Jerome** <note>A 4th-century scholar and Church Father who translated the Bible into Latin.</note> translates "Bishop" as **Superintendent** <note>original: "Supraattendentem"</note> (in his letter to Evagrius, regarding 1 Peter 5).

2. Therefore, the superstition of certain people who loathe the **Episcopal** name because of its abuse within the **Papacy** <note>The Roman Catholic Church hierarchy.</note> is useless. For the names of the **Church, Gospel, Ministry, Keys, Sacrament,** and what not? were also profaned there by abuse. If we reject everything from antiquity for that reason, not even retaining the names, what will finally remain? *Sacred nomenclature, established by divine appointment, is the sacred furniture of the House of God:* it is not to be thrown away if it has been polluted, but rather to be cleansed, as seen in **Isaiah** 52:11 and 66:20. Did not **Belshazzar** most shamefully profane the sacred vessels of the Temple of God? (**Daniel** 5:3, 4, 23.) Yet were they for that reason considered unworthy to be received back into the Temple of God and restored to their former uses? (**Ezra** 1:7, 8.) Christ, the eternal wisdom of God, handed down to us the rule for eternal use: *Things that are good in their origin, and bad only by abuse, are not to be cast away, but are to be recalled to their principles* (**Matthew** 19:8). I confess, indeed, that where one must live among the Papists (for whom **Episcopal dignity** signifies wealth, honors, and worldly pomp), the entirety of that name...

<meta>catchword: Greek-</meta>

<summary>This page defends the use of traditional ecclesiastical titles like "Consistory" and "Bishop," arguing that their historical abuse by the Roman Catholic Church does not invalidate their biblical origins or their proper use in a reformed church.</summary>

<keywords>Consistory, Synedrion, Bishop, Overseer, Episcopacy, Jan Łasicki, Church Reform, Unitas Fratrum, Belshazzar's Feast</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Notes" section regarding the offices of the church. The previous page ended with a reference to Saint Jerome's definition of a Bishop as an "Overseer." Here, the author defends the necessity of a hierarchical structure of oversight, arguing that even those who watch over others need someone to watch over them.</meta>

### On Chapter I.

...the Greek term should be avoided, and the Latin or vernacular used in its place; provided that the reality itself—namely, **watchfulness over the Lord’s flock** <note>original: *vigilantia super gregem Domini*</note>—is meanwhile urged all the more diligently. But when we are outside the walls of danger, why should we strip the Church of its ornaments and its foundations?

3. If someone should say: *To the Apostles, a Bishop is the same as a Pastor of the Church,* I answer: So be it. Let every Pastor be the **Bishop** of his own church, that is, the **Inspector** and **Superintendent** of his own flock: but will he not therefore also need his own Inspector? The Apostolic Church saw that he certainly did; and therefore, it established that the Inspectors of many churches should in turn have their own Inspector (called a **Bishop** *by way of eminence* <note>original: κατ' ἐξοχὴν (*kat' exochēn*)</note>). Why is this pious wisdom of antiquity despised? Is a flock of Pastors not also a flock? And what is a flock without a Shepherd? Our eye, which sees everything, does not see itself: but it is seen by another eye. Therefore, the Inspector of the Church, while inspecting others, cannot see himself: let him not, therefore, despise another Inspector who provides for him the same service that he provides for others. If it is beneficial for such a person to exist, it is also beneficial for him to be lawfully appointed: so that everyone may know who ought to watch over whom, and from whom an account must be demanded if things are not managed correctly.

4. In a word, whether it be one person or several who attend to the Ministers of the churches; and whether they are called by the Greek name **Bishops**, or the Latin **Inspectors** or **Superintendents**, or by whatever name is native to a particular nation, it does not matter much: provided the Church (even one gathered from many individual churches) is not a **house without a doorkeeper, a flock without a shepherd, a choir without a leader, a ship without a pilot, etc.** <note>original: *Domus sine Janitore, Grex sine Pastore, Chorus sine Chorago, Navis sine Gubernatore*</note>. Whether this office should be fixed or itinerant: of which more shortly.

---

->(R.)<-

Ordinarily there were two in **Bohemia**, two in **Moravia**, one in **Poland**, though sometimes there were also two there.

---

->(S.)<-

The question is asked: should the Superintendency, or the **presidency of governance**, be **fixed** <term>statam: A permanent or standing office where a person remains in the role once elected.</term> or **itinerant** <term>ambulatoriam: A rotating or temporary office that moves between different people.</term>? Response: Some churches in our age prefer the latter, and put it into practice, for these reasons—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on church governance, specifically debating whether leadership roles (like the office of Bishop or Elder) should be permanent or rotating. It refutes the idea of "temporary" or "rotating" officials by citing biblical precedents from both the Old and New Testaments.</meta>

### Notes.

...moved by these [reasons]. I. That there may be an opportunity for exercising [the gifts of] everyone. II. That ambition and pride, along with every abuse of dignity, may be guarded against, so that no **Diotrephes** <note>A biblical figure mentioned in 3 John 1:9, often cited as a warning against leaders who love to have preeminence and refuse to acknowledge authority.</note> might be able to disturb the Church. However, other Churches (including those of the **Brethren** <note>referring to the *Unitas Fratrum* or Moravian Brethren</note>) prefer the former [permanent] way. This is because God instituted all things by His law—Judges, Kings, Levites, Priests, and the High Priest himself—so that once they were appointed, they remained in office forever.

2. *It cannot be shown by any precept or example that Christ or the Apostles changed this.* 3. Rather, men were led by their own prudence. For example: the Jews, by rotating the **High Priesthood** <note>original: *Pontificatum summum*</note> among themselves annually during the time of Christ (Matthew 26:3, John 11:51, Acts 4:6). They seem to have arrived at this plan because of the abuses of that high office while it remained perpetually in the hands of individuals; for many were seizing it shamelessly, one pushing out another, as is clear from the books of the **Maccabees** <note>Historical books covering the Jewish revolt and the Hasmonean dynasty, a period where the high priesthood became a political and often corrupt prize.</note>. But did they change God’s institution with a good result? It becomes evident from the history of the Gospel that the Jewish Church was hardly ever in a worse state than under those rotating officials, regarding Faith and Morals (Acts 5:17). Therefore, at last, it was completely rejected by God (1 Thessalonians 2:15, 16).

4. Nor do we have any example of Christ instituting anything temporary; all was established. For example: He chose 70 disciples from the people and gave them the power of teaching, baptizing, and performing miracles: *they remained*. From these, He again selected 12 **Apostles**: *they remained*. From the Apostles, again, He employed three for specific works: was it by rotation? No: He used the same ones perpetually, **Peter, James, and John**. And as long as they lived, they were called "pillars" (Galatians 2:9).

5. Nor does any trace exist that the Apostles instituted Pastors or Elders of Churches to be temporary, or permitted duties to be swapped; on the contrary, testimonies exist. Such is that of Christ to the **Bishop of Smyrna** <note>Polycarp, traditionally identified as the "angel" or bishop of the church in Smyrna addressed in Revelation.</note>: *Be faithful unto death, and I will give you a crown of life* (Revelation 2:10).

6. Nor in the early Church, after the times of the Apostles, do any traces of rotating government exist.

7. And for that reason, in no Church to this day—Greek, Latin, Armenian, **Abyssinian** <note>The Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church.</note>, etc.—are these things anything but permanent, everywhere following the form of divine ordination.

It is clear, therefore, that *the arguments for the permanence of **Leaders** <note>original: *Antistitum*</note> are both more numerous and more valid: and those contrary arguments can easily be resolved.*

Namely so that
