<summary>This page argues that church leadership should be a lifelong, permanent appointment rather than a rotating office, using the stability of the High Priesthood and the Apostles as primary biblical evidence. It suggests that rotating leadership is a human invention often born from corruption, leading to a decline in faith and morals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on church governance, specifically responding to arguments that church leadership should be rotating or temporary. The previous page established that biblical leadership (Kings, Priests) was generally held for life. This page addresses how to prevent the potential for corruption in permanent roles without abolishing the roles themselves.</meta>

### To Chapter I.

F 5

(1) *Let everyone be exercised in order.* Response: Correct, but "in the lower rowing," as **Nazianzen** <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), a prominent theologian and Archbishop of Constantinople known for his writings on the priesthood.</note> speaks: they are not to be applied to the helm until they are already trained.

(2) *Let ambition, pride, and tyranny be prohibited.* Response: Let them be prohibited, but by means that do not overturn God’s own ordering—which God Himself has already shown us. Specifically, let a council be added to the Church Overseer, just as the seventy Elders were added to Moses and Aaron. Without their consent, he should do nothing in major matters. And according to the institution of Christ, the Apostles (and their successors, the Bishops) should not rule *the way the Kings of the Gentiles do: but he who is greatest in the Church should be like the least; and he who leads others, like one who serves* (Luke 22:25, 26). That is to say: worldly wealth and honors must not be permitted to them, nor violent power over others. Instead, they should be executors and ministers, as subject to the whole body as each individual is subject to them.

Are there not examples elsewhere of power being limited against such dangerous license? Can the **Spartans** <note>The ancient Greek city-state of Sparta utilized a system where two kings ruled simultaneously, checked by a council of elders and elected officials.</note> not keep their King from straying by joining the **Ephors** <note>original: *Ephoris*. These were senior magistrates in Sparta who shared power with the kings and acted as a check on their authority.</note> to him? Do the **Venetians** <note>Referring to the Republic of Venice, which had a complex system of checks and balances on its leader, the Doge.</note> not restrain their Duke, with the Senate always assisting him? And do the **Bohemian Brethren** <note>A Protestant group from the Czech lands known for their strict community life and discipline.</note> not restrain their Elders by placing one or two Co-elders at the side of each? And by demanding an account of all actions from all of them through the authority of the Synod? Truly, human prudence only serves divine wisdom well when it does not abolish divine ordering, but supports it from falling into ruin.

Regarding the final point, that *Overseers of the Brethren are left in office for the duration of their lives (unless someone conducts himself unworthily)*: it must be added that there have been fifty-five Bishops for the Brethren over these past two hundred years (as listed by **Regenvolscius** <note>Adrian Regenvolscius, the pseudonym for the historian Andrzej Węgierski, who chronicled the Slavic Reformation.</note> in *History of the Slavonic Churches*, Book 3, Chapter 10). Among all of these, however, we read that not a single one was removed from his position. This should be credited to divine goodness and the healthiness of the Church’s Discipline. However, there were six or seven who, weakened by age or labors, voluntarily stepped down from office and asked to be <term>honorably retired</term> <note>original: *rude donari*. A Latin idiom referring to the wooden sword given to a gladiator when they were honorably discharged from service.</note>, and their request was granted.

->(T.)<-

This care for the "little plants" of the Church was established in such a way that every Pastor was bound to nourish and train one or two young men of good character in his own home (with the consent, or rather usually at the request, of the parents). Whom, at the [time] of Visitation...

# Annotations.

In due time, the Overseers <term>Antistites: high-ranking church leaders or bishops</term> either confirmed them in their purpose and transferred them to themselves or elsewhere (for more abundant opportunities for practice), or again—if they did not seem to be a suitable instrument—dismissed them. Moreover, it was the duty of the Overseers to sustain these students of the Church in greater numbers among themselves and to equip them with greater opportunities for progress.

***

->(U.)<-

Namely, according to the most ancient Canons, which are called "Apostolic" <note>The *Apostolic Canons* are a set of early Christian decrees regarding church discipline and the hierarchy.</note>, which command that *Presbyters <note>original: *Presbyteros*; elders or priests</note> be ordained by a Bishop. A Bishop, however, is not to be ordained except by two or three Bishops.*

***

->(W.)<-

Regarding this presentation of Pastors to the churches (without the election by the people or those who have the right of patronage <note>The "right of patronage" refers to the historical privilege of a local landlord or noble to choose a parish priest.</note>, which is common elsewhere), full information will be given in Chapter II, Annotation Rr. Let it be seen there.

***

->(X.)<-

In this way, indeed, in this Fraternal coalition <note>Referring to the *Unitas Fratrum*, or Bohemian Brethren</note>, we depend on one another, so that we all finally depend on no one but that One alone, on whom Heaven and Earth depend. But concerning the benefit of this Dependence and the harm of Independence, there will be an occasion to treat this more fully in Chapter VII.

***

->(Y.)<-

The same necessity that existed for the Apostolic Churches to appoint an overseer, a **Bishop**, over the Pastors of several churches, was the same necessity (after the churches multiplied) for appointing an overseer, an **Archbishop**, over several Bishops; namely, the fear of Schism <note>A formal split or division within a religious body.</note> and the desire to keep the churches in unity. For the Brethren, there was indeed no necessity to appoint **Archbishops** (since their churches were not widely scattered); nevertheless, it was still necessary for a **President of the Council** <note>original: *Synedrij Præſidem*; a leader of the governing assembly</note> to be appointed among those equal in honor for the sake of order, but without the rite of a new title or a new ordination.

### To Chapter I.

->(Z.)<-

Regarding the custom of publishing books among us, *Lasitius* <note>Jan Łasicki (1534–1602) was a Polish historian who wrote extensively about the history and customs of the Bohemian Brethren.</note> correctly noted in Chapter XXI: *Nothing has been brought to light that was not first examined by several individuals to whom this was entrusted (selected from the number of Elders and Ministers). This was done so that those things which several wise men weighed might be more certain; and so that nothing false might be present, leading readers into error, or anything peevish, causing them offense.* Likewise: *Nothing was accustomed to be published (unless special reasons were present) in the name of any* ONE *person, but in the name of the* UNITY <note>original: *UNITATIS*; referring to the Unitas Fratrum or "The Unity of the Brethren"</note>: *so that the members of the same Body might bring honor to one another, and by this, the opportunity for vain glory (which usually tickles the minds of writers) might be cut off. Indeed, the writings themselves would have that much more weight and authority. Since this Discipline has been removed elsewhere today (he continues), while whatever comes into anyone's mind is permitted to be scattered among the common people, and no one thinks himself so unlearned or unfree that he does not have the full capacity to think whatever he likes and (as is human nature) to condemn even the best things: from this it arises that so many opinions on the same matters, and so many kinds of errors and sects, come forth.*

2. These are his words; to which I add that we are unaccustomed to being occupied with *polemical writings* <term>polemical: controversial or argumentative texts intended to attack an opposing theological view</term>. Both because it was poorly suited to our simplicity to be occupied with them, and because very little use of that matter was seen among others, but rather very evident harms. For what tragedies has that *itch for disputing* <note>original: *disputandi pruritus*</note> not stirred up? And what has been achieved? Discords have not been put to rest, but increased; nor have disputes been decided, but chopped into the smallest trifles, so that there is no number of questions and sub-questions. Where, then, will that saying of *Hilary* <note>St. Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367), a prominent Latin theologian and Bishop</note> remain, that *God does not call us to heaven through subtle questions*? Would that, among those subtleties, they had not forgotten that which is primary in the work of God: mutual charity and meekness, which *Christ* commanded to be learned from Him, promising only then rest to souls (*Matthew 11:29*). But our age <unclear>sees</unclear> with amazement how those who have professed Evangelical truth carry on with one another against Evangelical meekness, and into how many most bitter factions they have departed. Our people <unclear>truly</unclear> wished to be neither leaders nor followers of tumult: thinking it more advisable even to grant this, that they be good rather than learned: and content with that reputation (as *Seneca* <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Stoic philosopher who often wrote on the virtue of simple living and peace of mind</note> speaks) and title, for the sake of peace,

# Annotations.

...striving for harmony and for preserving order among themselves, rather than for attacking others.

3. *Indeed, they wrote **Defenses** <note>original: *Apologias*</note> **for the truth of their Doctrine and the innocence of their lives**, yet without insults toward others; only for the purpose of obtaining peace. Nor did they do so every time they were attacked: sometimes making silence their shield. And this was so much the case that they even suppressed certain writings, indeed even those already removed from the press: such a writing is in my hand, one or two of them, solid enough. But when dealing with a shameless adversary, who was able to hurl insults but unable to do harm, they preferred (having weighed the matter better) to punish insolence with silence, and to hand over the quarrelsome papers to be gnawed by moths.*

4. *They wrote few things, therefore, but practical ones, as the state of affairs required. For example: in the time of war* (at that time the Turkish war was near to us, which for more than a century shook Hungary with disturbances and us with fear), *they published a treatise on War, showing how cruel a scourge of God’s wrath it is, and how it should be turned away, etc. Likewise, in a time of hunger, they wrote on Hunger; in a time of plague, on the Plague, etc. If in a time of peace they saw spirits becoming luxury-loving and morals slipping, they wrote and published (not a large volume, but a weighty one) a booklet called **Náprawa** <note>Czech: *Náprawa* means "Amendment" or "Correction."</note> **concerning the Amendment of Things**: instructing themselves through all ranks, ages, and sexes, as to what needed to be corrected in each person's place (according to the laws of true Christianity), etc. etc. Would that we might imitate them, would that we might amend ourselves, we who have all fallen deeper!*

->[*Regarding pages 23 & 24. Concerning the Co-Elders.*]<-

*It was decreed throughout the whole world that, in order to remove schisms and disagreements* (in any city) *one of the Elders should be elected and placed over the others, as Jerome* <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), an early Christian priest and scholar who commented on the origins of the episcopacy.</note> *says. If, however, one region around a city was too large and the number of pastors greater than what one Bishop could suffice for in all duties, **Country Bishops** <term>Chorepiscopi: from the Greek *chora* (countryside) and *episkopos* (overseer); these were rural assistants to the main bishop.</term> were designated for certain places to act in the Bishop's stead in minor matters. Therefore, nothing was used by us in this regard except for the example of antiquity.*

2. *What is cautioned regarding the confidentiality of councils (§. 10. A. a.) was so strictly observed* (to foster reverence for the decrees and to prevent the inconveniences that usually arise from talkativeness) *that some were even removed from the Senate on account of council matters being published prematurely.*

# Annotations to Chapter II.

->[Bb.]<-

A sixth addition should be made to consider whether anything concerning the entire **Unity** <note>The *Unitas Fratrum*, or Unity of the Brethren—the Protestant denomination this text describes.</note> needs to be amended, and in what way. (Such as the debate that existed from the beginning of our Church regarding the **Power of the Sword** <term>Power of the Sword: The right of civil authorities to use force, wage war, or execute criminals—a major point of debate for the early, pacifist Brethren</term> and the **Magistracy** <note>original: *Magistratu*; referring to whether a member of the Brethren could hold public office</note>; likewise concerning those coming from the **Papacy** <note>The Roman Catholic Church</note> who were to be rebaptized. Truly, our people did not want to commit the sin which we all criticize in the Roman Catholics: attributing to themselves **impeccability**, or **inerrancy**, and consequently **incapability of reform**. They were right to do so, because just as each individual believer is an instrument, so the whole Church is a musical instrument in God's hand; even if it is very precisely tuned, the strings may nevertheless loosen and fall into disharmony, requiring it to be retuned from time to time. While some do not observe this, but instead imagine for themselves a full and fixed perfection, they not only fail to amend anything, but always fall even deeper. They forget that the Church is not the Sun (that quality is given only to the Groom of the Church <note>Christ</note>) but the moon, which can increase and decrease in light, etc. But to deal with that matter does not belong in this place; I will only add God's promise: *To the one who mends his way, I will show the salvation of God*, Psalm 50:23.)

---

->[Cc.]<-

Regarding the location of the **Synod** <note>A formal meeting of church leaders</note>, only the **Bishops** decided among themselves; then the one in whose **Diocese** the meeting was to be held revealed the matter to the local **Pastor** and instructed him on how the necessary provisions were to be made. The remaining Pastors were invited only a few days before and were warned to remain silent. This was the custom of prudence, both because of enemies outside and to avoid inconveniences at home.

---

->[Dd.]<-

The **Elders** <note>original: *Seniores*</note> and **Co-Elders** <note>original: *Conseniores*</note>, however, arrived one day early to better prepare everything. I will briefly mention how they were accustomed to spend that preliminary day. They met at the place designated for the sessions; and after a moving <note>original: *pathetica*</note> [speech/prayer] was delivered by the President...

<meta>This page continues the description of the procedural steps taken during a Synod of the Unity of the Brethren, specifically focusing on the spiritual and fraternal preparation required before legislative business can begin.</meta>

# Annotations.

They prostrated themselves in prayer, ardently imploring divine aid. Once the prayers were completed, the **President** <note>original: *Præside*; the presiding officer or Bishop</note> gave exhortations toward concord and the renewal of love, so that everyone might approach the deliberations for the common welfare with pure minds. Soon after, the Notary read aloud the **Register of Offices** <term>Register of Offices: original: *Regestrum Officiorum*; a handbook detailing the roles and responsibilities of church leaders</term>, as we called it <gloss>It was a book in which the Power given by Christ to the Church was described, along with the Offices of those whom the Lord commanded to be **Gatekeepers** <note>original: *Janitores*</note> and to keep watch, as in Mark 13:34, etc. etc.</gloss>. When this was finished, the President again urged that everyone should examine himself against those rules as if against a **touchstone** <note>original: *lydium lapidem*; literally "Lydian stone," used to test the purity of gold</note>. If anyone saw or was aware of any negligence or any vice, whether in himself or in a **Senior Brother** <note>original: *Confratre Seniore*</note> (or **Co-senior** <note>original: *Conseniore*; an assistant to the Senior or Bishop</note>), he was not to conceal it: for now was the time to "purge out the leaven" <note>A biblical reference to 1 Corinthians 5:7, meaning to remove corrupting influences</note>, however small it might be.

After these words, the Seniors went out into another chamber, and there the President again reminded his colleagues of the need for the purest sincerity. He urged them, as if in the Sanctuary of God and in the sight of the All-Seeing, to lay bare their hearts to one another to see clearly *whether they are one in the Lord, and whether each believes himself or his brother to be walking with a straight foot? Or indeed, does he see something else, or hear something whispered by others?* This was done so that once the nature of any issue was known, and if anything truly underlying was corrected, they could all gird themselves with a fully pure conscience and full confidence to perform the **work of God** (even in the present Synod). In a word, they did not leave that room until all things that could give cause for mutual mistrust had first been smoothed over among themselves.

5. When consensus had been renewed in this manner, and the **fraternal embrace** <note>original: *amplexuqve fraterno*; a formal gesture of peace and reconciliation</note> had been confirmed in the sight of God, they returned to the **Council** <term>Council: original: *Synedrium*; referring here to the full assembly of the Synod</term>. They then asked the assistant **Co-seniors** what they had done, what they had contributed, and what they had found, similarly smoothing over all matters among them and with them.

6. Finally, they moved on to the business at hand, contributing their shares of thought <note>original: *symbolas conferentes*</note> on what was considered useful to be done in the current Synod, and how the external needs of those invited were to be provided for, etc.

---

->[Ee.]<-

The Seniors indeed stayed in the Pastor’s own house, the rest stayed with the pious citizens in the neighborhood, who rejoiced to receive them like **Angels of God**.

# Regarding Chapter II.

<meta>catchword: [ Regarding</meta>

->[F f.]<-

Thanks are given to God for bringing everyone safely together under the protection of the angels <note>original: *comitatu Angelico*; literally "angelic company"</note>, and for the joy found in seeing one another again.

---

->[G g.]<-

Tables are set out in the dining halls, or even outdoors under the open sky during the summer.
*It might seem like a trivial thing that comes to mind to be mentioned now; and yet, because it is a small part of our order and serves to diminish worldly vanities, I will say it.* Concerning <term>the place of honor</term> <note>original Greek: *πρωτοκλισία* (protoklisia); the "first seat" or "chief place" at a table</note>—the prerogative of a seat for which the Pharisees once displayed such folly in seeking above others (Luke 14:7)—there are no disputes among us. Since everyone knows in which year they were called to the **Synod** <term>Synod: original: *Synodo*; the formal governing assembly of the church</term>, and in what order they stand among the others, they know how to keep their own place.

---

->[H h.]<-

Experience has taught us that even these seemingly tiny matters serve to foster brotherly love: *For the best seasoning of food is the brotherly simplicity of the heart, accompanied by joy*, as in Acts 2:46.

---

->[I i.]<-

For the **Overseers** <term>Overseers: original: *Antistites*; high-ranking leaders or Bishops</term> were accustomed to divide these public labors among themselves and their fellow brothers in good time.

---

->[K k.]<-

Proposals are offered to the **Pastors** <term>Pastors: original: *Pastoribus*; the ordained ministers of the local congregations</term> by the **Seniors** <term>Seniors: original: *Senioribus*; the Elders or Bishops who held the highest administrative authority</term> for deliberation. The Pastors are also encouraged to collect their own matters for deliberation and present them to the Seniors in the form of written Proposals. To ensure this is done without confusion, the Pastors elect a President and a Notary from among their own number by a free vote. **Deacons** <term>Deacons: original: *Diaconi*</term> and **Acolytes** <term>Acolytes: original: *Acoluthi*; junior assistants in training for the ministry</term> are not admitted to these council meetings. However, to ensure they are not idle or wandering about in the meantime, they are given specific reading material upon which they are later examined; the Seniors also appoint a Director for them to oversee their activities and maintain order.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the organizational rules of the Unity of the Brethren. While the previous page focused on the humility and order displayed during shared meals and social hierarchy, this page moves into the formal procedural rules for church meetings and synods. It specifically details how items are added to the agenda and the unique "bottom-up" voting order used to ensure freedom of conscience.</meta>

# Annotations.

### [Regarding page 26, section 7.

### Something must be said concerning the order of asking for and delivering opinions, and the forming of Conclusions from them; to note there also the traces of good Order, equally in the college of Pastors and Elders.

1. Once the assembly has taken their seats and silence is observed, the President <note>original: *Præses*; the moderator or presiding officer of the meeting</note> advises that the business (for the sake of which they have met) should begin by first collecting Proposals: so that the matters to be deliberated upon may be in view right from the start.

2. These are therefore collected, with each person indicating the main points of the items to be addressed in summary, and then (if so ordered) explaining them: until it is determined what must necessarily be treated as the <term>primary task</term> <note>original Greek: *ἔργον* (ergon); the main work or essential business</note>, and what should be removed as irrelevant, or at least set aside for later care as a <term>secondary matter</term> <note>original Greek: *πάρεργον* (parergon); a side-project or non-essential task</note> for the time being.

3. Then, first, the primary matters are taken up for discussion <note>original: *ventilandum*; literally "to be fanned" or "aired out," meaning a thorough examination</note>, with the opinions of all requested in order; so that each may open his mind in as few words as possible and support them with reasons.

4. They therefore state their opinions in order, proceeding from the younger to the older, and not the reverse, so that the President speaks last. *Experience has taught that this order (starting not from the first and highest-ranking, but from the lowest and last) is more suitable than if it were done otherwise.* For where the Leaders speak their opinions first, it can hardly happen that prejudices are avoided and the matter is worthily enough weighed by all. First, because the younger members, having heard the opinion of their seniors first, are overshadowed by either the reputation for wisdom (which is associated with the elderly) so that they do not look into the matter more sharply; or even if they seem to see something else, they may be afraid to freely bring forth what they feel, being deterred by the authority of the seniors. By this reasoning, it happens that either the judgment itself, or the freedom of that judgment, is bound, and the way is paved for <term>the master has spoken</term> <note>original Greek: *τὸ αὐτὸς ἔφα* (to autos epha); a reference to the Pythagorean "ipse dixit," where the leader's word is accepted without question</note>. However, if any of the younger members brings forward some exquisite point (which had not occurred to the seniors), a double disadvantage arises: namely, that the Seniors will perhaps be ashamed to retract their own opinion; or if the point is accepted, the younger members are exposed to the dangerous pitfall of being too pleased with themselves above others. These disadvantages are prevented by our practice. For while the younger members know they must state what they feel before the others, they become accustomed to meditate and to engage in matters with attention and reverence (even with silent sighs to God), and it is free for each to express whatever he feels—

<meta>catchword: express—</meta>

<summary>This page describes the procedural order of the Brethren's assemblies, highlighting a democratic "bottom-up" approach where younger members speak first to ensure freedom of speech and prevent the undue influence of seniority.</summary>
<keywords>voting order, church governance, seniority, freedom of conscience, assembly procedure, Synod, Unitans, consensus building</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of procedural rules for church meetings within the Unity of the Brethren. The previous page (96) ended mid-sentence, describing why younger members are asked to speak first: to allow them to express their thoughts without being intimidated by the authority of their superiors. This page completes that thought and details the roles of the President and Notary in reaching a consensus.</meta>

-># On Chapter II.<-

...to express themselves freely: because while no one among the superiors has yet spoken their opinion, no one is prejudiced by it. The Elders <note>original: *Seniores*; the seasoned leaders of the community</note>, for their part, if anything good has been brought forward, they refine it more skillfully with their authority intact. If they have a more refined perspective, they offer it more freely; in every instance, attention sharpens attention, and judgment sharpens judgment. *Such was our practice.*

5. *This also.* Lest deliberations become wandering or vague, no one is permitted to speak on any matter other than that which is currently proposed, until it has been brought to a conclusion.

6. Nor do several people speak at one time, but only one, interrupted by no one—unless perhaps he chooses to speak impertinences, or too verbosely, or with bile <note>original: *bile*; referring to the "bitter gall" of anger or resentment</note>, in which case he is called back to order by the President.

7. After he has spoken and another has begun to speak, even if a new thought occurs to the first speaker, he does not dare to resume his speech until the votes <note>original: *suffragiis*; here meaning the individual expressions of opinion</note> of everyone have been completed. Only then—having received permission from the President—may he further illustrate and strengthen his opinion, or amend it.

8. The Notary <note>original: *Notarius*; the secretary or record-keeper of the assembly</note> records the opinions of all, and the reasons by which each is supported; he records his own as well, if he holds a view different from the others.

9. Finally, the President weighs all these things against one another, considering whether they aim at the same goal or move in different directions, and whether they can be reconciled. If they can, he shows how a single common opinion can be formed so that all the reasons brought forward remain valid. If they cannot, he points out the fork in the road <note>original: *bivium*; a point where the path of the argument splits in two</note>, explaining which reasons support this side and which support that. Again—if the matter is very serious—he submits it to further investigation to see which reasons are thought to prevail, and how the opposing views might be suitably set aside.

10. It is therefore free for each person to speak again (but in order and briefly), comparing reason with reason to explain how the matter appears to them, until it seems otherwise to no one <note>This indicates the pursuit of total consensus rather than a simple majority vote.</note>. Only then are the Conclusions inserted into the book as common decisions. By this rational process, it is achieved that everyone can see the mind of the whole Church, speak of matters as if with one voice, and act upon things unanimously.

G

# Annotations.

### [To page 27, § 13.]

I find among the *Admonitions of a certain ancient Synod* <note>original: *Dehortationes antiquæ cujusdam Synodi*; these were formal warnings against specific behaviors issued by a church council</note> the following:

1. It is observed that some Brothers, through excessive socializing with everyone, prostitute the gravity of their office; this must be avoided.
2. Some also, in order to appear sophisticated <note>original: *urbani*; meaning "urbane" or "worldly wise"</note>, devote themselves to witticisms: this must be corrected. For since the Apostle forbids unseemly joking <note>original: *scurrilitatem*; referring to Ephesians 5:4</note> to all Christians, it is much more to be fled by the Teachers themselves (as Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople</note> pronounced: *Trifles in the mouth of a layman are trifles, but in the mouth of a priest they are blasphemies.*)
3. Regarding the avoidance of frequenting markets <note>original: *Nundinarum*; large public fairs or trading events</note>: as well as banquets.
4. And not immersing oneself in political business, nor in the arranging of marriages.
5. Regarding not practicing medicine.
6. Regarding the fleeing of greed and the hunting for money: especially Usury <note>original: *Usura*; the practice of lending money at interest, which was historically restricted or forbidden for clergy</note>.
7. A Pastor should not possess more than two hundred thalers <note>original: *thaleros*; a silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries</note> in money: if any surplus remains, he should transfer it to pious uses and to the poor.
8. No one shall maintain a correspondence of letters with those who are outside <note>meaning those outside the religious community</note>, unless a singular cause exists, and with the knowledge of the Provost <note>original: *Præposito*; a high-ranking administrative officer in the church</note>.
9. All should flee the pomp of titles, being content with the lovely name of *Brothers*.
10. No one should involve themselves in the drafting of others' wills; rather, each should have his own ready: for the sake of escaping troubles everywhere, even after death. And similar things.

---

This intimate examination of conscience was severely carried out, in imitation of the Prince of Pastors <note>Jesus Christ</note> lately examining his Peter (John 21:15, etc.), by which an opportunity was given to the candidate for the Ministry to thoroughly examine himself: *Whether Christ*

# To Chapter II.

...whether he proposes to follow the ministry out of pure love, or truly for the sake of bread? <note>original: *panis causa*; referring to seeking the office merely for a livelihood. The citation 1 John 6:26 likely refers to John 6:26: "you seek me... because you ate the loaves."</note> Does he seek the flock or the wool? <note>A traditional metaphor asking if the pastor cares for the souls of the people or their material wealth.</note> Is he prepared to impart to his listeners not only the Gospel of God, but also his own soul? (1 Thessalonians 2:8) and similar questions. And this final examination of Conscience <note>original: *Conscientiæ experimentum*</note> was sometimes conducted with such deep feeling <note>original: *patheticè*; in this context meaning "moving" or "solemn"</note> that there is no lack of examples of some (fearing their conscience, or their age, or their own tenderness) who pleaded to be excused from the office: being more prepared to work out their own salvation than to be applied to the care of others. Finally, encouraged by divine promises, they followed the divine calling with fear and trembling.

->[In the same place at the end, regarding Section 3.]<-

The order in which they are called here (just as they were arranged by the Elders, taking into account age or gifts) is the same order they always keep afterward, unless someone should be called to higher things. See what was noted a little earlier at **G g**.

->[To page 32, Section 6.]<-

The formula of the testimony <note>the formal recommendation for ordination</note> was as follows: *Venerable brother in Christ, Bishop, we testify in the sight of God and of this Church that the men present are honorably born, honorably educated, and have lived an honorable and blameless life thus far; also examined by us, they have been found sound in doctrine and faith, and of right intention for serving Christ and the Church. We ask, therefore, in the name of the Churches which they are to serve, that you confer upon them the Pastoral Office <term>Pastoralis munus</term>, by the power given to you by Christ and the Church, and confirm them in it.* The Ordainer <term>Ordinator</term> responds: *This testimony of yours, given before the Church of Christ, is accepted; and your petition shall be satisfied in the name of God.*

# Annotations.

...before the conclusion, it is customary to interpose a solemn moment that should not be passed over in silence. The Ordainer <note>The official presiding over the ceremony, often a Bishop or Senior.</note> addresses them again, saying: *“Beloved Brothers, so that you may conceive a firm hope of divine help, hear the eternal High Priest, Christ, praying for you; who, being just about to sanctify himself as a sacrifice for the sins of the world, most ardently commended to his Father all those of his followers who were to announce this Redemption to the nations,”* <note>original: *“Dilecti Fratres, ut auxilii divini firmam concipiatis spem...”*</note> etc. Then one of the Bishops <note>original: *Antistitum*; referring to the presiding high officials of the church.</note> reads that final prayer of Christ from the 17th chapter of John, in such a way that it is rarely heard without tears. Finally, they come to the act of confirmation itself, the **laying on of hands**. <note>original Greek: *χειροθεσιαν* (cheirothesia).</note>

### [Regarding page 33, § 12.]

Clearly, this proceeds if this act (the Ordination of Ministers) is the final act of the Synod; if not, it is deferred. However, whether the Eucharist <note>Holy Communion</note> is administered here or later, the **Formula of Blessing** (not only generally for everyone, as is the custom, but specifically) is directed toward the new Pastors, who are now to be the leaders for the salvation of others.

### [Regarding page 34. Pp.]

The ritual books <note>The official books containing the *Forms* for performing sacred ministries, which are called the **Agenda**</note> are not attached to the Catechisms in our tradition so that they might fall into the hands of the common people. Instead, they are published separately and given only to the Pastors—not privately in a corner, but in the sight of the Church, which is the rite described here. Truly, upon the death of a Pastor, these same booklets return to the hands of the Elders. *“By what superstition?”* someone might ask. I respond: Let others have their own freedom of judgment, but I do not believe we should act in such a way that nothing mystical is left for the Ministers of the sacred mysteries <note>original: *Mystis*; those initiated into the holy rites.</note> (things which should be revered by the people rather than insolently scrutinized, and thereafter held cheap). **Religion delights in veils**: <note>original: *Religio gaudet velis*; an expression suggesting that sacred things retain their dignity through a degree of mystery or privacy.</note> even the Supreme Master of all was accustomed to speak certain things to the Apostles separately, away from the crowd. Indeed, when the Apostle mandates to Bishop Timothy that he should commit certain things to faithful men who would be fit to teach others (2 Timothy 2:2), does he not imply a certain distinction between those things intended for everyone and those intended for the future...

# On Chapter 11.

...suggests that [these books] are given to future teachers? Certainly the word he uses, <term>to entrust</term> <note>original Greek: *παρατίθεσθαι* (paratithesthai)</note>, carries the force of committing or entrusting something unique, like a deposit recommended for safe-keeping. And when we read what is written of Christ—that after the Sacred Text was read and the Book put away, he began his sermon in such a way that all wondered, with their eyes fixed on him, at the **words of grace** <note>original: *verba gratiæ*</note> flowing from his mouth (Luke 4:20, 22)—do we think it happens differently for others? Indeed, if unexpected words flow from the mouth of God’s messenger, the listeners are made more attentive and become deeper admirers of God's grace. For if only prescribed things are always recited, what will there be to excite attention? It would instead excite curiosity, so that this person or 그hat person might look at the same things in their own book to see whether they are being read accurately enough; what place is there here for devotion? Truly, it must not be thought that our people are bound to the words and syllables of those little books handed to them; they are free to use whatever serves to stir zeal (according to the variety of occasions), drawn from the treasures of **mystical Wisdom**. The result is that the pious listeners hardly ever attend the sacred mysteries without a new stirring of the heart.

---

->[Regarding page 35, § Qq. and the following page, § Rr.]<-

If Christ’s saying, *“As the Father sent me, so also I send you,”* and likewise, *“You did not choose me, but I chose you,”* holds true anywhere in this age, we trust it has held true among us. Among us, no one sought the Ministry *without being called*; no one sought a Flock for himself *without being sent*; nor did anyone expect a call or a mission from any other source than from those who were themselves called and sent. Consequently, there was no Church among us where a local Lord or Magistrate would seek out a Pastor for himself; rather, they confidently left this care to the primary Pastors of their souls, allowing them to judge *who should be given to them as a teacher and leader.* This followed entirely the example of the Apostolic Church: where the votes <note>original: *suffragia*</note> of those to be ordained were indeed received from the common people, but the decision of where those ordained should be sent belonged to the oversight of the **Seniors** <note>The high-ranking elders or superintendents of the Brethren’s Church</note> (Acts 14:23, 1 Timothy 1:3, and 2 Timothy 4:12, Titus 1:5). Hence **Hugo Grotius** <note>A famous Dutch jurist and theologian (1583–1645) whose views on church government were highly influential.</note> says: *“It does not appear from the entire history of the New Testament that Pastors were elected by the common people.”* (Concerning the Power...

<meta>This page continues the defense of the church's method for appointing ministers, concluding a reference to Hugo Grotius's "On the Power of the Sovereign in Religious Matters" and listing six reasons why the appointment of clergy should remain with the oversight of bishops and synods rather than popular election.</meta>

# Annotations.

[...the Sovereignty] of the supreme powers regarding sacred matters <note>original Latin: *De Imperio Summarum Potestatum circa Sacra*, chapter 10, page 252</note>. And that this was the practice of the Church after the times of the Apostles is clear from Jerome, who says: *“Let the Bishops hear, who have the power of appointing Presbyters <note>or Elders</note> throughout individual cities.”* And also from the Synod of Laodicea (the Canons of which were approved by the Ecumenical Council) which rejects popular elections <note>original Latin: *populares electiones*, elections by the common people</note>. (*H. Grotius, page 263.*) And that it ought to be done this way, several reasons demonstrate. Namely, because in this way:

1. *The Bishops’ diligence in caring for the Church is more keenly sharpened.*
2. *The Ministers’ confidence in their own mission [is strengthened].*
3. *The confidence of the Pious Listeners toward the servants of Christ is fostered—recognizing they do not come of their own accord (Matthew 7:15) or run without being sent (Jeremiah 23:21), but are truly sent—so that, ensuring nothing is done through personal ambition, they may say what Cornelius said to Peter: “We are here, ready to hear all things that God has commanded you.”* <note>Acts 10:33</note>
4. *And all Churches can hope that they will be provided for in this way, as men distinguished by the primary gifts of God are set over the primary Churches, etc.*
5. *And because students do not choose their own Master, nor hire him for a fee (since they do not understand what is necessary for themselves), but leave that care to Parents or Tutors: why should we not think it is done rightly if the same occurs here? For certainly the Church is the School of Christ: the Overseers of the Church are Fathers (1 Corinthians 4:15); not to mention that Sheep do not seek out Shepherds for themselves, but obey the one whom the Lord of the flock sets over them, etc.*
6. *In short, it is in no man’s interest to disturb that Apostolic order, except for the ambitious: those who prefer to thrust themselves in through campaigning, bribery, entreaties, and persistence, rather than waiting for a calling <term>vocation</term>. Again, there are those who make a profit from such trade <note>original: *nundinatione*, referring to a marketplace or fair</note>, to the great disgrace and harm of the Church. For while hirelings occupy the flock, whom do you hope they will feed? The flock, or themselves? Indeed, they feed their own bellies, money-chests, and fantasies—even though the Lord cries out, “Woe to the Shepherds who feed themselves!” <note>Ezekiel 34:2</note> Is it not a kind of sacrilege either to buy and sell sacred Offices, or to chase and seize them? What is easier, when things are done this way, than for the Lord’s field to grow wild, the Lord’s flock to waste away, faithful Workers to go hungry, and the arrogant...*

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the page is "in," which likely begins a phrase such as "in their pride" or "in authority" on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the appointment of church ministers should be handled by bishops and established authorities rather than through popular voting. It provides six theological and practical justifications, comparing the church to a school where "students" (the laity) do not choose their own "teachers" (the clergy).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of reasons against the popular election of ministers, specifically addressing the danger of "itching ears" and the historical precedent for appointments made by the clergy rather than the laity.</meta>

### To Chapter II.

...to grow insolent, and the **Mercenaries** <note>original Latin: *Mercenariós*; here referring to ministers who serve only for pay rather than a true calling</note> to become brutish along with the corruptible crowd itself?

Indeed, among us as well, that **itching** <note>original Latin: *prurigo*; a reference to the "itching ears" of the Bible</note> to choose teachers for themselves according to their own peculiar desires (2 Timothy 4:3) was finally beginning to creep in, though with no good result. For those mutual solicitations and canvassing were among the causes of loosened **Discipline** <note>original Latin: *Disciplinæ*; the strict moral and organizational code of the church</note> and collapsed **Order**. I remember, and could name, an illustrious man among the Barons who recognized his error in this and expressed regret. For when he had refused the Pastor offered to him, and had persistently demanded another (whom he had grown fond of because of his gifts of eloquence) and obtained him, he later found that man to be too puffed up with self-confidence and quite harsh. He eventually asked that the man be removed, promising that he would never again allow himself to refuse to acquiesce to the judgment of those to whom he had entrusted his salvation and who hold the care for the common edification. Because this matter is of deep consideration, I pray to God that He may rule by His Spirit those to whom opportunities are offered to restore the fallen affairs of the Church to their former state.

### [*On page 36. S.*]

Namely, between a living **Predecessor** <note>original Latin: *Decessorem*</note> being transferred to another Church, and this new man succeeding him.

### [*On page 37. T t.*]

There is no reason why it should seem unusual that the **Superintendents of the Unity** <note>original Latin: *Superattendentes Unitatis*; the high-ranking oversight officials of the Unitas Fratrum, or Bohemian Brethren</note> are chosen by the Pastors alone, and are not truly established by the common people or the Magistrate: for this comes from the primary practice of the early Church. Regarding this, there is a well-known and remarkable passage of **Jerome** <note>a 4th-century Church Father known for translating the Bible into Latin</note>: *"At Alexandria,"* (he says) *"from Mark the Evangelist until the Bishops Heraclas and Dionysius, the Presbyters <note>elders</note> always named one chosen from among themselves, and placed in a higher rank, as Bishop."* When this custom had been moved away from in the progress of time, and the People obtained a power of voting equal to the Clergy in the election of Bishops, **Gregory of Nazianzus** <note>a 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople</note> wished the election to be permitted either to the Clergy alone, or chiefly to them: *for in this way less*

<meta>This page continues the previous discussion on church governance, concluding a point about the dangers of wealthy influence in elections before moving into annotations regarding the role of secular rulers and the necessity of re-ordination for bishops.</meta>

# Annotations

...of evils coming upon the Churches, than if the votes of the wealthiest and most powerful were to prevail.

Furthermore, because it could be said that in the time of the Apostles the Church was in its infancy, supported by no secular power (just as the Church of the **Bohemian Brethren** <note>original Latin: *Fratrum Boh. Ecclefiam*; the Unitas Fratrum, a pre-Reformation Protestant group</note> was not), should it then be that after God fulfilled His promises and gave the Church *nursing Kings and nursing Queens* (*Isaiah 49:23*), just as much right over the Christian Church and its **Overseers** <note>original Latin: *Præfules*; referring to high-ranking church officials or bishops</note> should be granted to them as was granted to the Hebrew Kings over their own Church? Let **Hugo Grotius** <note>A famous Dutch jurist and theologian (1583–1645) who wrote extensively on the relationship between church and state</note> be consulted (who, among others, treated this subject most accurately): he affirms this, but in such a way that the Church is not stripped of its liberty.

Others raise another dispute: *Whether Pastors elected to the **Episcopacy** <note>original Latin: *Epifcopatum*; derived from the Greek for "oversight"</note> (oversight) must be ordained by some new rite?* I respond:

1. What harm is there if new labors are commended to them with a new solemnity (along with the invocation of the divine name)?
2. If the **Elders** <note>original Latin: *Presbyteri*; here referring to the "better part of the people" chosen to assist the pastors</note> are to be ordained by some rite, why not those who oversee the Pastors?
3. We have an explicit example in the Apostolic Men: who, when they had selected certain ones from their midst for a particular work of ministry (preaching the Gospel among the Gentiles), added a new ordination: *Acts 13:3*.
4. Why would anyone wish to see this custom, which has become common to the Churches of the world since that time, abolished? What reason could he bring forward?

---

->[*To page 38. U u.*]<-

It contributes to the reverence of this action that the election, conducted so gravely in the name of God, is covered by a sacred silence until it is revealed in that same name of God with the whole Church present.

---

->[*To page 39. X x.*]<-

Nevertheless, the Patrons of the Churches who are present remain after the assembly is dismissed: and having congratulated the sacred Council on its renewal,

## Notes on Chapter 3

->[Regarding page 41. A.]<-

It is related here: 1. That the entire Lord's Day was spent publicly in holy worship (about which more will be said later).
2. That effort was made so the Word of God might dwell in us richly, with the Prophetic, Evangelical, and Apostolic Scriptures being explained.
3. And this according to a harmony, with all the articles of Religion arranged in a sequence, so that in any given week a certain **Common Place** <note>original Latin: *Communis locus*; a standard theological theme or topic used for systematic study and preaching</note> would be illustrated from the oracles of both Testaments.

---

->[Regarding page 42. B.]<-

Concerning the system of singing in our churches, there exists the testimony of a learned and pious man, **Esrom Rudiger** <note>A 16th-century German educator and theologian who grew close to the Bohemian Brethren</note> (in the preface to Book II of his *Paraphrase of the Psalms*), in these words: *“In the zeal for singing, I do not know if the churches of the Bohemian Brethren <note>original Latin: *Fratrum Bohemicorum*; a pre-Reformation Protestant group known for their communal life and music</note> surpass all others. For I do not know who sings more—or even as much—of praises, thanksgivings, prayers, and doctrine; I will also add, who sings better, etc.”* He wrote this then (in the year 1577) when our **Songbook** <term>original Latin: *Cantionale*; a collection of hymns used by the congregation</term> contained nothing else but Gospel Songs, numbering 743. Shortly afterward, however, the **Psalter** <term>A book containing the Psalms of David</term> of David was added, set to **French melodies** <note>Likely referring to the Genevan Psalter tunes, which were widely influential in Reformed worship</note>, along with other songs of the ancient Church. Esrom added (in a preface addressed to Baron **Jan Žerotín** <note>A prominent Moravian nobleman and protector of the Bohemian Brethren</note>): *“You also sing all those things that are taught, and many of your songs are like sermons.”* (Namely, alluding to that Apostolic word: *“Teaching and admonishing one another in psalms, and hymns, and spiritual songs,” Colossians 3:16.*) The same man continues: *“Such...”*

<summary>This page outlines the liturgical practices of the Bohemian Brethren, emphasizing their systematic approach to preaching and their renowned tradition of congregational singing.</summary>
<keywords>liturgy, Lord's Day, preaching, Common Place, hymnody, Esrom Rudiger, Bohemian Brethren, Jan Žerotín, Songbook, Colossians 3:16</keywords>

**Annotations.**

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual power of music within the Bohemian Brethren, following a description of their liturgical life. It provides poetic testimonials from scholars who admired their hymnody and historical resilience.</meta>

*Songs are the voice of the Church, which then acts as a whole in whatever it does; in this, there is the greatest power in prayers. (For everyone sang, both young and old, as if with a single voice.)* He finally adds an epigram, composed by himself, in these words:

> *What your people, O Christ, have sung and still sing,*
> *And will sing in all places and times,*
> *Now even your congregation of Brothers sings to you,*
> *Which the pious Nobility cherishes by your grace,*
> *Here where the Moravians plow, and rising near the furrows,*
> *The Bohemian land lives in exile within its own homeland.* <note>This poignant line refers to the Bohemian Brethren’s state of internal or nearby exile during periods of religious persecution in the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>
> *A small band, but having professed you through long ages,*
> *The only ones remaining for you from the Hussite ashes.* <note>A reference to Jan Hus, the Czech reformer burned at the stake in 1415, from whose followers the Brethren originated.</note>
> *Observed also by all foreign nations.*
> *But our glory of homeland and praise is yours, O Christ.*

**Simon Stein**, a Professor from Heidelberg, added an epigram, the conclusion of which is this:

> *O holy ashes of Hus, rejoice, rejoice among the sands!*
> *You live throughout the whole world through your offspring.*
> *You live and finally show your enemies that the threatening*
> *Flame and the funeral pyre cannot take away the truth.*

And **Matthias Martinius**:

> *Let Amphion yield, let Thracian Orpheus give way:* <note>In Greek mythology, Amphion and Orpheus were musicians so skilled they could move stones and charm all living things; Martinius argues the Brethren's music is even more powerful.</note>
> *The Greek lyre is conquered by the Czech plectrum:* <note>original: *Czechopectine Græca chelys*. A plectrum is the tool used to pluck a stringed instrument; Martinius is metaphorically saying Bohemian music surpasses classical Greek music.</note>
> *By the plectrum, which strikes heaven with a divine-powered request,*
> *And turns an angry God with love.*
> *Such is the power of faith here, sprinkled with the blood of Christ!*
> *And such is the piety that has worshipped God!*

I will add on this occasion: it seems superstitious that some today want nothing to be sung in the churches except the Psalms of David; they are led by the reasoning that the Psalms contain the Word of God directly. As if, indeed, the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in his letters to the Ephesians and Colossians.</note> had joined "Hymns and spiritual Songs" to "Psalms" in vain. Or as if God had commanded through the mouth of David that "new songs" be sung in vain; and certainly in those places where the <meta>catchword: Messiah</meta>

### On Chapter III.

The kingdom of the Messiah was foretold (in Psalms 96, 98, and 149). It is as if the Spirit of God in the new Church could no longer inspire anyone to lead new songs, just as He once inspired *Moses*, *Deborah*, *David*, *Asaph*, *Ethan*, and others. <note>These are biblical figures credited with composing songs of praise; the author argues that divine inspiration for music did not end with the Bible.</note> Or as if it were a sound argument for someone in the Church to want to hear no Sermons—which are, of course, human explanations of the mysteries—but only the pure word of God in the bare text. Or finally, as if the *Psalms* which are sung in our own languages were the immediate words of God or of David, when in fact they are the words of translators. In short: *The ancient Church had its songs; let the new Church have hers as well, in her own fitting way, now that the mysteries have been more clearly revealed.* <note>original: "Habuit Ecclesia vetus Cantica sua, habeat & nova, suo sibi convenienti modo, mysteriis clarius detectis."</note>

---

->[Regarding page 42, C.]<-

Church assemblies are nothing other than schools of piety; therefore, they cannot do without examinations any more than other schools can. Otherwise, like students left to their own devices who think they know everything, when they are commanded to report, or to teach and work, they will be able to do nothing correctly.

---

->[Regarding page 43, E.]<-

*The Word is added to the element, and it becomes a Sacrament.* <note>original: "Accedit Verbum ad elementum, & fit Sacramentum." This is a foundational theological rule from St. Augustine, explaining that a physical element (like water or bread) only becomes a holy sacrament when combined with the spoken Word of God.</note>

---

->[In the same place, F.]<-

Therefore, among us, *Godfathers and Godmothers* are not chosen merely out of custom, or just for the name, nor are they used only as witnesses to the event. Instead, they serve a more serious purpose: so that they truly become "co-parents" <note>The Latin "compatres" and "commatres" literally mean "with-fathers" and "with-mothers," emphasizing a shared parental responsibility.</note> of the reborn children of God, sacredly obligated to nourish them for God. Parents are therefore advised not to choose strangers, or foreigners, or the elderly who might (perhaps) die soon, or prominent people chosen only for the sake of honor, favor, or money, and so on. This ensures that no false intentions defile such a holy work.

<summary>This page defends the use of modern hymns and sermons alongside scripture and outlines the Brethren's view of the church as a "school of piety" requiring rigorous examination. It also explains the spiritual responsibilities of godparents, urging parents to choose mentors who will truly aid in a child's religious upbringing.</summary>

<keywords>hymnody, new songs, sermons, church discipline, examinations, sacraments, baptism, godparents, piety, liturgy</keywords>

# Annotations

->[Regarding page 44, G. and 45, H.]<-

It was a <term>slander</term> <note>original: "Calumnia"; a false and malicious accusation meant to damage the reputation of the group.</note> cast against the **Brethren** <note>The "Bohemian Brethren" or "Unity of the Brethren," a Protestant group that predated Luther.</note> that they bound people to themselves with an oath, so that whoever once joined them could never leave their **Unity**. For they did not bind people to themselves, but to God and their own consciences, to remain constant in the purpose of true Christianity once undertaken. They did this at the urging of the Apostle himself (1 Peter 3:21) and indeed by the example of Christ, being accustomed to say: 
> *If anyone wishes to come after me, let him take up his cross and follow me.* <note>Matthew 16:24</note>

Likewise: 
> *Whoever puts his hand to the plow and looks back at what is behind is not fit for the kingdom of God.* <note>Luke 9:62</note>

And again: 
> *Whoever does not say farewell to all that he has cannot become my disciple.* <note>Luke 14:33</note>

---

->[In the same place, I.]<-

Here, it might seem that they are binding people to men. **Response:** Yes, insofar as whoever gives himself to Christ also owes a duty to His servants, in the place of Christ, according to 2 Corinthians 5:19, 20. <note>In this passage, Paul describes ministers as ambassadors acting on behalf of Christ.</note>

---

->[In the same place, K.]<-

Behold the fruit of obedience! To have a refuge in Christ through His servants, for counsel or comfort in any case of conscience.

---

->[Page 47, L.]<-

Therefore, **Flacius Illyricus** <note>Matthias Flacius Illyricus (1520–1575), a prominent and often confrontational Lutheran theologian.</note> was in error when he called that rite of receiving new members a "repetition of Baptism." "I see nothing missing from Baptism," he used to say, "except the water." **Response:** "Therefore you do not see Baptism (which cannot happen without water) but a **renewal of the Covenant**." <note>original: "Fœderis iterationem"; a ceremony similar to confirmation where an adult takes personal responsibility for the vows made at their infant baptism.</note> We established this both for the sake of one's own conscience and to remove the doubts that the **Anabaptists** <note>A radical Reformation group that argued infant baptism was invalid and that only adult "believer's baptism" counted.</note> stir up in themselves and others, attacking **infant baptism** <note>original: "Pædobaptismum"; the practice of baptizing babies.</note> on those grounds that...
<meta>catchword: because (quod)</meta>

<summary>This page defends the Bohemian Brethren against charges of being a cult-like sect that forced members into oaths. It explains their membership rite not as a "second baptism," as critics like Flacius Illyricus claimed, but as a "renewal of the covenant" intended to strengthen the faith of those baptized as infants.</summary>
<keywords>Bohemian Brethren, Unity of the Brethren, Matthias Flacius Illyricus, Anabaptists, infant baptism, covenant renewal, religious discipline, oaths of constancy</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological defense of the practices of the Bohemian Brethren, transitioning from the discipline of the community to the specific justifications for the rite of Confirmation and the requirements for participating in the Lord's Supper.</meta>

# To Chapter III.

That children initiated into the Church either do not know, or do not remember, or do not understand that they have entered into a **covenant** <note>original: "fœdus"; a formal agreement or spiritual contract between God and His people.</note> with God is a fact. However, this cannot happen where adults finally, knowingly and willingly, devote themselves to Christ. It must be admitted; but a remedy is given for that disadvantage through the rite of **Confirmation** <note>original: "Confirmationis ritus"</note>, which was practiced by the ancients (before it fell into abuse) and which is still piously practiced in certain Churches. In this rite, new Christians—who are just about to be admitted to the communion of the **Holy Supper** <note>original: "sacræ Cænæ"; referring to the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>—renew the covenant entered into at **Baptism**. With their own mouths, they renounce the flesh, the World, and Satan, and they pledge full obedience to God.

This is beautiful, and it is an imitation of what **Moses** did by God's command: so that he might teach again the People—who were present at the proclamation of the Law in their childhood or infancy and had no memory of what was done—and renew the covenant between God and them, just as they were about to clash with their enemies (Deuteronomy 5:1 etc. and 29:1 etc.). In those passages, it is so often said (Note Well) that the covenant is repeated with those with whom it was first established. Why should this not be a <term>type</term> <note>In biblical interpretation, a "type" is a historical person or event that foreshadows a later reality.</note> for us and our children? We who, as little ones, are led across the Red Sea of Christ’s blood, and the Law is immediately given to us, so that we may be a chosen People, sanctified to God. Because truly at that time God had not yet given us a mind to understand, eyes to see, and ears to hear (as is said figuratively in Deuteronomy 29:4), the covenant of God must be renewed with those entering the years of adolescence, when the battle with the flesh, the world, and various vices is just beginning.

---

->[Regarding page 48, M.]<-

For **Christ** forbade giving what is holy to dogs; He Himself, before admitting His own to the mystical feast, washed their feet. When **Peter** refused to be washed, Christ openly threatened that he would have no part with Him. Terrified by this word, Peter immediately submitted himself. Therefore, whoever is warned of some blemish before the **Holy Supper** and does not wish to be washed <note>Here "washed" refers to spiritual cleansing through confession or repentance.</note>, must be frightened by removal from the banquet of Christ, until they desist from their stubbornness.

[In the same place, N.]

<vocab>rite of Confirmation, Baptism, covenant, Moses, Christ, Holy Supper, Deuteronomy 29:4</vocab>

<summary>This page justifies the rite of Confirmation by drawing a parallel to Moses renewing the Covenant with the Israelites, arguing that children baptized as infants must personally reaffirm their faith before receiving Communion. It also emphasizes the necessity of spiritual cleansing and submission to church discipline before participating in the Holy Supper.</summary>
<keywords>Confirmation, Baptism, Covenant, Moses, Holy Supper, Church Discipline, spiritual maturity, Bohemian Brethren</keywords>

# Notes

<meta>catchword: To</meta>

### [In the same place N.]

How great the joy is to a faithful soul to be absolved from the sense of sin and the wrath of God was experienced by **David** <note>King David, the biblical author of many Psalms.</note> when all his bones exulted (**Psalm 51:10**). In order to better excite this exultation of the spirit within us, we do these two things in our gatherings after the **absolution** <note>original: "absolutionem"; the formal declaration of forgiveness of sins by a minister.</note> has been announced:

1. The entire congregation of the faithful cries out with one voice **AMEN** (just as is done elsewhere after solemn public prayers, or the announcement of a blessing, etc.; that this is neither a new practice nor a superstitious one is clear from the **Apostle** <note>Referring to St. Paul.</note>, **1 Corinthians 14:16**).

2. The entire Church sings a hymn of praise, celebrating the mercy of God poured out upon them. For this purpose, we have certain short songs designated; of which I will give an example of one from their number (*O, encourage yourself, my soul* <note>original: "Ey ermuntre se Duße má"; likely a phonetic or corrupted transcription of a hymn title in a mixture of German ("Ey ermuntre") and Czech ("duše má" - my soul).</note>) having made a literal translation. 

*O, cease to be sad, my soul! Trust in your Lord! Behold, He has now forgiven everything for you and confirmed it by the testimony of truth: and now He will seal it with His own body and blood, that He no longer wishes to be angry with you, nor to impute the remains of sin. Receive trustingly the words of the faithful God! Entrust yourself securely to His eternal mercy! Heaven and Earth will pass away more easily than His words shall perish. Though the malignant enemy* <note>The Devil.</note> *prowls like a Lion and seeks to devour my confidence toward You: I fear nothing, having with me the victor, who crushed the dragon’s head. And why should I fear? For You have commanded me to trust and promised to help. You have often sent various temptations upon Your saints, O God, yet You have deserted no one, because You only tested them. No one trusting in Your words has perished: these examples, announced to us by Your testimonies, lift me up. Therefore, dispose of me according to Your will and my necessity! Whatever cross You impose, You will not desert me, I trust. I have committed my whole self into Your hands! Lead me through the valley of the shadow of death into the rest of the soul, and into the resurrection of life. There I shall sing to You, and celebrate You with the saints, with all my strength, O Triune God! Receive the sacrifices of praise, poured out from the whole heart! I have nothing better under the sun, I long to be with You in the heavens! Amen.*

I have set this down as an example of how faithful souls are taught to exult in God once Absolution is received. There are, however, many more of this kind on various subjects.

<summary>This page describes the liturgical response to the forgiveness of sins within the Bohemian Brethren community, specifically the congregational "Amen" and the singing of hymns of praise. It includes a full translation of a hymn text that emphasizes trust in God's mercy and the victory over evil.</summary>
<keywords>Absolution, Amen, liturgical music, Bohemian Brethren, Psalm 51, David, 1 Corinthians 14, Triune God, hymnody, mercy</keywords>

To Chapter IV. 111

### Notes on Chapter IV.

->[*Page 57, last line.*]<-

Our younger members were trained with singular care in prompt **Obedience** to all commands of their superiors: being accustomed to living according to another’s will rather than their own, they might more easily understand the foundation of Christianity (which is **Self-denial** <note>original: "Sui abnegatio"</note>), and later become more capable of governing others. For indeed, *he does not know how to command who has not learned to obey.*

---

->[*At page 59, last line.*]<-

Certainly for their own and the common good. For in this way, the young people, accustomed to attentiveness in the things they do, became more capable in any business (even if they happened to move to another way of life later on): so much so that even among statesmen <note>original: "Politicos"</note> it was used as a proverb: *From the Discipline of the Brethren*. In contrast, scholars who are nothing but scholars are often accused of **Scholasticism** or **Pedantry** (that is, an ineptitude for practical business). Why? Because they are nothing but theorists—mere **Thaleses**, gazing at the stars while failing to see what is before their feet, and thus falling into pits, deservedly mocked by servant girls. <note>A reference to an anecdote about Thales of Miletus, an ancient Greek philosopher, who was so intent on observing the sky that he fell into a well, prompting a Thracian maidservant to laugh at him for wanting to know the heavens while being ignorant of the earth at his feet.</note>

---

### To Chapter VII.

<margin>since the very beginnings of **our Unity** <note>original: "Unitatis nostræ"; referring to the "Unitas Fratrum" or the Unity of the Brethren.</note>, all have submitted themselves: it should be added, **Voluntarily and not by Force**, and they have similarly maintained the same by their own will.</margin>
I believe that what is passed down in Chapters V and VI is clear by its own light and requires no commentary. However, regarding what is said in Chapter VII (p. 70), *by the wholesome institution of Discipline, already*—this is so that it may be understood that the legitimate Order of the Church, and its bond of Discipline, is not something harsh, violent, or slavish (as it often seems to the unrestrained), but rather that **sweet yoke**,

<meta>catchword: and light</meta>

# Notes on Chapter VII.

<margin>...since the very beginnings of **our Unity** <note>original: "Unitatis nostræ"; referring to the Unitas Fratrum or the Bohemian Brethren.</note>, all have submitted to this easy yoke</margin> and the light burden of Christ, in which souls devoted to God find rest, Matthew 11:29, 30. <note>The author alludes to the biblical promise that Christ's "yoke is easy and His burden is light," contrasting the strict discipline of the Brethren with a sense of spiritual peace.</note>

---

### To the Conclusion, page 74.

The character of true **charity** <term>Charity (Charitas): In this historical context, this refers to "agape"—the selfless, unconditional Christian love for others.</term> is *to wish and desire well-being not only for oneself, but for all.* And if one does not, in actual practice, bear the care and concern for all the Churches (which was the specific duty of the Apostles, 2 Corinthians 11:28), one should at least pray for all the saints (Ephesians 6:18).
